Share (3 min, cards hidden), prepare on the workbook frame (6 min), present (3 min per group) to another group acting as the board, who ask two "Supposing… / What if…" questions from the board-question cards. With time, run two rounds so each group presents and questions once. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SDelayed correction in three columns. Accuracy: "If they had built the warehouse, they would have been the leader now." · "But for they found a co-op…". Range: praise were to, on condition that, if it hadn't been for. Appropriacy: "We'll keep a wholesaler on standby if the harvest fails": precaution or condition? One Praise · Probe · Polish round for one volunteer group.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 2.1 on screen; play one 🔊 model sentence and have the class shadow it. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box in the workbook.
Total907 + 5 + 12 + 6 + 11 + 5 + 6 + 10 + 18 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: mixed conditionals. The condition and the result sit in different times because they genuinely do. Past → present: a decision not taken then explains a situation now. Present → past: a permanent trait or general situation explains a one-off past event.

"If we had accepted the offer, we'd be twice the size now." Did we accept? (No.) When was the decision? (In the past.) Are we twice the size? (No.) When is "twice the size"? (Now.) · "If she weren't so cautious, she would have invested years ago." Is she cautious? (Yes, she is; it's her character.) Did she invest? (No.) Is "cautious" about one moment or always? (Always.) So why weren't and not hadn't been? (Because she is still cautious now.)

Meaning: the three shades.

"If the merger fails, we'll cut costs." Is this a real possibility? (Yes, we're planning for it.) · "If the merger should fail…" More likely or less likely? (Less: unlikely, but we're considering it.) · "If the merger were to fail…" Where would you hear this? (A boardroom, a formal report: it's a hypothesis.) Does the event change? (No, only the probability and the formality.)

Meaning: the alternatives.

"I'll take an umbrella in case it rains." When do I take it? (Now, before.) Is it raining? (No, it might rain later.) · "I'll take an umbrella if it rains." When do I take it? (Only when it rains.) · "We'll leave at six unless you object." If you don't object? (We leave at six.) Can I say "except if"? (Yes: so unless is safe.) · "I'd be amazed unless she won"? Can I say "except if she won"? (No, so it's wrong: "if she didn't win.") · "You can borrow it provided that you return it by Monday." Is this a free choice or a condition? (A condition, and a strict one.) · "Even if it rains, we'll go." Does the rain change anything? (No.)

Meaning: but for.

"But for your help, I would have failed." Did you help? (Yes.) Did I fail? (No.) What made the difference? (Your help: one factor.) · "If it weren't for the traffic, the commute would be pleasant." Is there traffic now? (Yes.) Is the commute pleasant? (No.) · What comes after but for: a noun or a clause? (A noun: "your help," not "you helped.")

Form.

Past → presentif + past perfect, would / could / might + baseIf I had taken that job, I'd be living in Berlin now.
Present → pastif + past simple / were, would / could / might + have + ppIf I spoke Japanese, that meeting would have gone differently.
Tentativeif + should + base (→ Should you…)If you should see him, pass on my thanks.
Formal hypothesisif + were to + base (→ Were…to)If the deal were to collapse, jobs would be lost.
Alternativesprovided / providing (that) · as / so long as · on condition that · unless · supposing · in case · otherwise · even if+ present tense for real future conditions (no will)
Decisive factorbut for / if it weren't for / if it hadn't been for + nounIf it hadn't been for the grant, we would have closed.

Pronunciation. Contractions carry the naturalness: If I'd known /aɪd ˈnoʊn/, I'd be…, would have → /wʊdəv/ ("would've," never written "would of"), wouldn't have → /ˈwʊdntəv/. In mixed conditionals, stress the time words that justify the tense: "I'd be a doctor now" · "she would have invested years ago." Provided is /prəˈvaɪdɪd/ (three syllables; learners often drop the /ɪd/). In negotiation, provided that and on condition that take a small pause after them, which signals "here comes the term."

Drill script
  1. Diagnose and say (point at the arrows): T: "I didn't study medicine. I'm not a doctor now." → S: "If I had studied medicine, I'd be a doctor now." T: "He's afraid of flying. He didn't come to the wedding." → S: "If he weren't afraid of flying, he would have come to the wedding." Choral, then fast individuals.
  2. Now or then? T gives only the half and a time word: T: "If we hadn't missed that flight… now." → S: "…we'd be in Lisbon now." T: "If she were more organized… last week." → S: "…she wouldn't have missed the deadline last week."
  3. Swap the if (hold up an alternative card): T: "We'll sign if the date stays in March. Strict condition." → S: "We'll sign provided that the date stays in March." T: "Take a charger. The battery might die." → S: "Take a charger in case the battery dies." T: "We'll go. It doesn't matter if it rains." → S: "Even if it rains, we'll go."
  4. One factor: T: "You helped. I didn't fail." → S: "But for your help, I would have failed." T: "The traffic is terrible. The commute isn't pleasant." → S: "If it weren't for the traffic, the commute would be pleasant."
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 states a fact about an invented company ("Cobalt Cycles took the Swift contract."); S2 turns it into a mixed conditional; S3 adds but for or an alternative. Then all pairs, 1 minute, using the case headlines on slide 8.
The Case Study Panel — how to run it
  • Groups of 3–4 are consultants. Each group gets one invented case: Harbor Fresh (grocery delivery), Kestrel Software (clinic scheduling app), Maple & Stone (family furniture maker) or Northgate Textiles (recycled fabrics). Learner A holds "The story" (what happened, in order); learner B holds "The numbers & hinge factors" (costs, character traits, the lucky break, the offer now on the table). No showing cards.
  • Share (3 min): A and B tell the group their halves in ordinary spoken English. Others take notes on the workbook frame.
  • Prepare (6 min): the group plans a three-minute analysis for the board: what happened, the hinge factor, a past → present conditional, a present → past conditional (from a character trait), but for / if it hadn't been for, a recommendation with terms (provided that, as long as, unless, in case) and a projection with were to or should. Everybody speaks at least once.
  • Present and question (4 + 4 min per round): the group presents to another group (the board). The board draws two board-question cards ("Supposing the co-op raised its prices?") or asks its own, beginning Supposing… or What if…. The consultants answer on the spot. Then the groups swap roles.
  • Roles: lead analyst (opens and closes) · numbers expert (holds card B; handles fact questions) · strategist (the recommendation and terms) · language monitor (ticks the checklist during the presentation and gives one Praise · Probe · Polish at the end). Give the monitor role to a dominant speaker.
  • Say before you start: "These companies are invented. You're analyzing them, not judging anyone's real life."
ICQs before the Case Study Panel
Can you show your card to the group? (No, tell them.) · How long is the presentation? (Three minutes.) · How many mixed conditionals? (At least two, one in each direction.) · What comes after but for? (A noun.) · Who ticks the checklist? (The language monitor.) · What do the board's questions start with? ("Supposing…" or "What if…") · Are these real companies? (No, invented.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Some C1 learners speak far more fluently than they write, or read slowly in the Latin script. Give a two-arrow sentence strip ("If ___ had ___, ___ would ___ now. · If ___ weren't ___, ___ would have ___.") and let them build their two mixed conditionals from it. Read Card A aloud to them before the share stage, or pair them as the numbers expert with a partner who reads the story. Workbook: Ex 2 items 1–4, Ex 3 items 1–6, Ex 4 items 1–3. Target: two correct mixed conditionals and one correct in case, said aloud in the panel.

Stretch (toward C2)

Add fixed conditionals: if need be · if so / if not · failing that · barring (further) delays · come what may · assuming (that). Ask for a continuous mixed conditional ("If they'd been listening to their customers, they'd be selling online by now"). In the panel, the stretch learner must use Were it not for or Had it not been for once (previewing 2.2) and answer one board question with a counter-hypothesis: "Even if that were the case, …". Early finishers rewrite the dialogue as formal written minutes and compare the registers.

L1 watch-outs
  • in case as a false friend: Spanish en caso de que, Italian nel caso in cui, Portuguese caso, German im Fall, dass / falls and French au cas où all work as plain conditions ("if"), so learners say "Call me in case you need help" meaning "if." Use the umbrella test every time: before or when?
  • would in the if-clause: "If I would have known…" is common from German and Dutch speakers (wenn ich … hätte / würde) and appears in informal American speech too. Say: "It's heard, but it isn't written. Keep would for the result."
  • One conditional form for all times: Russian, Polish and Ukrainian use one particle (by) for present and past unreal, and Chinese, Vietnamese, Thai and Indonesian mark time with adverbs, not tense. Mixed conditionals can feel arbitrary. Always anchor with a time word (now, years ago, last week) and draw the arrows.
  • unless overuse: learners who learned "unless = if not" produce "I'd be surprised unless…". Spanish a menos que and French à moins que have the same limit, so point out the parallel: "except if" must fit.
  • Arabic speakers often have a helpful distinction already (ʾidhā for real conditions, law for unreal ones): use it. "Law = the unreal ones today."
  • But for + clause: "But for you helped me" is common for everyone; Spanish/Portuguese si no fuera por + noun is a good bridge: "It's the same: por + noun."
Homework

Online lesson 2.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_04.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and shadow them (same stress, same speed); do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "What-If Analysis" project, choosing an invented company, a new identity card's decision, or an invention or discovery (record two minutes on a phone if possible). Workbook 2.1: write up your panel analysis in 120–150 words, "My new words" and the journal (one mixed conditional, rewritten with but for or if it weren't for).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C hadn't sent · Q2 B in case · Q3 (new) A provided that. "The Hinge" (Ex 1): 1 had accepted · would be · 2 weren't · would have taken · 3 But for · 4 provided that · 5 in case · 6 were to · 7 Unless.

2.2 · C5

Inversion for Emphasis & Style

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • invert auxiliary and subject after a fronted negative or restrictive adverbial (Never, Rarely, Little, Not only, Not until, No sooner, Hardly, Only then / by / when, Under no circumstances, At no time, Nor), adding do / does / did and a bare verb when there is no auxiliary
  • use conditional inversion with Had, Were, Should, keeping negatives uncontracted (Had it not been for…)
  • use So + adjective + be + subject and Such + be + subject with a that-clause of result, and recognize place inversion (In the doorway stood…)
  • avoid false inversion: a mid-position negative, Only + subject, a fronted ordinary topic, a pronoun after Here / There
  • judge register and density: where inversion adds weight, where it sounds pompous, and how many a paragraph can carry
Materials
  • Workbook 2.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Speechwriter frame)
  • Slides 9–12 · Inversion-trigger flashcards · Had / Were / Should cards · Speechwriter briefs (4) · WEIGHT / POMP cards (1 per learner) · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 2.2-A (Volunteer of the Year acceptance speech) and 2.2-B (dictation)

Left: the mechanism. I had never seen… → Never had I seen… · I never imagined… → Never did I imagine… (imagined) · 1 front the trigger → 2 auxiliary + subject → 3 no auxiliary? do / does / did + bare verb.

Center: the triggers. Never (before) · Rarely · Seldom · Little · Not only… but also · Not until… · No sooner… than · Hardly… when · Only then / by / when · Under no circumstances · At no time · Nor. If → inversion: Had I known · Were she to accept · Should you require · Had it not been for (Hadn't it). So great was the demand that… · Such was his anger that…

Right: weight or pomp? ✔ speeches · tributes · reviews · warnings · formal letters. ✘ texts · small talk · quick emails. Density rule: one well-placed inversion beats three. No inversion: I have never… · Only Sarah knew · Here it comes · That book, I've never read.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: terms & conditions7S–SReview C4. Pairs negotiate a quick, light deal from a slip (borrowing a neighbor's car for a weekend, sharing an office fridge, lending a friend a camera). Each must use provided that, unless, in case and one mixed conditional ("If you'd returned the ladder, I'd be happier to lend the car"). Two pairs perform their best exchange. Note errors for feedback.
Lead-in: which opening?5T–SBoard two openings: "I've never seen so many people here." / "Never have I seen so many people here." Ask: "Same meaning? Which one for a text to a friend? Which one for a keynote?" Elicit: same content, different weight and formality. "Today you're speechwriters."
Negative & restrictive inversion12T–SSlide 9. Build the mechanism on the board in three steps, first with an auxiliary (had never seen), then without (never imagined → Never did I imagine). Stick the trigger cards up and elicit one sentence per card. Spend time on the two traps: Only when X happened did we… (the main clause inverts) and Not only did she…, but she also… (only the first clause inverts). Meaning, form, pronunciation, CCQs.
Drill6T–S → SFront-and-flip drill (see Drill script). Choral, then individuals.
Conditional, so / such & place inversion10T–S → S–SSlide 10. Link back to C4: "Should you see him…" and "Were the deal to collapse…" were already there. Build Had / Were / Should, then the negative (Had it not been for, never Hadn't it: "That's a question!"). Then So great was… and Such was…, and a one-minute look at place inversion (In the doorway stood a stranger · Here comes the bus / Here it comes).
Listening: the acceptance speech6T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 2.2-A twice, with a little ceremony (stand, pause before each inverted line). First listening: "What is the speaker thanking people for?" Second: learners complete the six inversions. Pairs compare and name each trigger. Ask: "Six inversions in one short speech: too many? Which would you cut?"
Register: weight or pomp?4T–SSlide 11. Read the model dialogue with a strong learner. Then read three quick lines and learners hold up WEIGHT or POMP cards: a toast ("Rarely does a team…"), a text ("Never have I been so hungry") and a four-inversion paragraph. Establish the density rule.
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2–5 alone (front and invert, drop if, faulty or fine, weight or pomp), then compare. Board-check Ex 2 item 3 and Ex 4 items 3–4, which cause the most debate. Ex 5 has room for disagreement: accept any reasoned answer.
Freer: The Speechwriter20S–S → T–SSlide 12. Pairs (or threes) get a brief: a tribute to a retiring custodian, a statement after an IT outage, an opening address for a science fair, or a safety briefing. Rewrite (10 min) on the workbook frame: 3–5 inversions, three different triggers, one conditional, one so / such. The editor must then cut one inversion that isn't earning its place. Podium (10 min): one learner per pair delivers the strongest paragraph (60–90 seconds); the audience holds up WEIGHT / POMP after each and one reviewer gives Praise · Probe · Polish. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SAccuracy: "Never I have seen…" · "Rarely does he admits…" · "Hadn't it been for…". Range: praise So modest was he…, At no time was…. Appropriacy: read one over-inverted line back and ask the room: "Weight or pomp?" Then show how one plain sentence between two inversions makes both land.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 2.2 and a 🔊 button; shadow "Rarely does an opportunity like this come along." Set the homework (below). Learners tick "I can…".
Total907 + 5 + 12 + 6 + 10 + 6 + 4 + 10 + 20 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: inversion adds weight, not content. Fronting a negative or restrictive adverbial puts the emphasis first and signals a heightened, formal register. The propositional meaning does not change. The choice is whether to front at all; once fronted, inversion is obligatory.

"Never have I seen such a turnout." Does this mean something different from "I have never seen such a turnout"? (No, the same fact.) What's different? (It's more emphatic, more formal.) Where would you hear it? (A speech, a toast, a review.) · "Never I have seen…" Is this possible? (No: once you front Never, you must invert.) · "I have never seen it." Is there inversion here? (No: never isn't at the front.)
"Only when the water went down did we realize how much she had saved." Which clause inverts: "when the water went down" or "we realize"? (The main clause: did we realize.) · "Only Sarah knew the password." Inversion? (No: Only Sarah is the subject, not an adverbial.) · "Not only did she resign, but she also exposed the fraud." How many clauses invert? (Only the first.)

Meaning: conditional inversion.

"Had I known, I would have called." Did I know? (No.) Is this past or present? (Past.) Formal or neutral? (More formal than "If I'd known.") · "Should you require assistance, please ask." Do they expect you to need help? (Not really: it's polite and tentative.) Where do you see this? (Hotels, formal letters, customer service.) · "Hadn't I known…" Is this a condition? (No, it's a question! Say "Had I not known.")

Meaning: register and density.

"Never have I been so bored!" to a friend over coffee: natural or theatrical? (Theatrical: fine only as a joke.) · A paragraph with four inversions: more emphatic or less? (Less: they cancel each other out and sound pompous.) · How many in a one-minute speech? (Two or three, well placed.)

Form.

With an auxiliarytrigger + auxiliary + subject + restRarely have we faced such a choice.
Without an auxiliarytrigger + do / does / did + subject + bare verbLittle did he know. (knew)
Only / Not until + clausethe main clause invertsNot until the review ended did the truth emerge.
ConditionalHad / Were / Should + subject (no if) · negative: + subject + notHad it not been for the fog, we'd have arrived on time.
So / SuchSo + adj + be + subject + that · Such + be + subject + thatSo convincing were the results that critics fell silent.
Placeadverbial of place + verb + noun subject (not a pronoun)Down the road came the parade. · Here it comes.

Pronunciation. An inverted sentence is delivered, not rushed. Stress the trigger and pause lightly after the fronted phrase when it is long: "Only when the water went down | did we realize…". Keep the auxiliary weak (/dɪd wi/, /həv aɪ/) and let the stress fall on the main verb and the key noun: "Never have I seen such a turnout." In Had it not been for, not is stressed and never contracted. For the podium: slower than conversation, a falling tone at the end of each inverted sentence, and a real pause before the next one so it lands.

Drill script
  1. Front and flip (hold up a trigger card): T: "I have never seen such generosity. Never." → S: "Never have I seen such generosity." T: "She rarely admits a mistake. Rarely." → S: "Rarely does she admit a mistake." T: "He little suspected the truth. Little." → S: "Little did he suspect the truth." Choral, then fast individuals.
  2. Which clause? T: "We realized the cost only then." → S: "Only then did we realize the cost." T: "The truth emerged only after the audit." → S: "Only after the audit did the truth emerge."
  3. Drop the if (hold up Had / Were / Should): T: "If I had known…" → S: "Had I known…" T: "If you should need help…" → S: "Should you need help…" T: "If it hadn't been for the guide…" → S: "Had it not been for the guide…" (insist on not after the subject).
  4. Turn up the volume: T: "The demand was very great. The servers crashed." → S: "So great was the demand that the servers crashed." T: "His anger was enormous. Nobody spoke." → S: "Such was his anger that nobody spoke."
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 gives a plain sentence about an invented event ("We had never had so many volunteers."); S2 delivers it "from the podium" with an inversion; S3 says WEIGHT or POMP and why. Then all pairs, 1 minute.
The Speechwriter — how to run it
  • Pairs (or threes) are a speechwriting team. Each gets a brief: the occasion, the speaker, the audience, and a plain, flat draft of about 120 words. Brief 1: a tribute to Walter Chen, retiring after 28 years as head custodian of Riverside High. Brief 2: the CEO of Kestrel Software apologizing to customers after a six-hour outage. Brief 3: the opening address at the tenth Riverside Community Science Fair. Brief 4: a safety briefing for new volunteers at the Harborview food pantry warehouse.
  • Rewrite (10 min): the team rewrites the draft for the occasion on the workbook frame. Requirements: 3–5 inversions in total, at least three different triggers, one conditional inversion, one so / such. The underlined lines on the brief are good candidates, but teams choose.
  • The editor's cut: before the podium, the editor must cut (or un-invert) one inversion that is doing no work and explain why to the partner. This is the heart of the lesson: judgment, not decoration.
  • Podium (10 min): one learner per team delivers the strongest paragraph (60–90 seconds) standing, from a lectern or a turned chair. After each, the audience holds up WEIGHT or POMP, and one named reviewer gives Praise · Probe · Polish. Keep a brisk pace: the whole round should take about 90 seconds per team.
  • Roles: writer (drafts), editor (checks the auxiliary and the bare verb, makes the cut), speaker (delivers). In a pair, the writer speaks and the editor edits. A dominant speaker is the editor.
  • Anxiety: nobody has to speak from the podium alone if it causes real distress. A pair may deliver alternately, one sentence each, or the learner may read seated. Offer this privately.
ICQs before the Speechwriter
How many inversions: as many as possible? (No, three to five.) · How many different triggers? (At least three.) · Must you have a Had / Were / Should sentence? (Yes, one.) · What does the editor do before the podium? (Cuts one inversion.) · How long is the paragraph you deliver? (60–90 seconds.) · What does the audience hold up? (WEIGHT or POMP.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on four triggers only: Never have I…, Not only did…, Under no circumstances should…, Had I known…. Give a sentence strip with the auxiliary slot highlighted ("Never ___ I ___ …"). In the speechwriter task, the learner rewrites three underlined lines with the partner's help and delivers those three sentences, practiced once with you. Workbook: Ex 2 items 1, 2, 4, 6; Ex 3 items 1, 2, 4; Ex 4 items 1, 2, 6. Target: three correct inversions, delivered aloud with the stress on the trigger.

Stretch (toward C2)

Add rarer patterns: Not once did…, So quickly did the news spread that… (so + adverb), Nowhere is this more evident than…, Only later was it discovered that… (passive), comparative inversion (She worked hard, as did her colleagues · more than did any other team) and fixed forms (Suffice it to say… · Be that as it may…). In the speech, the stretch learner must achieve the effect with two inversions only and explain the placement. Early finishers write the same message twice, once as a formal statement and once as a friendly text, and annotate every difference.

L1 watch-outs
  • Verb-second transfer: German, Dutch, Swedish, Norwegian and Danish invert the main verb after any fronted element (Nie habe ich… / Gestern ging ich…). Expect "Never saw I such a thing" (no do) and over-inversion after ordinary adverbials: "Yesterday went I…", "That book have I never read." Contrast: English inverts only after negative / restrictive triggers, and only the auxiliary moves.
  • Free subject position: Spanish, Portuguese and Italian allow the subject after the verb (Nunca he visto yo…), so learners produce "Never have seen I…" or forget the subject. French often keeps normal order after jamais. Drill the auxiliary + subject pair as one chunk: have I · did we · was the.
  • No do-support in L1: Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Vietnamese, Thai and Indonesian speakers often skip do / does / did ("Rarely she admits") or double-mark tense ("Little did I knew"). Treat it as a question: "How do you ask? Did you know? Same shape."
  • Contracted negative conditionals ("Hadn't I known…") are universal. Write the question mark on the board: "Hadn't I known?"
  • Register transfer: learners from languages or schools where formal style is highly valued (for example, Arabic, Persian, Russian or French academic traditions) may enjoy inversion so much that they overuse it. Praise the ambition, then apply the density rule.
Homework

Online lesson 2.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_05.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and shadow them, pausing where the speaker pauses; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "Emphatic Transformation & Set Piece" project (a 150–200-word toast, review or safety warning with three to five inversions; record it if possible). Workbook 2.2: write a clean final version of your speech (or the other brief), "My new words" and the journal (one inverted sentence and an honest verdict: stronger, or just louder?). Bring one inverted line to the next class for the warmer.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A Rarely does she admit a mistake. · Q2 A Had I known, I would have called. · Q3 (new) B Not only did she apologize, but she also offered a refund. Acceptance speech (Ex 1): 1 have I been · 2 did I imagine · 3 did you keep · 4 was the response · 5 Had it not been · 6 will we be.

2.3 · C6

Cleft Sentences & Fronting for Cohesion

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • use an it-cleft to spotlight one element, to contrast, and to correct politely (It was the timing, not the price, that worried us · It wasn't until Monday that…)
  • use a what-cleft and related frames (All / The thing / The reason / The way / The one who… + be) to save the focus for the end, with correct agreement and tense of be
  • front a given element to link a sentence back to the previous one, without inverting
  • apply given before new and end-weight, including extraposition with anticipatory it (It's a pity that…)
  • edit a choppy text into a cohesive one, and use clefts with restraint
Materials
  • Workbook 2.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Editing Desk with Article A, Record Straight checklist)
  • Slides 13–18 · Cleft-frame and fronting flashcards · Editing Desk articles B and C · Record Straight A/B cards (6) · The Emphasis Track board game (early finishers) · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 2.3-A (radio interview: The Copper Kettle) and 2.3-B ("Which question does it answer?")

Left: one fact, three shapes. Her attitude impressed me. → It was her attitude that impressed me. (spotlight / "not her CV") → What impressed me was her attitude. (focus at the end) → Her attitude, I could not fault. (fronted topic)

Center: frames. It wasn't until… that… · It was X, not Y, that… · What happened was (that)… · All I want is… · The reason… is that… (because) · The thing that worries me is… · be agrees: What I need is a break / are answers · What surprised us was…

Right: the flow. GIVEN → NEW · light → HEAVY. We tried three strategies. The first we dismissed at once. · That you missed it is a pity. → It's a pity (that) you missed it. · Fronting ≠ inversion: That book have I never read. · Restraint: max 3 clefts per paragraph.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: one-line toasts7S → T–SReview C5. Each learner stands and delivers the inverted line they brought from homework (or one from their speech). The class holds up WEIGHT / POMP; you board two good ones and one error without names. Keep it quick and warm: 20 seconds each, applause after every line.
Lead-in: which question?5T–SBoard: "Her attitude impressed me." Then the three reshaped versions (Board plan, left). Ask: "Which one answers: Was it her CV that impressed you? Which one answers: What impressed you? Which one continues a conversation about her attitude?" Elicit: word order carries information. "Today: moving the weight across a whole paragraph."
It-clefts & what-clefts12T–SSlides 13–14. It-cleft: spotlight, contrast, the corrective frame, It wasn't until…. What-cleft: the curtain drawn back, focus at the end. Then the other frames (hold up the cards: All / The thing / The reason / The way). Agreement and tense of be; The reason is that. Meaning, form, pronunciation, CCQs.
Drill6T–S → SSpotlight drill and "correct me" drill (see Drill script). Choral, then individuals, with the stress on the focus.
Fronting, given → new, end-weight10T–S → S–SSlide 15. Read the flat "missed deadline" paragraph from the online lesson, then the cohesive version. Pairs underline what changed. Name the two principles. Show fronting ("The first we dismissed at once") and extraposition ("It's a pity that…"). Warn: fronting doesn't invert unless there is a negative trigger (link to C5).
Listening: The Copper Kettle6T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 2.3-A twice, with two voices or with a confident learner as Nadia. First listening: "What's the misconception?" Second: the six gaps. Then Script 2.3-B (quick, oral): learners call out which question each cleft answers.
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2–5 alone (rewrite for focus, front the link, move the weight, which flows better), then compare. Board-check Ex 5 with reasons, not just letters.
Freer 1: The Editing Desk15S–S → T–SSlide 17. Groups of 3 edit a choppy article (A in the workbook; B and C on cards) so that it flows: every fact kept, at least one it-cleft, one what-cleft (or other frame), two frontings or extrapositions, and no more than three clefts. 10 minutes editing, 5 minutes for two groups to read aloud and one Praise · Probe · Polish each. ICQs first.
Freer 2: Setting the Record Straight9S–SSlides 16–17. Pairs get Record Straight cards: A reads "What people think," B (the spokesperson) corrects it politely from "What actually happened" with a corrective it-cleft and a what-cleft, then adds one fact. Swap cards and roles every 90 seconds; three rounds. Stress the focus word.
Feedback5T–SAccuracy: "What surprised us is the silence." · "The reason is because…" · "That book, I have read it." Range: praise corrective clefts and fronted comparatives (Even more surprising is…). Appropriacy: read an over-clefted paragraph back ("It was… What… The reason…"): "How does it sound?" Restraint is the C1 skill.
Homework & module wrap-up5T–SSlide 18 (recap). Show online lesson 2.3 and shadow one 🔊 sentence. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists. Unticked items go on a sticky note for you.
Total907 + 5 + 12 + 6 + 10 + 6 + 10 + 15 + 9 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: it-clefts. The it-cleft lifts one element into the spotlight and backgrounds the rest. It often carries "…and not something else," which makes it the natural frame for polite correction.

"It was her attitude that impressed me." What impressed me? (Her attitude.) Is there a hint of contrast? (Yes: not her CV, not her experience.) · "Actually, it was the timing that worried us, not the price." What did the other person think? (That the price worried us.) Is the speaker rude? (No, it's a calm correction.) · "It wasn't until Monday that we noticed." Did we notice on Friday? (No.) When? (On Monday, later than expected.)

Meaning: what-clefts and frames. The what-cleft holds back the focus and delivers it at the end, which builds a little anticipation. Frames name the aspect: the reason, the thing, the way, the person.

"What we need right now is a clear explanation." Where is the important part: the start or the end? (The end.) Why use this and not "We need a clear explanation"? (It prepares the listener: something important is coming.) · "All I want is a quiet evening." A lot or a little? (A little: all minimizes.) · "The reason I called is because…" Is this OK in a report? (Not in formal writing: "The reason… is that…")

Meaning: fronting and end-weight.

"We tried three strategies. The first we dismissed at once." Is "the first" new or already known? (Known: we just heard "three strategies.") Why put it first? (It links back.) What's the new part? (Dismissed at once.) · "That you missed it is a pity." Natural? (Top-heavy.) Better? ("It's a pity (that) you missed it.") Where does the heavy clause go? (To the end.) · "That book have I never read." Correct? (No: fronting a topic doesn't invert. "That book, I have never read.")

Form.

It-cleftIt + be + focus + that / who + rest (who for people)It was my sister who suggested it.
Negative timeIt wasn't until + time / clause + that…It wasn't until later that I realized.
What-cleftWhat + clause + be + focus (be agrees with the focus and matches the time)What surprised us was the silence.
FramesAll / The thing / The reason / The way / The place / The one who + clause + beThe way she handled it was masterful.
Frontinggiven element first, then normal order (no inversion without a negative trigger)Of the three options, the first seems best.
ExtrapositionIt + be / verb + … + heavy clause at the endIt's unclear whether they'll agree.

Pronunciation. The grammar and the voice must agree: the focus carries the main stress. It-cleft: "It was her attitude that impressed me" (the rest is low and quick). Corrective: contrastive stress on both items: "It was the timing, not the price." What-cleft: a slight rise and a small pause after was / is, then a strong fall on the focus: "What we need ↗ | is time ↘." Fronted topics get their own tone unit: "The first ↗ | we dismissed at once ↘."

Drill script
  1. Spotlight it: T: "Maria fixed the printer. Spotlight Maria." → S: "It was Maria who fixed the printer." T: "Spotlight the printer." → S: "It was the printer that Maria fixed." T: "We met in Lisbon. Spotlight the place." → S: "It was in Lisbon that we met."
  2. Save it for the end: T: "I need a break." → S: "What I need is a break." T: "The engine overheated." → S: "What happened was (that) the engine overheated." T: "I want a quiet evening, nothing more." → S: "All I want is a quiet evening."
  3. Correct me (T states a wrong assumption; S corrects with contrastive stress): T: "So the price put customers off?" → S: "No, it was the parking that put them off, not the price." T: "You found the error on Friday?" → S: "No, it wasn't until Monday that we found it."
  4. Lighten the front: T: "That she said nothing surprised me." → S: "It surprised me that she said nothing." T: "To argue with him is pointless." → S: "It's pointless to argue with him."
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 says a plain fact about an invented workplace; S2 asks a wrong-assumption question about it; S1 corrects it with a cleft. Then all pairs, 1 minute.
The Editing Desk & Setting the Record Straight — how to run them
  • Editing Desk (groups of 3, 10 min): each group gets one choppy article of 11–14 short sentences: A Tallis Print Works' four-day week (in the workbook), B nap pods at Eastgate Distribution Center, C the Greenway Middle School garden. The brief: every fact stays; use at least one it-cleft, one what-cleft (or another frame) and two instances of fronting or extraposition; no more than three clefts in total; check each change against given → new. Roles: writer, fact-checker (ticks each fact as it survives), reader (reads each new sentence aloud; if it sounds staged, the group reconsiders).
  • Read-out (5 min): two groups read their edited articles aloud. The class listens for flow and names one sentence that "clicked." One Praise · Probe · Polish each. Model versions are in the answer key.
  • Setting the Record Straight (pairs, 9 min): A reads the misconception from a "What people think" card as a journalist or a neighbor would say it ("So the pool is closing because it's unsafe?"). B, the spokesperson, holds the matching "What actually happened" card and corrects it politely: a corrective it-cleft, a what-cleft, one extra fact. Swap after 90 seconds and take a new card pair.
  • Tone matters: the goal is calm, courteous correction, not "winning." Model it: "I can see why people think that. Actually, it wasn't… What really happened was…". Every card is about an invented organization; no learner corrects a claim about a real person.
  • Early finishers play The Emphasis Track board game, which recycles the whole module.
ICQs before the tasks
Editing Desk: can you delete a fact? (No, every fact stays.) · How many clefts: as many as possible? (No, three at most.) · Who ticks the facts? (The fact-checker.) · What goes first in a sentence, the given or the new? (The given.) · Record Straight: are you arguing with the journalist? (No, correcting politely.) · Which structures? (An it-cleft and a what-cleft.) · Which word do you stress? (The focus, the correct information.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on two frames: It was ___ that… and What ___ was…. In the Editing Desk, give the learner Article A with the sentences already cut into strips so they can physically move them, and make them the fact-checker. In Record Straight, provide the frame on a card ("Actually, it wasn't ___ that ___. It was ___.") and let them read the correction from it the first time, then try without. Workbook: Ex 2 items 1–5, Ex 3 items 1–2, Ex 5. Target: two correct corrective clefts said aloud with the stress on the focus.

Stretch (toward C2)

Add reversed pseudo-clefts (That's what I mean · This is where it gets interesting), It's not that…, it's that…, What… did was to…, and fronted participles and comparatives (Equally important is… · Gone are the days when…). At text level, ask the stretch group to map the thematic progression of their edited article: does each sentence start from something in the one before? In Record Straight, the stretch learner must also concede one point gracefully ("The one thing we did get wrong was…").

L1 watch-outs
  • Missing dummy it: Spanish, Portuguese and Italian (Es importante que… · É uma pena que…) and Arabic often produce "Is a pity that you missed it" / "Is unclear whether…". Point at the empty subject slot: "English needs it."
  • Topic-comment transfer: Chinese, Japanese and Korean put the topic first very naturally, which helps with fronting, but learners often add a resumptive pronoun ("This problem, we must solve it"). Cross out the it.
  • Cleft overuse: French uses c'est… qui / que far more than English does, and learners may cleft every other sentence. Praise accuracy, then apply the restraint rule.
  • Frozen is and agreement: "What surprised us is the silence" (for a past event) and "What we need is answers" occur for all L1s. Accept plural focus with is in speech; ask for agreement in writing.
  • "The reason is because" is universal (and common in native speech). Correct it in writing only.
  • Free word order languages (Russian, Polish, Ukrainian, Czech) put new information last simply by moving words, so learners may produce "The engineering nobody mentioned" without a comma or pause, which can sound abrupt. Teach the pause and the fronted topic's own intonation.
Homework

Online lesson 2.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_06.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and shadow them, stressing the focus; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "From Flat to Cohesive" project (use a paragraph you wrote about work or study, or one of the other Editing Desk articles). Workbook 2.3: a clean final copy of your edited article, "My new words" and the journal (two sentences from a message this week, rewritten with a cleft or a fronted element, with a one-line explanation).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B What I admire most of all is her honesty. · Q2 C The last, we rejected outright. · Q3 (new) B The reason sales fell is that the store moved. Radio interview (Ex 1): 1 wasn't · that · 2 What we noticed · 3 wasn't until · 4 All we · 5 was that · 6 we didn't lose.

Closing the Module

End of C6: Module 2 wrap-up

Recap on slide 18, "weigh, add, place": If they had accepted, they'd be bigger now · provided that the date holds · but for one loyal customer base (weigh the options) → Never have I… · Had it not been for… · So modest was he that… (add the weight, sparingly) → It wasn't the price that…; it was the parking · What happened was… · The first we dismissed at once (place the weight). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists; anything unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Review it in the C7 warmer (The Emphasis Track is ready for that) and in the C13 progress-test catch-up.

Watch for in Module 3: 3.1 nominalization (the decline of the economy) works hand in hand with end-weight and extraposition: "What caused the decline…" Make the link out loud. 3.2 (reference, substitution and ellipsis) continues the cohesion work of C6: "if so / if not" from C4 are substitution in action. 3.3 (hedging and diplomatic English) builds directly on the tentative should (Should you require…) and the corrective it-cleft: both are diplomatic tools.

Progress Test 1 (C13) covers Modules 1–4. From this module it tests: mixed conditionals in both directions, the meaning of in case / unless / provided that / even if, but for + noun and if it weren't / hadn't been for with the right time frame, inversion after negative and restrictive adverbials (including do-support and Only when… did…), conditional inversion with an uncontracted negative, So / Such inversion, it-clefts and what-clefts (agreement of be, The reason is that), and choosing the continuation that respects given → new and end-weight. The writing and speaking tasks reward appropriate use: one well-placed inversion or cleft scores higher than four.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 3 — Cohesion, Register & Style

Three classes about the packaging of meaning rather than the meaning itself. First, nominalization: turning actions into nouns (they decided to expand → the decision to expand) to write in a compact, impersonal academic register, and, just as important, turning them back into verbs when a plain-English reader needs clarity. Then cohesion: reference, substitution and ellipsis (this trend · the latter · do so · I hope not · I'd love to), the quiet devices that stop a text repeating itself. Last, softening: academic hedging (the data suggest… to some extent), diplomatic English for bad news, disagreement, refusals and feedback (I was wondering if… · I'm afraid… the thing is…), and casual vague language (eightish, or so).

3 lessons Lessons 3.1–3.3 Classes C7–C9 Weeks 4–5 90 min per class CEFR C1

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill → controlled practice (workbook) → a demanding freer task (a workshop, not a quick chat) → feedback with delayed error correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-3-cohesion-register.html. Print materials/module-3/classroom-materials.html before C7. It holds the flashcards, the new identity cards, the Register Workshop source texts (verbal → academic) and Plain English notices (academic → plain), the Repair the Report drafts and talk-topic cards, the Diplomatic Role-Play Series A/B cards with observer sheets, the Soften It! board game and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences again at home.
  • These are skills classes, so run them as workshops. Every class spends roughly a quarter of its time on one extended product: a rewritten text in two registers (C7), a repaired report plus a two-minute talk (C8), a series of four difficult workplace conversations (C9). Protect that time. If you're running late, shorten the controlled practice (set the rest for homework), never the workshop.
  • Refinement, not a first meeting. At B2 learners met the passive and impersonal reporting (B2 3.1), reduced clauses (B2 3.2), linking and formal/informal style (B2 Module 4) and polite requests. Don't re-teach any of it. The C1 step is control of register: choosing, sentence by sentence, the packaging that fits this reader, and being able to justify the choice.
  • Feedback on three things. In every freer task, take notes in three columns: accuracy (errors to fix: the arrivement, an increase of prices, I don't hope so), range (ambitious, well-chosen language to praise and spread: pose a threat to, such a move, the latter, I'd have thought…) and appropriacy (grammatical but wrong for the room: a nominalized friendly email, the former / the latter in a chat, sort of in a report, a "diplomatic" refusal so soft nobody noticed it was a refusal). At C1 the appropriacy column is often the most useful. In the feedback stage, board 2–3 items from each column, without names.
  • Peer review, every class. Each workshop ends with a structured peer-review step, and each has its own test: the "Who does what?" test (C7: can a reader say who did what in each sentence?), the "Point at it" test (C8: draw an arrow from every it / this / one to what it refers to) and the "What must change?" test (C9: the listener says back what they understood they must do or accept). Reviewers always give one strength and one suggestion, in that order, and quote the text instead of summarizing it ("In line 3, this could point at two things").
  • Dominant speakers. C1 classes often include confident professionals who are used to running meetings. Use the built-in roles: give them the jobs that need listening, not talking. In C7 they are the plain-English reader (who must honestly say where they get lost); in C8 the tally-keeper in the two-minute talk; in C9 the observer with the checklist. Say it kindly: "Your ear is excellent. I want you on the checklist this round." In whole-class feedback, use "a new voice first" before opening the floor.
  • Stretching strong learners toward C2. Each lesson has a stretch box. The C2 direction for this module is deliberate effect: nominalizing to hide an agent on purpose and noticing when a text does it to you (C7), cohesion across paragraphs with summary nouns and cataphoric reference (The problem is this: …) (C8), and calibrated understatement that a listener has to read between the lines (C9). Ask strong learners to explain the effect of a choice, not just make it.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British spellings and words (nominalisation, utilisation, analyse, behaviour, favour, criticise, colour, flat, quid, film). Teach nominalization, utilization, analyze, behavior, favor, criticize, color, apartment, dollars / bucks, movie. Accept British forms whenever a learner uses them. Note that British English uses more understatement than American English; point that out in C9 rather than asking learners to copy it.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Package it → connect it → soften it. In C7 learners choose how to package an idea: as a verb (we cut the budget, so staff were unhappy) or as a noun (budget cuts led to a decline in morale), and learn that the best writers move in both directions. In C8 they connect ideas across sentences without repeating them (this decline · such a move · the latter · I hope so · I'd love to). In C9 they control force: hedging a claim so it is defensible and softening a message so the relationship survives it, without losing the message. Every class produces something another learner reads or hears: a two-register rewrite, a repaired report, a set of difficult conversations.

Trauma-informed: realistic workplaces, low personal stakes

Diplomatic English lives next to difficult moments: criticism, refusals, conflict with a boss, bad news. Keep every scenario workplace-realistic but low-stakes and invented: a delayed project, a rejected budget request, a vacation that can't be approved this month, a report that needs another draft, a colleague asking for too much help. Never role-play a firing or layoff, a disciplinary hearing, a medical diagnosis, a landlord eviction, a police or immigration interview, or a family conflict. The online lesson's journal asks learners to recall a real difficult conversation: always offer "a real one, an invented one, or one from a new identity card." Every role-play position is assigned on the card; learners play the role, not themselves, and anyone can say "Pass." Some learners come from workplaces where disagreeing with a manager was genuinely unsafe. Make it explicit that the C9 scenarios practice language options, not a rule that they must disagree with anyone in real life.

Before the Module

  • Print the flashcards (one set for the board): verb → noun and light-verb collocation cards (C7); cohesion device cards (C8); softening cards color-coded by toolkit, H / D / V (C9). Four picture "register rooms" cards (book, email, coffee, notebook) are used in all three classes. Cut them before C7.
  • For C7: one set of Register Workshop source cards (6 chatty texts, one per group of 3) and Plain English notices (4 over-nominalized texts, one per pair), plus one two-register worksheet per group.
  • For C8: one Repair the Report draft per group of 3 (3 different reports), a device checklist per group, a set of two-minute talk topic cards and a phone timer.
  • For C9: the Diplomatic Role-Play Series (4 scenarios × 2 versions, each an A/B pair) and one observer checklist per group of 3. The Soften It! board game (1 board per group, 1 die, counters) is for early finishers in C9 and the C10 warmer.
  • Print the new identity cards (workplace profiles) and keep them on a side table where anyone can take one without asking.
  • Optional but powerful: bring two real texts on the same light topic in two registers, for example a city's recycling page written for residents and the council report behind it, or a clinic's patient leaflet and the study it cites. Learners love finding the same fact packaged twice.

Lesson Plans

3.1 · C7

Nominalization & Academic Style

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • form the noun from a verb or adjective with the right suffix, including irregulars (believe → belief, choose → choice, grow → growth) and zero-derivation (increase → an increase), and choose the process noun over the person noun (the analysis, not the analyst, of the data)
  • rewrite a verb-heavy, agent-visible sentence in a compact, impersonal, noun-based style, using a light verb (carry out, make, reach, pose, provide, bring about) and the right preposition after the noun (a reduction in, the impact on, an investigation into)
  • explain the three effects of nominal style (concise, impersonal, abstract) and recognize its partners: the passive, there is / there are, long noun phrases
  • diagnose over-nominalization with the "Who does what?" test and rebalance the sentence with working verbs, producing plain English that is still serious
  • choose the packaging for a given reader and genre, and justify the choice
Materials
  • Workbook 3.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Register Workshop frame, self-check)
  • Slides 3–7 · Verb → noun and light-verb flashcards · "Register rooms" picture cards · Register Workshop source cards (6) · Plain English notices (4) · two-register worksheets · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 3.1-A (the team update and the written summary) and 3.1-B (dictation)

Left: same fact, two packages. They decided to expand, which reduced their costs. (verbal · spoken · agent visible) → The decision to expand led to a reduction in costs. (nominal · written · agent hidden). Suffixes: -tion/-sion · -ment · -ance/-ence · -ity · -ness · -al · -ure · -th · zero (an increase). Irregulars: belief · choice · growth · analysis.

Center: the machinery. noun + light verb: carry out an investigation into · make an assessment of · pose a threat to · reach a conclusion · bring about a change in · have an impact on. Partners: passive · there is · long noun phrases. Effects: concise · impersonal · abstract.

Right: the other direction. The implementation of the utilization of renewable resources is a requirement for the achievement of a reduction in emissions. ✘ → To reduce emissions, we must use renewable resources. ✔ Test: Who does what? Can I tell in 3 seconds? If not → turn a noun back into a verb.

Warmer: fronted and clefted7S–SReview C6 (2.3, clefts and fronting). Board two flat sentences: "The schedule change annoyed people most." · "We need clearer instructions." Pairs rewrite each as a cleft and a fronted version (What annoyed people most was… · Clearer instructions are what we need. · Most annoying of all was…), then write one true or invented sentence about their week starting "What I…" (new identity cards available). Two pairs read theirs. Note one error and one range example.
Lead-in: two writers5T–SSlide 3 (the four "register rooms": book, email, coffee, notebook). Board: "They decided to expand, which reduced their costs." and "The decision to expand led to a reduction in costs." Ask: "Same facts? (Yes.) Which one is from a coffee chat, which from a report? How do you know? Where did they go?" Elicit: the actions have become things. "Today: how to package an idea as a noun, and when not to."
Form: building the nouns10T–S → S–SSlide 4. Don't lecture the suffix table: give pairs 90 seconds to sort ten verb and adjective flashcards by suffix, then check on the slide. Highlight irregulars (belief, choice, growth, analysis, failure), zero-derivation (an increase, a rise, a change, a use) and process vs person nouns (analysis / analyst, production / product). CCQs (below).
Meaning: why and how10T–SSlide 5. The three effects (concise, impersonal, abstract) with one example each; the partners (passive, there is, long noun phrases). Then light verbs: a noun still needs a verb, but an empty one. Build We investigated the cause → We carried out an investigation into the cause and show why it's useful: the noun can take adjectives (a thorough / preliminary / independent investigation). Noun + preposition on the board: a rise in, the impact on, a threat to, the cause of.
