Lesson Plans

4.1 · C10

Phrasal Verbs & Multi-word Verbs

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • sort a multi-word verb into one of four types (intransitive set off · separable turn off · inseparable look after · three-part put up with) and predict where its object goes
  • apply the pronoun rule: a pronoun object must split a separable verb (turn it off) and must follow an inseparable one (look after it); keep long objects at the end (turned off every light in the house)
  • work out the sense of a polysemous phrasal verb from its collocates (the plane took off / sales took off / take off your coat)
  • perform a register swap in both directions (look into ↔ investigate, put off ↔ postpone, find out ↔ discover, get rid of ↔ eliminate) and justify which one fits a given reader, including when to keep the phrasal verb
  • use and pronounce the nominalized forms with first-syllable stress (a BREAKdown, an OUTcome, a TAKEover)
Materials
  • Workbook 4.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Register Swap planner)
  • Slides 3–8 · Type cards and register-pair flashcards · Picture cards for the literal senses (flight, coat, luggage, mechanic) · Register Swap A/B cards (6 stories) · "Which fits?" context cards · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 4.1-A (the launch debrief, a voice message) and 4.1-B (dictation)

Left: four boxes. ① no object: The car broke down. · We set off at dawn. ② separable: turn off the light / turn the light off / turn it off ✔ · turn off it ✘ ③ inseparable: look after the kids / look after them ✔ · look them after ✘ ④ three-part: put up with the noise / put up with it (never split). Test: try a pronoun.

Center: the register swap. SPOKEN ⇄ WRITTEN: put off ⇄ postpone · find out ⇄ discover · look into ⇄ investigate · go up ⇄ increase / rise · set up ⇄ establish · give up ⇄ abandon · put up with ⇄ tolerate · carry out ⇄ conduct · get rid of ⇄ eliminate · come across ⇄ encounter. Under it: Who's reading? Where? Why? · "Don't over-correct": carry out, point out, set up are fine in formal writing.

Right: one verb, many jobs. take off: ✈️ leave the ground · 📈 succeed · 🧥 remove · pick up: collect · learn informally · improve. Nouns: break DOWN → a BREAKdown · take OVER → a TAKEover · turn OUT → an OUTcome · break OUT → an OUTbreak (flip!).

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: diplomatic makeover8S–SReview C9 (3.3, hedging and diplomatic English). Board three blunt lines: "Your figures are wrong." · "This plan won't work." · "You're late with the report again." Pairs soften each one twice: once with hedging (There does appear to be a slight problem with the figures.) and once with vague language (a bit of an issue, sort of). Two pairs read their most diplomatic version. Note one range example to praise. Link forward: "Softening changes the grammar. Today we change the verbs."
Lead-in: same story, two voices5T–SSlide 7 (hide the labels). Show B's spoken line ("Two suppliers pulled out at the last minute, so we had to put it off by a week") and the formal line ("Two suppliers withdrew at short notice, obliging us to postpone it by a week"). "Same facts? Who's speaking to whom in each? Which verbs changed?" Elicit pulled out → withdrew, put off → postpone. "Today: the verbs that make English sound spoken, and their formal twins."
Four types & the pronoun rule12T–SSlides 3–4. Build the four boxes on the board by sorting eight type cards with the class. Teach the diagnostic: "Does it take an object? Try a pronoun." Then the pronoun rule and the "heavy object goes last" corollary (turned off every light in the house before she left). Meaning, form, pronunciation, CCQs (below). This is fast at C1: the pronoun rule is the only place to linger.
Drill6T–S → SPronoun drill, stress drill and noun-stress drill (see Drill script). Choral, then fast individuals, then open pairs.
One verb, many meanings9T–S → S–SSlide 5. Hold up the flight and coat picture cards: "The plane took off. She took off her coat." Then say "Sales took off after the launch" with no picture: "What took off?" Pairs do Workbook Ex 4 (four polysemous verbs), then share one sentence each for pick up in three senses. Five minutes on the nominalized forms at the end (Grammar box) with the stress shift: break DOWN → a BREAKdown.
Listening: the launch debrief7T–S → SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 4.1-A twice as a voice message from a project lead to her team. First listen: "Did the launch happen? What went wrong? What's the good news?" Second listen: learners complete eight phrasal verbs, then label each one's type (1–4). Pairs compare.
The register swap8T–SSlide 6. Present the pairs by situation, not as a list: the same leak reported in a text to a roommate (Can you look into it?) and in an email to the property manager (Please investigate the matter). CCQs. Then the "don't over-correct" rule: read "I shall relinquish caffeine" in a posh voice: "Would you say that at lunch?" Show three phrasal verbs that are perfectly formal (carry out, point out, set up).
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2, 3 and 5 alone (object placement, the register swap with context choices, verb → noun). Pairs check. Board-check only disagreements. In Ex 3b, the discussion of why is the point: accept two answers where the reason is good.
Freer: the Register Swap15S–S → T–SSlide 8. Pairs, one Register Swap story. Round 1 (4 min): A reads the informal voice note on their card, then tells it to B from memory, naturally, adding phrasal verbs; B notes every phrasal verb heard. Round 2 (6 min): together they write the formal version (a 70–90-word incident report or update for a manager or committee), swapping where the register calls for it and keeping at least one phrasal verb on purpose. Round 3 (3 min): the reverse: B's card is a formal memo; A turns it into a 30-second spoken update. Round 4 (2 min): groups of 4 swap reports and give Praise – Question – Polish. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SDelayed correction, three columns (no names). Accuracy: "Could you turn off it?" · "We need to look the complaint into." · "There was a break down in the system." Range: "Things picked up" / "obliging us to postpone." Appropriacy: "The committee will sort out the budget" (formal report) · "We shall commence at eight" (text to a coworker). Learners fix and explain.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 4.1 on screen; point at a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box in the workbook.
Total908 + 5 + 12 + 6 + 9 + 7 + 8 + 10 + 15 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: what counts as a multi-word verb. A verb plus one or two particles that behave as one unit, often with a meaning you can't build from the parts. Whether the particle is an adverb (off, up, out, away, back) or a preposition (after, with, for, into) decides where the object can go.

"She looked after her neighbor's cat." Is she looking behind the cat? (No. She took care of it.) Can I say "She looked the cat after"? (No: inseparable.) · "He turned the offer down." Did he turn anything physically? (No: he refused it.) Can I move "the offer"? (Yes: turn down the offer / turn the offer down.) With "it"? (Only "turn it down.")

Meaning: the pronoun rule.

"Can you fill in the form?" Two positions possible? (Yes.) "Can you fill in it?" (No: "fill it in.") · "Can you deal with the form?" "Deal the form with"? (No: inseparable.) "Deal with it"? (Yes: the pronoun stays after.) · How do I test a new verb? (Try it with "it.")

Meaning: polysemy.

"Business is picking up." Is someone lifting something? (No: it's improving.) · "He picked up Portuguese while traveling." Did he take a class? (No: he learned informally.) · "I could just make out a signature." Was it easy to see? (No, only just.) · "He made out that he'd done all the work." Is that true? (Probably not: he claimed it.)

Meaning: the register swap. Phrasal verbs lean spoken and informal; their single-word synonyms (often from Latin or French) lean written and formal. Neither is better. The skill is choosing by reader, setting and purpose.

"We'll look into the complaint." / "We will investigate the complaint." Same meaning? (Yes.) Which one in a meeting with your team? (look into.) Which one in a formal reply to a customer? (investigate.) Is "look into" wrong in the reply? (Not wrong, just less formal.) · "I shall relinquish caffeine," said at lunch. Correct grammar? (Yes.) Good English? (No: absurdly formal. "I'm giving up coffee.") · "The team carried out a survey." In a formal report, must I change it to "conducted"? (No: "carry out" is fine in formal writing.)

Form.

① Intransitiveverb + particle, no objectThe car broke down. · We set off at dawn.
② Separableverb + particle + object or verb + object + particle; a pronoun must splitthrow away the receipts / throw the receipts away / throw them away
③ Inseparableverb + preposition + object; the object (even a pronoun) always followsrely on a friend · rely on her
④ Three-partverb + adverb + preposition + object; never splitlook forward to the trip · run out of it
Heavy objecta long object goes after the particleShe turned off every light in the house before she left.
Noun formsfused spelling, first-syllable stressa breakdown · an outbreak · a takeover · an outcome · input · a setback

Pronunciation. In phrasal verbs (types ①, ②, ④) the particle usually takes the main stress: "set OFF", "turn it OFF", "put up WITH it" (with a noun object, the noun takes it: "put up with the NOISE"). In prepositional verbs (type ③) the verb is stressed and the preposition is weak: "LOOK after them" /ˈlʊk æftər ðəm/, "DEAL with it". The nouns flip to the first syllable: "The car broke DOWN" → "a BREAKdown"; "they took OVER" → "a TAKEover". Linking makes pronoun splits smooth: "turn it off" /tɜrnɪˈtɔf/, "pick it up" /pɪkɪˈtʌp/; in American English the t flaps: "put it off" /pʊɾɪˈɔf/, "set it up" /sɛɾɪˈɾʌp/.

Drill script
  1. Pronoun please (point at box ② or ③): T: "Turn off the heater." → S: "Turn it off." T: "Look after the plants." → S: "Look after them." T: "Throw away the old files." → S: "Throw them away." T: "Put up with the delays." → S: "Put up with them." T: "Pick up the kids at six." → S: "Pick them up at six." Fast, choral then individual.
  2. Swap it up / swap it down (flashcards): T holds up postpone: S: "put off." T: "find out": S: "discover." T: "tolerate": S: "put up with." Then full sentences: T: "We'll look into it. Formal!" → S: "We will investigate the matter." T: "The price has increased. Spoken!" → S: "The price has gone up."
  3. Stress flip: T: "The system broke down." → S: "There was a breakdown." T: "A competitor took them over." → S: "It was a takeover." T: "Things turned out well." → S: "It was a good outcome." Clap the stress each time.
  4. Which one here? T gives a context and a pair: "A text to your brother: put off or postpone the dinner?" → S: "Let's put it off." T: "A letter from a hospital: find out or discover?" → S: "…discovered." T: "A safety report: carry out or conduct?" → S: "Either. Both are fine in formal writing."
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 says something they need to do this week, with a phrasal verb and a noun object ("I need to fill in my tax form."); S2 repeats it with a pronoun ("You need to fill it in.") and then formally ("You are required to complete it."). Then all pairs, 1 minute.
The Register Swap — how to run it
  • Pairs. Give each pair one Register Swap story (Set C): six workplace and community stories (a community center leak, a library app pilot, a clinic's new booking system, a school fundraiser, a delivery company schedule change, a food bank move). Each story has an A card (an informal voice note, dense with phrasal verbs) and a B card (a formal memo about a later stage of the same story, dense with Latinate verbs).
  • Round 1: tell it (4 min). A reads their voice note silently, turns it over and tells it to B in their own words, like a coworker at the coffee machine. B jots down every phrasal verb heard (a tally on the back of the card).
  • Round 2: formalize it (6 min). Together they write a 70–90-word formal update (for the manager, the board or the parents' committee) on the lines in the workbook. Rules: swap wherever the register calls for it; keep at least one phrasal verb on purpose (e.g., carry out, set up, point out) and write K above it; no idioms. Check every pronoun object.
  • Round 3: reverse it (3 min). B reads the formal memo on their card, turns it over, and gives A a 30-second spoken update in natural spoken English: "OK, so basically, they've put off the…". A listens for any "stiff" word that survived and says "Too formal!" when they hear one.
  • Round 4: peer review (2 min). Pairs join another pair and swap written updates. Each pair gives Praise – Question – Polish: one swap that lands, one question about a choice ("Why did you keep set up?"), one word to polish.
  • Roles: in a group of 3, the third learner is the register judge: holds the "Which fits?" context cards, decides disputes, and reads the final version aloud. Give this job to a dominant speaker.
  • Early finishers: deal three "Which fits?" context cards (a text to a friend, a job interview, a complaint letter, a team stand-up, a university essay, a podcast, a safety report, a wedding toast) and say the same sentence in the right register for each.
ICQs before the Register Swap
A, do you read your card aloud? (No, read it silently, then tell it.) · B, what do you write down in Round 1? (Every phrasal verb I hear.) · How long is the formal update? (70 to 90 words.) · Must you change every phrasal verb? (No: keep at least one on purpose and mark it K.) · Idioms in the formal update? (No.) · In Round 3, is B's update formal or spoken? (Spoken, 30 seconds.) · What are the three steps of peer review? (Praise, question, polish.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on six register pairs only (put off / postpone, find out / discover, look into / investigate, go up / increase, set up / establish, put up with / tolerate) and the pronoun rule with it. Give a color-coded card: blue = spoken, red = written. In the Register Swap, the learner tells the story (Round 1) and underlines the phrasal verbs on the partner's draft instead of writing the whole update; accept a 40-word update. Workbook: Ex 2 items 1–4, Ex 3a only. Target: 4 correct swaps and 4 correct pronoun splits, said aloud.

Stretch (toward C2)

Work on the particle's own meaning: up = completion (use up, eat up, drink up), out = to the end / thoroughly (sort out, work out, figure out), off = separation (call off, cut off). Add less common three-part verbs (come up against, get round to, face up to, own up to). In the formal update, use one nominalized phrasal noun (the breakdown of the talks, a positive outcome) and write a one-line justification for every phrasal verb kept. Early finishers write a third version for a different reader (a press release) and explain which features changed.

L1 watch-outs
  • Latinate by default: Spanish, Portuguese, French, Italian and Romanian speakers find investigate, postpone, tolerate easy because of cognates (investigar, postergar, tolerar), and may sound stiff in speech ("I will investigate where my keys are"). Praise the accuracy, then offer the spoken twin. The C1 target for them is the phrasal verb in speech.
  • Pronoun after the particle: "switch on it," "pick up her." Very common for all L1s, including learners whose own language has particle verbs. Always test with it.
  • German and Dutch separable verbs (anrufen → ruft … an, opbellen) help with the idea of splitting but can push the particle to the very end of a long clause ("She turned every light in the house she had lived in for years off"). Teach the heavy-object rule.
  • Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Vietnamese and Arabic speakers may avoid phrasal verbs altogether and use the single-word verb everywhere; their writing is accurate but their speech sounds formal. Praise every attempt at a phrasal verb in speech.
  • Extra or missing prepositions: "discuss about," "enter into the room" (Arabic, Spanish, Russian, Persian), but also "look forward to see you" (to is a preposition: look forward to seeing). Link to the three-part box.
  • Verb vs noun spelling: "There was a break down." "The out come was positive." Some Tagalog and Spanish speakers also stress both parts equally. Drill the noun stress.
Homework

Online lesson 4.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_10.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back, stressing the particles; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "Narrate, Then Formalize" project (record Version A on a phone if possible). Workbook 4.1: finish the Register Swap planner with a second story (your own, a new identity card, or another story card), "My new words" and the journal (one idea from the week said twice: with a phrasal verb to a friend, with its single-word twin in a formal email). Optional, from the online lesson: collect five phrasal verbs from something you read or watched, with type, formal twin and register.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B Could you turn it off? · Q2 C we will investigate the complaint · Q3 (new) B I can't put up with it any longer. Launch debrief (Ex 1): 1 fell through (①) · 2 pulled out (①) · 3 put it off (②) · 4 set up (②) · 5 held up (①) · 6 picked up (①) · 7 look into (③) · 8 cut down on (④).

4.2 · C11

Collocation, Idiom & Connotation

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • use natural collocations of five types (heavy traffic · make a decision · deeply concerned · apologize profusely · a round of applause) and avoid translated ones (make a photo, strong rain, do a mistake)
  • tell strong collocations (fixed, predictable: bitterly cold, meet a deadline, pay attention) from weak ones (open, combinable: very cold, a good idea) and upgrade a weak one when precision matters
  • place near-synonyms on a connotation cline (slender · slim · thin · skinny · scrawny; thrifty · economical · stingy) and choose the word that praises, stays neutral or criticizes
  • use an idiom accurately and appropriately, and rephrase it plainly for formal writing (the tip of the iceberg → only a small fraction of the problem)
  • discuss how vocabulary is kept alive ("use it or lose it") and plan to use new chunks in real life
Materials
  • Workbook 4.2 (New words, Lexis box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, the Precise Talk planner)
  • Slides 9–13 · Collocation picture cards (coffee, umbrella, winter, worried, nervous, angry, sad, mobile phone) · Cline cards (6 envelopes) · Idiom cards + "Use it or lose it" discussion cards · Chunk-bingo listener cards · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 4.2-A (the pilot program meeting) and 4.2-B (dictation)

Left: partners. adj + noun: heavy rain · strong coffee · a bitter disappointment · verb + noun: make a decision · do research · meet a deadline · raise an issue · adv + adj: deeply concerned · highly unlikely · fully aware · verb + adv: apologize profusely · noun + noun: a round of applause · a pang of guilt. make a photo → take · strong rain → heavy.

Center: weak → strong. very cold → bitterly cold · very unlikely → highly unlikely · very worried → deeply concerned · a big problem → a pressing problem. The cline: 🙂 slender · slim — 😐 thin — 🙁 skinny · scrawny. 🙂 thrifty · frugal — 😐 economical — 🙁 stingy · tight. Am I praising or criticizing?

Right: idioms. bite the bullet · get cold feet · the tip of the iceberg · on the same page · a gray area · food for thought · hit the nail on the head · cut corners. Three rules: spoken (not in formal writing) · a pinch, not a plateful · exact form (hit the nail on its head). Bottom corner: USE IT OR LOSE IT.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: register tennis7S–SReview C10. Pairs, register-pair flashcards face down. A turns a card and "serves" the formal verb in a sentence ("The council will investigate the noise."); B "returns" it as spoken English with a pronoun ("They'll look into it."). Swap every card. Then two pairs share their homework "found" phrasal verbs. Note pronoun slips for the feedback stage.
Lead-in: which sounds English?5T–SSlide 9. Picture cards: coffee, umbrella, winter. "Powerful coffee or strong coffee? Strong rain or heavy rain? Very cold or bitterly cold?" All are grammatical and understandable, so why does one sound right? Elicit "because people say it." "That's collocation, and it makes you sound native-like even more than grammar does."
Collocation: types, strong & weak12T–SSlide 10. The five types, one example each from the pictures. Then strong vs weak: a strong collocation is fixed and predictable (you hear bitterly, you expect cold); a weak one combines with almost anything (very, good, big). Weak is never wrong, but strong is precise. Show upgrades. Then the translation trap (make a photo, do a mistake, strong rain, say a joke): each learner says one collocation from their own language that doesn't translate. CCQs.
Collocation grid8S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2. Learners tick the grid alone (verbs make / do / take / pay / raise / meet × nouns; adverbs × adjectives), then compare. Where pairs disagree, they must say the collocation aloud in a sentence and decide by ear. Board-check the famous traps only (make a decision / do research / take a risk / pay a compliment).
Drill5T–S → SCollocation chains and the upgrade drill (see Drill script). Choral, then individuals.
Connotation clines10S–S → T–SSlide 11. Pairs open a cline envelope (Set D) and arrange the word cards on the cline strip from 🙂 to 🙁 (the body set: slender, slim, thin, skinny, scrawny; then money, confidence, fame, curiosity, determination). Then they check each against the invented character on the envelope ("Leon never pays for coffee": thrifty or stingy?). Class feedback: CCQs, and childlike / childish. Remind: invented people only.
Idioms: listen & judge8T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 4.2-A twice (a team meeting about a failed pilot program; give the B lines to a confident learner). First listen: "What went wrong? What do they decide?" Second listen: learners tick the idioms they hear and match them to meanings. Then slide 12: the three appropriacy rules. Read "This is merely the tip of the iceberg" as a line from a research paper: "Right or wrong here?" (Wrong register: represents only a small fraction of the problem.)
"Use it or lose it"5S–S (groups of 3)Board: USE IT OR LOSE IT. "Is this an idiom? What does it mean?" (If you don't practice a skill, you lose it.) Groups take one discussion card (Set E) and talk for 4 minutes: Is it true of vocabulary? Of idioms in particular? Which words have you "lost" in English or your first language? Which three chunks from today will you use this week, and where? Each learner writes their three chunks in the workbook. They report back in the C12 warmer.
Controlled practice8S → S–SWorkbook Ex 3–5 alone (upgrade the weak collocation, choose the shade and register, fix or rephrase the idiom). Pairs check. Board-check disagreements. Early finishers start the talk planner.
Freer: the Precise Talk12S → S–S (groups of 3)Slide 13. Plan (3 min): each learner picks a topic (a place they know well, a trend in their field, a decision they had to make, a piece of news that gave them pause; real or from a new identity card) and notes 6 collocations and 1–2 idioms that fit. Talk (3 × 90 seconds): the two listeners each hold a chunk-bingo card and tick every strong collocation and idiom they hear; one of them also listens for a register slip. Shades + feedback (90 seconds): after each talk, the speaker explains one cline they'd use for the topic ("I'd describe the invented manager on my card as frugal, not stingy, because…"), and each listener gives one line of Praise – Question – Polish. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SThree columns (no names). Accuracy: "We need to do a decision." · "It was a blessing in the disguise." · "strongly unlikely." Range: "a pressing concern," "it paid off handsomely." Appropriacy / connotation: "My friend is very skinny and beautiful." (critical word, admiring intent) · "The tip of the iceberg" in a written report. Learners fix and explain.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 4.2 and a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Point at the Advanced Communicator topic cards on the side table: "Choose a topic before C12. You'll give a three-minute talk." Learners tick "I can…".
Total907 + 5 + 12 + 8 + 5 + 10 + 8 + 5 + 8 + 12 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: collocation. Words that habitually occur together and sound right to an expert ear, often for no logical reason. The alternative is usually grammatical and understandable; it just isn't what people say.

"There was strong rain." Can you understand it? (Yes.) Is it English? (Not quite: we say heavy rain.) Is there a rule? (No, it's convention: learn the pair.) · "She made a decision." "She did a decision"? (No.) · "I need to take a photo." Why not "make"? (Convention again. Many languages use "make" here.)

Meaning: strong vs weak collocations.

"It was bitterly cold." If I say "bitterly," can you guess the next word? (Yes: cold, disappointed.) Strong or weak? (Strong: a fixed pair.) · "It was very cold." Is "very" wrong? (No, but it goes with everything: weak.) Which is more precise and vivid? (bitterly cold.) · "bitterly hot"? (No: "bitterly" goes with cold and disappointment, not heat.)

Meaning: connotation. Same denotation (dictionary meaning), different feeling. The word you choose shows your judgment.

"She's slender." Compliment or criticism? (Compliment.) · "She's skinny." Same body? (Yes.) Same message? (No: critical, maybe unhealthy.) · "He's thrifty." Is that good? (Yes: sensible with money.) "He's stingy." (Bad: he won't share.) · "The town is notorious for its traffic." Famous for something good? (No, for something bad.) · "a childlike sense of wonder" vs "Stop being so childish." Which one is positive? (childlike.)

Meaning: idiom appropriacy.

"We finally bit the bullet and booked the repairs." Did anyone bite anything? (No: they did something unpleasant but necessary.) Spoken or written English? (Mostly spoken.) · In a formal report, "This is just the tip of the iceberg." OK? (No. "This represents only a small fraction of the problem.") · "He hit the nail on its head." Nearly right. Is nearly right OK for an idiom? (No: idioms are fixed. A broken idiom is worse than none.)

Form.

adj + nouna strong argument · heavy traffic · a bitter disappointment · a key issue · a valid pointWe made a strong argument for the change.
verb + nounmake a decision · do research · pay attention · raise an issue · meet a deadline · take a riskShe raised an important issue.
adv + adjdeeply concerned · highly unlikely · bitterly cold · fully aware · widely regarded (as)It's highly unlikely to rain.
verb + advapologize profusely · argue convincingly · fail miserablyHe apologized profusely.
noun + nouna round of applause · a surge of anger · a pang of guilt · a flurry of activityThere was a flurry of activity.
Idiomsfixed form; mostly informal; one or two per talkLet's make sure we're on the same page.

Pronunciation. Say a collocation as one chunk, with no pause inside it: "heavy RAIN," "make a deCIsion," "DEEPly conCERNED." In adverb + adjective pairs both words are stressed, and the adverb often carries the emphasis ("It's HIGHly unlikely"). Idioms are also chunks; the last content word carries the stress: "bite the BULlet," "the tip of the ICEberg," "food for THOUGHT." Connotation lives in the voice too: a warm fall on "She's SLENder ↘" vs a flat, clipped "He's STINgy." American English: gray area; the flap in "a lot of" /əˈlɑɾəv/.

Drill script
  1. Finish the chunk (fast): T: "heavy…" → S: "traffic / rain." T: "meet a…" → S: "deadline." T: "pay…" → S: "attention." T: "deeply…" → S: "concerned." T: "a round of…" → S: "applause." T: "bitterly…" → S: "cold / disappointed."
  2. Upgrade it: T: "It's very unlikely." → S: "It's highly unlikely." T: "I'm very worried." → S: "I'm deeply concerned." T: "He said sorry a lot." → S: "He apologized profusely." T: "It was a big disappointment." → S: "It was a bitter disappointment."
  3. Fix the translation: T: "Can you make a photo?" → S: "Can you take a photo?" T: "I did a mistake." → S: "I made a mistake." T: "There was strong rain." → S: "There was heavy rain."
  4. Praise it / criticize it (cline cards): T: "He's careful with money. Praise!" → S: "He's thrifty." T: "Criticize!" → S: "He's stingy." T: "She's sure of herself. Praise!" → S: "She's self-assured." T: "Criticize!" → S: "She's arrogant."
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 describes an invented person or an object with a neutral word ("My neighbor's dog is thin."); S2 says it twice, once kindly and once critically ("It's slender. / It's scrawny."). Then all pairs, 1 minute.
The Precise Talk and "Use it or lose it" — how to run them
  • 90 seconds, not three minutes. The online project asks for 2–3 minutes; in class, 90 seconds per speaker in groups of 3 keeps it moving. The full version is homework. Three minutes of planning plus three speakers × (90 seconds of talk + 90 seconds of shades and feedback) fill the 12 minutes exactly.
  • Chunk bingo. Each listener has a 4 × 4 card (Set E) with collocations from the lesson, an "idiom used well" square and three free squares ("a strong collocation not on the card," "a precise near-synonym"). They tick what they hear. One listener also has a register-slip box. The bingo is feedback, not a competition: after the talk, the listener reads out the two best chunks.
  • Don't force the third idiom. Say it before planning: "One or two idioms that genuinely fit. If it sounds bolted on, cut it." Praise the learner who didn't use an idiom because it didn't fit.
  • Shades. After each talk, the speaker explains one near-synonym set as it applies to their topic: "If I were writing about my town for a tourist brochure, I'd say it's renowned for its market; in a complaint to the council, I'd say it's notorious for parking." Push for register and connotation, both.
  • "Use it or lose it" (5 min, before the talk). Groups of 3 take one discussion card. Questions: Is "use it or lose it" true of vocabulary? Have you ever "lost" words in your first language or in English? Are idioms more fragile than collocations? What's a realistic way to use three new chunks this week? Each learner writes three chunks and one place they'll use them (an email at work, a conversation with a neighbor, a message to a friend). This becomes the C12 warmer report. Keep it light: nobody is asked about losing their first language unless they choose to talk about it.
  • Roles: the chunk counter (a dominant speaker) holds the bingo card and gives feedback last.
ICQs before the Precise Talk
How long is each talk? (90 seconds.) · How many collocations do you plan? (At least six.) · How many idioms? (One or two, only if they fit.) · What do listeners do during the talk? (Tick the chunks on the bingo card.) · Is the bingo a competition? (No, it's feedback.) · After the talk, what does the speaker explain? (One set of near-synonyms and how to choose.) · Can you describe a real classmate as "skinny" or "stingy"? (No, only invented people.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on verb + noun and adverb + adjective only, with the picture cards: heavy rain, strong coffee, bitterly cold, deeply concerned, take a photo, make a decision. In the grid, give only the top half (make / do / take × 6 nouns). For the clines, give the money set and the confidence set (4 cards each) with 🙂 😐 🙁 faces on the strip. In the talk, a 45-second version with 3 collocations and no idiom is a complete task. Target: 6 collocations and one cline said aloud correctly.

Stretch (toward C2)

Introduce semantic prosody: some words attract negative partners (cause problems / damage / delays, not cause success; rife with corruption / rumors; set in: the rain / boredom set in). Ask them to find two more. Add finer clines (frugal vs thrifty: frugal is closer to austere; slender vs slim: slender is more elegant and literary). In the talk, vary one idiom deliberately for effect ("the tip of a very large iceberg") and explain why it works. Write a formal paragraph on the same topic with no idioms and compare.

L1 watch-outs
  • make / do / take: "make a photo" (German ein Foto machen, Russian sdelat' foto, Spanish hacer una foto, Dutch een foto maken); "do a mistake" (Arabic, Hindi, Greek and others use one "do/make" verb); "make research," "do a decision." The priority collocations to correct every time.
  • "strong rain" / "big rain": Spanish lluvia fuerte, Italian pioggia forte, Chinese dà yǔ (big rain), Japanese ōame (big rain). Teach heavy rain / traffic / snow as a family.
  • "win money" for a salary (Spanish ganar, Portuguese ganhar, French gagner): earn / make money; you win a prize or the lottery.
  • Translated idioms: learners may translate a colorful idiom from their own language word for word. Enjoy it (it's often a great cultural moment), then give the English equivalent if there is one. Keep a class "idiom exchange" corner on the board.
  • False-friend connotations: Spanish flaco is often affectionate (even a nickname), but skinny is usually critical in English; Portuguese and Spanish ambicioso, French ambitieux can be negative, while ambitious is usually positive in American English. Ask "praising or criticizing?" every time.
  • Idiom density: learners who have studied idioms from lists (common in exam-prep backgrounds everywhere) may overload a talk with them. Praise accuracy, then apply the "a pinch, not a plateful" rule.
Homework

Online lesson 4.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_11.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back as chunks; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and "The Rich, Precise Talk" (record the 2–3 minute version). Workbook 4.2: use your three "use it or lose it" chunks in real life this week and note where; start "My phrasebook" (five collocations, three idioms with a note on when to use them, one near-synonym set) and write the journal (an invented person, place or object described twice, kindly and critically). Choose your C12 topic from the Advanced Communicator topic cards and bring two or three pieces of evidence or examples.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A There was heavy traffic, so we had to make a quick decision. · Q2 A thrifty · Q3 (new) C nosy. Pilot program meeting (Ex 1): idioms heard: the last straw · cut corners · hit the nail on the head · the tip of the iceberg · a blessing in disguise · on the same page · food for thought · bite the bullet. Not heard: get cold feet · a gray area.

4.3 · C12

Review Project — The Advanced Communicator

90 min
A task-based review, not a new-language class

There is no new grammar today. The new language is the register spectrum (shifting vocabulary, grammar and structure as a set), a handful of rhetorical devices used sparingly, signposting for extended talk, and diplomatic Q&A. The class is built around one combined task in three parts: a three-minute persuasive presentation to a small group, two minutes of questions and challenges, and a 150-word formal written summary of the argument that includes the strongest objection and the response. You listen and read with the speaking & writing checklist (Set G). Language work comes from the tasks: a short focus-on-form clinic between them. The checklist results feed C13 (Progress Test 1): they are your spoken and written evidence for Modules 1–4, and they tell you who goes to which catch-up station.

Objectives — learners can…
  • shift the register of one message (formal ↔ neutral ↔ informal), changing vocabulary, grammar and structure together, and repair a register clash
  • structure and deliver a three-minute persuasive talk with a compelling opening, two or three developed points and a memorable close, signposted at every join
  • use one or two rhetorical devices at the hinges of the argument (a rhetorical question, a rule of three, contrast, a cleft or inversion)
  • field questions and challenges diplomatically, conceding before countering (I take your point, but I'd argue… · That's fair, though I'd push back on one thing…)
  • write a 150-word formal summary of their argument (nominalized, hedged, no idioms) that fairly reports the strongest objection and their response
  • deploy structures and lexis from all four modules (aspect and modality, the subjunctive, mixed conditionals, inversion and clefts, nominalization, cohesion, hedging, phrasal verbs, collocation) in extended, controlled output
Materials
  • Workbook 4.3 (phrase banks, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, the talk planner, the Q&A card, the written summary page, the Module 4 "I can" checklist)
  • Slides 14–18 · Register Ladder review game (Set H) + dice + counters · Advanced Communicator topic cards, side cards, persona cards, challenge starters, talking tokens (Set F) · Speaking & writing checklist: one class grid + score slips (Set G) · a phone timer per group
  • Listening scripts 4.3-A (the model talk and Q&A: a paid learning day) and 4.3-B ("Clash or consistent?", for the clinic or a C13 station) · optional: phones for recording

Left: the talk map. ① OPEN a rhetorical question or a bold claim · I'd like to make the case that… → ② PREVIEW There are three points I want to raise. → ③ POINTS claim + evidence + example · This brings me to… · Turning now to… · Crucially,… · Admittedly,… That said,… → ④ CLOSE In essence,… · The choice, then, is a simple one. → ⑤ Q&A I take your point, but I'd argue… · That's a fair challenge. What I'd say is…

Center: the spectrum (shift as a set). FORMAL The initiative will commence next quarter and is anticipated to yield significant benefits. · NEUTRAL The project starts next quarter and should bring real benefits. · INFORMAL We're kicking this off next quarter, and it should pay off nicely. Clash: The committee will sort out the discrepancies. → resolve. Devices (one or two!): Can we really afford to wait? · cheaper, cleaner and already here · not a lack of money but a lack of will · What we need is…

Right: the C1 toolkit. M1 will have shifted · may well prove · It is essential that it be… M2 Had we acted…, we would now… · But for… · Not only… but… · What we need is… M3 The introduction of… · the former / the latter · It would seem that… M4 look into / investigate · a pressing concern · deeply concerned · (spoken only) the tip of the iceberg. Written summary = formal: nominalize, hedge, no idioms.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: Register Ladder10S–S (groups of 3–4)Review C1–C11 with the Register Ladder board game (Set H). Roll, move, answer the square in full sentences; USE IT squares ask the player to report one of their three "use it or lose it" chunks from the C11 homework (where they used it, how it went). Stop after 10 minutes; nearest the finish wins. Walk around and note which squares (which modules) cause trouble: early evidence for C13.
The communicator's toolkit12T–S → S–SSlides 14–16. (a) Spectrum (4 min): one message in three registers; elicit what changed (vocabulary, voice, sentence length, contractions, phrasal verbs, hedging). (b) Devices and signposts (4 min): learners name the device in four model sentences (Workbook Ex 3), and you set the rule: one or two, at the hinges. (c) One point, two voices (4 min): two learners read the formal and informal versions of "the deadline should be pushed back" (slide 16); pairs find five features that shifted together. CCQs.
Model: listen7T–SRead Script 4.3-A twice (a three-minute talk proposing one paid learning day a month, followed by two challenges; ask two confident learners to read the questioners). First listen: "What's the proposal? How many points? What objection comes up, and how does she answer it?" Second listen: Workbook Ex 1: number the signposts in order and tick the C1 structures you hear.
Plan the talk11SSlide 17. Learners fill the talk planner (Workbook 4.3): notes, not a script. Topic from the Set F cards (chosen after C11 or drawn now) or their own everyday topic. Opening device, preview, two or three points (claim + evidence + example), close. Then the Q&A card: two likely objections and a concede-and-counter answer to each. The planner has a toolkit strip (M1–M4): each learner plans at least one structure from each module. Walk around and check that each plan has a signpost at every join.
Presentation circles + Q&A + checklist20S–S (groups of 4) · T–SGroups of 4. Each speaker presents for up to 3 minutes (planner turned over; a quick look is OK), then takes 2 minutes of questions and challenges: each listener asks one, using a challenge starter (Set F). A timekeeper in each group. Listeners also write one note on the back of their challenge card: the speaker's main claim in one sentence and the best challenge. The speaker collects them: they help with the written summary. You sit with one group at a time and score speaking criteria 1–5.
Focus-on-form clinic5T–SBoard 4 sentences you heard (no names): 2 to fix, 2 to upgrade (e.g., "It is essential that he is told" → be told · "Not only it saves money" → does it save · "We have to look into this deeply" → investigate this thoroughly (formal) · "This is the tip of the iceberg" → fine in the talk, but rephrase it for the summary). Learners fix in pairs; check. Keep it short and positive.
Written summary + peer review15S → S–SSlide 17. Learners write a 150-word formal summary of their own argument on the workbook page, as if for a committee's briefing pack: the proposal, the two strongest reasons, the strongest objection raised in the Q&A (reported with a precise reporting verb and hedging) and their response. Formal register throughout: nominalize, hedge, Latinate verbs where they fit, no idioms, no contractions. 11 minutes writing; then 4 minutes of peer review with the checklist on the page (register consistent? objection reported fairly? one Praise – Question – Polish). Collect the summaries: you score writing criteria W1–W4 after class.
Feedback + halfway wrap5T–SContent first: "Which argument was the strongest you heard? Did anyone change your mind, even a little?" Then 2–3 sentences from your checklist notes, no names: one range example, one appropriacy example. Learners tick the Module 4 "I can…" lists. Celebrate: halfway through C1.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 4.3; record the full 4–5 minute version of your talk at home, plus the register shift (formal and informal versions of your core message); the journal. Tell learners plainly about C13: "Next class is Progress Test 1 on Modules 1–4, plus catch-up time. Today's checklist helps me plan that time for you." Hand out score slips in C13, not today.
Total9010 + 12 + 7 + 11 + 20 + 5 + 15 + 5 + 5
Hearing everyone

In 20 minutes of circles you can score about 8 speakers well (two groups). Choose one of these for the rest, and tell the class which one before the circles: (a) learners record their talk and Q&A on a phone during the circles and play it for you during the C13 catch-up time; (b) the rest present to you at a station in C13; (c) in a large class, split the class: half present in C12 while the other half act as challengers, and the second half present in C13. The written summaries give you evidence for everyone. Anyone who prefers to present only to you, not a group, may do so. That is a normal choice, not a problem.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation (review focus)

Meaning: register shifts as a set.

"The committee will sort out the discrepancies." What's wrong? (A formal subject and noun with an informal phrasal verb: a register clash.) Two fixes? ("…will resolve the discrepancies," or all informal: "The committee's going to sort out the mess.") · "Hey team, pursuant to our discussion…" Why is it funny? ("Hey team" is informal; "pursuant to" is legal English.) · Is the formal register always best at work? (No: it can sound cold. Warmth lives in the neutral middle.)

Meaning: rhetoric is architecture.

"Can we really afford to wait another decade?" Does the speaker want an answer? (No: the listener supplies it: "No, we can't.") · "It is cheaper, it is cleaner, and it is already here." Why three? (Rhythm, balance, a sense of completeness.) · Three rhetorical questions in a row: more persuasive? (No: it sounds like a game-show host. Use devices at the hinges: the opening, the turn, the close.)

Meaning: diplomatic Q&A.

"I take your point, but I'd argue that the cost falls over time." Does the speaker agree? (Partly: they concede, then counter.) Why concede first? (It shows they listened and makes their view sound reasonable.) · "That's a fair challenge. What I'd say is…" Is that weak? (No: calm and confident.) · "No, that's wrong." Same effect? (No: combative; the discussion stops.)

Meaning: talk vs written summary.

In the talk you said, "Honestly, it's a no-brainer." Can that go in the written summary? (No: informal idiom.) What would? ("The benefits would appear to outweigh the costs.") · In the summary, how do you report the objection? (With a reporting verb and fairly: "It was argued that… / One colleague questioned whether…") · Contractions in the summary? (No.)

Form: the review table.

Registerformal: nominal, passive, hedged, Latinate, long subordinated sentences, no contractions · neutral: balanced, some hedging · informal: verbal, active, contracted, phrasal verbs, discourse markers (so, look, honestly)commence → start → kick off · require → need → could use
Devicesrhetorical question · rule of three · repetition (anaphora) · contrast (antithesis) · cleft or inversion · one well-placed idiom (spoken only)The problem is not a lack of money, but a lack of will.
SignpostsLet me begin by… · There are three points I want to raise. · This brings me to… · Turning now to… · Crucially,… · To put it another way,… · Admittedly,… That said,… · In essence,… · The bottom line is this:…This brings me to my second point.
Q&AI take your point, but I'd argue… · That's a fair challenge; what I'd say is… · I'd push back on one thing… · If I understand you correctly, your concern is… · That's beyond the scope of this proposal, but…If I understand you correctly, your concern is cost.
Module 1future perfect / continuous · modals of speculation (may well, might as well, must have) · the subjunctive (It is essential that it be…)By 2030, the savings will have paid for the scheme.
Module 2mixed conditionals and if-alternatives (Had we…, But for…, Supposing…) · inversion (Not only…, Rarely…) · clefts and fronting (What we need is…, It was… that…)Had we invested earlier, we would be ahead now.
Module 3nominalization · cohesion (reference, substitution, ellipsis) · hedging and diplomatic languageThe introduction of the scheme would appear to reduce turnover.
Module 4phrasal verbs by register · strong collocations · connotation · idioms (spoken, sparingly)It remains a pressing concern that we must look into.

Pronunciation. Signposts are chunks followed by a clear pause: "This brings me to | my second point." Slow down for the preview and the close. A rule of three rises, rises, falls: "It's cheaper ↗ | it's cleaner ↗ | and it's already here ↘." Contrast puts the stress on the opposed words: "not a lack of MONey, but a lack of WILL." In a cleft, the spotlight word carries the stress: "What we need is time." The concede-and-counter move has two tunes: a warm fall on "I take your point ↘" and then a fresh, higher start on "but I'd argue…". In the formal register, keep full forms and a steadier pace; in the informal restatement, contract and speed up.

Drill script
  1. Three rungs: T gives a neutral line and a direction: "We need more time. Up!" → S: "Additional time will be required." "Down!" → S: "We could really use a bit more time." T: "The project starts in May. Up!" → S: "The project will commence in May." "Down!" → S: "We're kicking it off in May."
  2. Signpost chain: T: "Topic: a paid learning day." → S1: "I'd like to make the case that…" → S2: "There are three points I want to raise." → S3: "Let me begin by…" → S4: "This brings me to…" → S5: "In essence,…" Round the room; pause after each signpost.
  3. Concede → counter: T reads a challenge ("Won't that cost too much?"); S answers with a frame and one reason: "I take your point, but I'd argue that the cost of replacing staff is far higher." Rotate: That's a fair challenge; what I'd say is… · I'd push back on one thing…
  4. Upgrade with a module: T says a plain sentence and names a module; S upgrades it. T: "I'm sure it will help. M1." → S: "It may well prove decisive." T: "We didn't act, so we're behind. M2." → S: "Had we acted, we wouldn't be behind now." T: "They introduced the policy, and waste went down. M3." → S: "The introduction of the policy led to a marked reduction in waste." T: "We'll check it. M4, formal." → S: "We will investigate the matter."
The Advanced Communicator — how to run it
  • Three minutes, not five. The online capstone asks for 4–5 minutes; in class, three minutes plus two minutes of Q&A per speaker keeps four speakers inside 20 minutes and still shows structure and range. The full version is homework (a phone recording, with the register shift).
  • Topics. Everyday proposals and trade-offs in workplace, education, technology, health habits and community life (Set F: a paid learning day, a four-day week for office jobs, phones locked away in school for under-16s, apprenticeships vs degrees, car-free town centers on Sundays, AI tools for homework, pricier sugary drinks in workplace cafeterias, public libraries as community hubs, remote work as the default for office jobs, first-aid training for every adult). A learner's own topic is welcome if it stays in this territory. The side is on the card or chosen freely; it is not the speaker's personal opinion unless they want it to be.
  • Q&A. Every listener asks one question or challenge with a starter (Set F: "Isn't there a risk that…?" · "How would that work for…?" · "You said…, but surely…?" · "What would you say to someone who…?"). The speaker concedes before countering at least once. The timekeeper stops the Q&A after two minutes, kindly. A speaker may say "That's beyond the scope of this proposal, but…" once: a legitimate C1 move.
  • Challenge notes feed the summary. After each talk, listeners write the speaker's main claim in one sentence and the best challenge on the back of their card, and hand them over. The speaker now has an outsider's version of their argument and the strongest objection, ready for the written summary.
  • The written summary is a register shift of the talk: the same argument, formal and concise, for readers who weren't there. 150 words (130–170 is fine). It must include the strongest objection, reported fairly with a reporting verb (One participant questioned whether…) and hedged (It could be argued that…), and the response. No idioms, no contractions, no rhetorical questions. The page has a peer-review checklist.
  • Talking tokens in the Q&A if one listener dominates: two each; one token per question. Give the timekeeper and last questioner roles to the most dominant speakers in each group.
  • Persona cards (a hospital night-shift nurse, a small-business owner, a university student, a parent of three, a retired teacher, a city bus driver) give learners a point of view that isn't theirs: "Speaking as a small-business owner, I'd be deeply concerned about…". Great for quieter learners and for anyone who doesn't want to speak for themselves.
  • If a Q&A gets heated, the timekeeper (or you) says: "Great challenge. One sentence to answer, then we move on." Challenge the idea, never the person.
The speaking & writing checklist — how to score it (feeds C13)

Speaking: five criteria, 0–3 points each, /15. Writing: four criteria, 0–3 points each, /12. 0 = no response · 1 = not yet C1 · 2 = C1 · 3 = strong C1 (moving toward C2). Score the whole spoken performance across the talk and the Q&A, and the written summary separately. Tick the module boxes (M1–M4) when you hear or read a structure from that module used correctly. The full descriptors, the class grid and the score slips are in the classroom materials (Set G).

CriterionWhat 3 points looks like
S1 · Task & organizationA compelling opening, a preview, 2–3 developed points each with evidence or an example, a memorable close; signposted at every join; within time.
S2 · Range (Modules 1–4)Structures from at least 3 of the 4 modules used naturally and without prompting; precise, strong collocations; one or two devices well placed.
S3 · AccuracyConsistent control of complex grammar; errors are rare, minor and often self-corrected.
S4 · Register & interactionRegister consistent and appropriate to the audience, shifted on purpose; Q&A handled diplomatically (concedes before countering, clarifies the question, stays calm).
S5 · Fluency & deliveryEven pace with pauses at signposts; stress highlights contrast and key points; little searching for words; easy to follow for three minutes.
W1 · ContentAccurately summarizes the proposal and main reasons; reports the strongest objection fairly and responds to it; 130–170 words.
W2 · Organization & cohesionLogical order; cohesive devices (reference, linkers, the former / the latter) make it read as one text.
W3 · Register & lexisConsistently formal: nominalization, hedging, precise collocations, Latinate verbs where they fit; no idioms, contractions or clashes.
W4 · AccuracyComplex sentences controlled; errors rare and do not affect meaning; punctuation supports the structure.

Feeding C13. Record each learner's scores and module ticks on the class grid. In C13, after Progress Test 1, send learners to a catch-up station for any module with no tick and for their lowest criterion: no M1 tick → time & modality station (future perfect/continuous, may well / might as well / needn't have, the subjunctive); no M2 tick → conditionals & emphasis station (mixed conditionals, Had I… / But for…, inversion, clefts); no M3 tick → cohesion & hedging station (nominalization rewrites, reference and ellipsis, softening); no M4 tick → lexis station (Register Swap cards and cline cards from Sets C and D); S4 or W3 ≤ 1 → register-shift station (Workbook 4.3 Ex 2 and 4 + one "three rungs" round); S5 ≤ 1 → shadowing station (the 🔊 model sentences in online lessons 4.1–4.3); W4 ≤ 1 → editing station (rewrite two sentences of your own summary with the teacher's codes). If Progress Test 1 has its own speaking and writing tasks, keep this grid anyway: it is your evidence of extended output for Modules 1–4, and it lets you give each learner one kind, specific next step.

ICQs before the presentations and the summary
Presentations: How long do you speak? (Up to three minutes.) · Can you read your notes? (Only a quick look.) · What does each listener do? (Asks one question or challenge, then writes the main claim and the best challenge on the card.) · How do you answer a challenge? (Concede, then counter.) · Do you have to present to the group? (No, you can present only to the teacher.)
Summary: Who is the reader? (A committee that wasn't there.) · How many words? (About 150.) · Formal or informal? (Formal.) · Idioms or contractions? (No.) · What must you include besides your reasons? (The strongest objection and your response.) · What do you do in the last four minutes? (Peer review with the checklist.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give a talk strip: "I'd like to make the case that… · There are two points I want to raise. · Let me begin by… because… · This brings me to… For example,… · In essence,…" and a reply strip: "I take your point, but… · That's a fair challenge. What I'd say is…". A 90-second talk with two points is a complete task. For the summary, give a frame with the first words of each sentence ("This proposal argues that… · The principal reason is… · A further benefit would be… · It was argued, however, that… · In response,…") and accept 80–100 words. Score on the same checklist: a clear, well-signposted short talk can earn full marks on S1 and S5.

Stretch (toward C2)

Use one structure from each module and one device at each hinge (opening, turn, close), no more. In the Q&A, paraphrase the challenge before answering ("If I understand you correctly, your concern is…"). Shift register within the talk on purpose: open formally, land the key point with one well-judged informal line, and explain the choice afterward. In the summary, use a concessive structure (Whilst / While it is true that…, …) and one nominalized phrasal noun (the likely outcome). Homework: the 4–5 minute version with no notes, plus the formal and informal restatements.

L1 watch-outs
  • Over-formal by default: speakers of Romance languages, Greek and Russian (where academic speech is highly nominal) may deliver the whole talk in the formal register. Praise the control, then ask for one neutral or informal moment to create warmth.
  • Contractions and idioms in the summary: learners who have worked hard on idioms may carry them into writing. Link to C11: "Spoken: yes. Written summary: rephrase it."
  • False friends in signposts: "Resuming…" (Spanish resumiendo, French en résumé) → In essence / To sum up; "Actually…" meaning "currently" (Spanish actualmente, French actuellement, German aktuell); "Eventually…" meaning "possibly" (Spanish eventualmente, French éventuellement, German eventuell).
  • "According to me…" (French selon moi, Spanish según yo, Indian English): according to is for other sources. Offer "In my view…" or "I'd argue that…".
  • The subjunctive: "It is essential that he is told" is common in British English and acceptable, but the C1 target (Lesson 1.3) is that he be told; the mandative subjunctive is standard in formal American English. Don't mark the indicative wrong; tick M1 only for the subjunctive.
  • Directness differs. Some learners counter without conceding ("No, this is not correct."); others concede without countering, so their view disappears. Teach the English middle path, concede and then counter, as a technique, not a personality change.
Homework

Online lesson 4.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_12.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Record the full 4–5 minute version of your talk, your answers to the two objections, and the register shift (your core message once formally, once informally), then listen back with the project checklist. Workbook 4.3: Exercises 2, 4 and 5 (register shifts, clash repairs and toolkit upgrades: good preparation for C13), a clean copy of your written summary if you want more feedback, and the journal (one thing you can do now that you couldn't at the start of C1, and one thing to sharpen next). The online journal prompt (your core message in three registers, annotated) is a strong optional extra. Review the Module 1–4 "I can…" lists before C13.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C A rhetorical question ("Can we really afford to wait?") · Q2 B It is imperative that action be taken at once. · Q3 (new) B The committee will resolve the discrepancies. Model talk (Ex 1a, Script 4.3-A): signposts in order: Ask yourself this (1) · I'd like to make the case that (2) · There are three points I want to raise (3) · Let me begin by (4) · This brings me to (5) · Turning now to (6) · In essence (7) · I take your point, but I'd argue (8) · That's a fair challenge. What I'd say is (9). Ex 1b structures heard: future perfect (will have paid for itself), subjunctive (it is essential that the day be protected), mixed conditional (had we offered this earlier, we would not now be…), not only inversion, what-cleft (What we need is…), nominalization (the introduction of…), hedging (would appear to), strong collocation (a pressing concern). Not heard: needn't have, ellipsis with so / not.

Closing the Module

End of C12: Module 4 wrap-up and the halfway point

Recap on slide 18, "choose the verb, the partner, the voice": We'll look into it. / We will investigate the matter. (the register swap) → heavy traffic · a bitter disappointment · slender, not skinny · thrifty, not stingy (the partner and the shade) → one argument, three voices: the talk, the Q&A, the formal summary. Learners tick the three "I can…" lists. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Use those notes, the Register Ladder observations and your checklist to plan the C13 catch-up stations.

Celebrate the halfway point. Learners have finished half of C1. Ask each person to say one thing they can do now that they couldn't do in Week 1, in any register they like ("What I'm proudest of is…", "Never have I spoken for three minutes without notes before!"). Keep it short and warm.

Progress Test 1 (C13) covers Modules 1–4. From this module it can test: the four multi-word verb types and pronoun placement (turn it off / look after it / put up with it), the register pairs in both directions and choosing by context, polysemous phrasal verbs in context, nominalized forms (breakdown, outcome), collocation types and strong collocations (heavy, deeply, highly, meet, make / do / take), connotation (slender / skinny, thrifty / stingy, renowned / notorious, childlike / childish), idiom meaning and appropriacy, register shifting and clash repair, rhetorical devices and signposting. The C12 speaking and writing checklist gives you the extended-output evidence.

Watch for in Module 5: participle and absolute clauses (5.1) build on the nominal, compressed style of the written summary (Having weighed the evidence, the committee… · The budget approved, work began.). Advanced prepositions (5.2) connect directly to the inseparable and three-part verbs of 4.1 (in response to, with regard to, on behalf of), and academic vocabulary (5.3) extends the formal column of the register swap. Keep a two-minute "register tennis" or "praise it / criticize it" warmer in C14–C16: it is quick, and it keeps Module 4 lexis alive in speech. Use it or lose it.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 5 — Structure & Advanced Vocabulary

Three workshop classes on the quiet machinery of formal English. First, participle and absolute clauses fold a whole clause into the edge of a sentence (Having reviewed the figures, the board… · The meeting over, the staff went home), and learners hunt the dangling participle that betrays a careless draft. Second, dependent prepositions and fixed formal phrases (comply with, aware of, an increase in, in light of, by virtue of, with regard to) are tested in a formal-email correction challenge. Third, academic and formal vocabulary (Academic Word List families, get → obtain, show → demonstrate, stance verbs graded by strength) is put to work summarizing a short research abstract without over-claiming. The module starts right after Progress Test 1, so C14 opens by handing the tests back in a warm, low-stress way.

3 lessons Lessons 5.1–5.3 Classes C14–C16 Weeks 7–8 90 min per class CEFR C1

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill (choral, then individual) → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice (here, a writing or editing workshop in small teams) → feedback with delayed error correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-5-structure-vocabulary.html. Print materials/module-5/classroom-materials.html before C14. It holds the phrase and chunk flashcards, the Dangler Hunt strips and the three Condense the Report cards, the formal email for the Correction Challenge with its reply cards and score sheet, the two research abstracts with the summary peer-review card, "The Editor's Path" board game, and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences again at home.
  • Refinement, not a first meeting. B2 Lesson 3.2 taught basic participle clauses (Walking home, I saw…; Having finished, she left) and reduced relatives, and B2 learners have used interested in, depend on and a handful of formal phrases for years. Check what they know in each lead-in and move quickly where they are strong. The new C1 work is the voice-driven choice of participle (-ing / having done / past participle), the absolute clause as the fix for different subjects, formal and noun-based preposition chunks (an increase in / of, a solution to, by virtue of), and register judgment: knowing when the formal word is sharper and when it is merely fancy.
  • Workshops, not lectures. All three classes end with a team editing or writing task (a report to condense, an email to correct, an abstract to summarize). Keep the input stages brisk so the workshop gets its full time: that is where the learning becomes visible.
  • Feedback on range, accuracy and appropriacy. In every workshop, circulate and take notes; don't stop a team to fix aware about. At feedback, give three kinds of comment, without names: one accuracy fix ("comply to → comply with"), one range upgrade ("because the funds ran out → the funds having run out"), and one appropriacy note ("commence is right in the report, but in the email to a colleague, start is better"). Write them in three board columns: Fix · Upgrade · Fit.
  • Peer-review protocol (all three classes): Praise · Question · Polish. Reviewers give one specific praise ("Your absolute in line 2 is elegant"), ask one genuine question ("Who is doing the reviewing here?"), and suggest one polish, never more. The writer decides whether to accept it. Reviewers write their three points on the review card, not on the draft. This keeps critique precise and adult, and stops one strong editor rewriting another team's work.
  • Dominant speakers. Every workshop has built-in roles: scribe (holds the pen, writes only what the team agrees), checker (runs the "who is doing this?" test or the preposition-only proofread), and word-counter / timekeeper. Give the most dominant learner the checker role: they may only ask questions, not propose wording. In whole-class discussion, use talking chips (3 per learner). Say it kindly: "Thanks, Omar, that's a sharp point. Let's hear from a team that hasn't reported yet."
  • Stretching strong learners toward C2. Each class has a "Stretch" box with C2-leaning material (with-absolutes and literary absolutes, prepositions fronted in relative clauses such as the policy in accordance with which…, stacking and calibrating hedges, precise academic collocations such as conduct research, draw a conclusion). Offer it; don't require it.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British spelling and some British forms throughout this module: realise, practised, cancelled, analyse, emphasise, utilisation, programme, enrolment, enquiry, queue, CV, towards, "we've no buffer". Teach realize, practiced, canceled, analyze, emphasize, utilization, program, enrollment, inquiry, line, résumé, toward, "we have no buffer." Note that American usage prefers in light of; British English often says in the light of. Accept British forms when learners use them.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Compress → connect → choose. In C14 learners compress clauses so formal prose carries more meaning in fewer words, and check that every participle is anchored to the right subject. In C15 they connect words with the exact small preposition the pattern demands, and the formal phrase that fits the genre. In C16 they choose the precise academic word and the right strength of stance verb, and learn to hear over-formality as an error. Each class ends with a text the team improves together: an over-connected report condensed, a flawed formal email corrected, a research abstract summarized faithfully. These are the editing habits Module 6 (critical reading, essays, professional writing) will rely on.

Trauma-informed: tests, formal letters and research topics

The test return. Some adults, including highly qualified professionals whose credentials were not recognized here, carry painful associations with being graded. Keep the return brief, private and warm (see C14). Never read scores aloud or compare learners.

Formal correspondence. For many newcomers, formal letters mean immigration offices, benefits agencies, courts or landlords. Keep every formal text in this module in neutral territory: a college parking notice, a workplace training update, a clinic's appointment system, a library's Wi-Fi. Don't use immigration, asylum, benefits, court or eviction letters as examples, and don't ask learners to bring in real letters they have received.

Research topics. The C16 abstracts are about walking breaks at work and a reusable-cup scheme on a campus: low-stakes, everyday science. Learners discuss the study, not their own health. Journal prompts always allow an invented or reported example ("something a colleague concluded," "a news item"). As in every module, a new identity or an invented example is always a good answer.

Before the Module

  • Mark Progress Test 1 before C14. Write a short, positive comment on every paper: one strength + one thing to practice, phrased as an upgrade, not a deficit ("Your conditionals are accurate; next, try mixing time frames"). Collect 4–5 common errors from different papers for the C14 board (no names). Note which learners scored below about 60% so you can offer a quiet 1:1 chat at the break.
  • Print the flashcards (Set A): fixed participial phrases, preposition chunks and academic words, most of them word-only; 6 picture cards for the phrase drill. Card stock helps.
  • For C14: one set of Dangler Hunt strips per team of 3 and one Condense the Report card per team (Set B; three different reports, so neighboring teams differ), plus one peer-review card per team.
  • For C15: one copy of the Riverside College parking email per team, one score sheet, and one reply card (A or B) per team (Set C).
  • For C16: one research abstract per learner (Set D: Abstract 1 for most teams, Abstract 2 for fast or strong teams), an A/B "explain it to a friend" card per pair, and one summary peer-review card per team. One Editor's Path board (Set E) per group of 3–4, a die or coin, and counters.
  • Have a phone ready to "play" the voicemail in C15 (read the script while holding the phone to your ear).

Module at a Glance
