Lesson Plans

5.1 · C14

Participle & Absolute Clauses

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • reflect on their Progress Test 1 results and set one personal goal
  • compress adverbial and relative clauses into participle clauses, choosing by voice: -ing (active, same time), having + p.p. (active, earlier), past participle (passive): Written in 1920, the novel…
  • build absolute clauses with their own subject when the two subjects differ: The funds having run out, the project was suspended.
  • use common fixed participial phrases as sentence adverbials: having said that, all things considered, weather permitting, given that
  • identify and repair a dangling participle in two ways (change the main subject, or give the participle its own subject)
  • condense an over-connected report in a team, keeping every fact and a clear, formal rhythm
Materials
  • Marked Progress Test 1 papers (in folders or face-down) · sticky notes · Workbook "My goal after Progress Test 1" box
  • Workbook 5.1 (Key phrases, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Condense the Report frame)
  • Slides 3–9 · Fixed-phrase picture cards and word cards (Set A) · Dangler Hunt strips + Condense the Report cards + peer-review cards (Set B)
  • Listening scripts 5.1-A (the chair's announcement) and 5.1-B (dictation)

Left: a "compression funnel." Top: When she realized the risk, she paused. Arrow down, crossing out When and she: Realizing the risk, she paused. Second example: The novel was written in 1920, and it… → Written in 1920, the novel…

Center: three boxes labeled by voice, not tense: -ing = does it, same time · having + p.p. = did it, earlier · p.p. = had it done to it. Under them: ABSOLUTE = own subject + participle: The meeting over, … · The weather being fine, … · Her work done, …

Right: the one-question test in a box: "Who is doing this -ing / being this -ed?" → it must be the next subject. ❌ Walking home, the rain started. Two repairs: ① change the subject ② give the participle its own subject.

Far right: three empty feedback columns: Fix · Upgrade · Fit.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Welcome & Progress Test 1 return12T–S · S–S · SSlide 3. See "Returning Progress Test 1" below: strengths on the board, papers back privately, pairs fix 4 anonymous errors, each learner writes one goal in the workbook box.
Lead-in: the baggy notice5T–S · S–SSlide 4. Show the "baggy" version of a notice: "The committee reviewed last year's attendance, and it has secured a larger venue. The session will be held in the east wing, which is being used for the first time…" Ask: "Is anything wrong?" (No, it's correct.) "How does it feel?" (Wordy, like speech.) Then show the compressed version from the online lesson. Pairs spot three differences in 60 seconds. Elicit: fewer and / which / because; subjects disappear; participles do the work.
Participle clauses: M / F / P12T–SSlides 4–5. Recycle B2 quickly ("Who remembers Having finished, she left?"). Then the C1 system: present (simultaneous, reason, result: …, flattening dozens of homes), perfect (earlier), past participle (passive); reduced relatives (members wishing to…, items damaged in transit). Key rule: choose by voice, not by tense. CCQs (below).
Absolutes & fixed phrases10T–SSlides 6–7. Absolute = the fix for different subjects. Show the problem first ("Because the road was blocked, the drivers turned back: can we just drop because?"), then the solution: The road blocked, the drivers turned back. Omitted being (Dinner over, …). Then the fixed phrases with the picture cards (umbrella → weather permitting, flight → all flights having been canceled). Register note: fixed phrases work in speech; full absolutes are written.
Drill5T–S → SSee Drill script: the "compress it" oral drill, the voice switch, and the comma-pause rhythm.
Listening: the chair's announcement5T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 5.1-A twice in a formal "meeting chair" voice. Learners write the eight participle chunks they hear. Pairs compare. Ask: "Which ones have their own subject?" (the gymnasium being unavailable, the schedule being unusually full)
Controlled practice9S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2–4 alone (compress · choose by voice · absolutes), then check in pairs. Board-check only the items pairs disagree on. Ex 5 (sound or dangling?) opens the workshop, so hold it.
Workshop: Condense the Report22S–S (teams of 3) → S–S (teams swap)Slide 9. (1) Dangler Hunt (5 min): teams get 8 strips from "an intern's draft"; sort them into sound and dangling (five dangle), and fix two aloud. Workbook Ex 5 is the same skill. (2) Condense (12 min): each team gets a report card (~125 words, over-connected). Target: 90 words or fewer, every fact kept, at least four participle or absolute constructions (including one absolute) and one fixed phrase. The scribe writes; the checker runs the one-question test on every leading participle. (3) Peer review (5 min): teams swap drafts with a team that had a different report and use the review card: Praise · Question · Polish, plus "any danglers?" ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SDelayed correction in the Fix · Upgrade · Fit columns, e.g. Fix: "Having reviewed the budget, the proposal seemed too expensive." · Fix: "Building in 1968, the center needs a roof." · Upgrade: "Because the budget had not been spent, …" → "The budget not having been spent, …" · Fit: a full absolute in a text message to a friend ("My shift over, I'll call you") sounds oddly stiff. Read out the tightest condensed report (with permission).
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 5.1 on screen; point at a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box in the workbook.
Total9012 + 5 + 12 + 10 + 5 + 5 + 9 + 22 + 5 + 5
If you run long

The test return must stay at 12 minutes. Cut Workbook Ex 4 and the dictation (Script 5.1-B) and set them as homework. If the workshop is squeezed, shorten the Dangler Hunt to "find the five" without fixing, but never cut the peer review: checking another team's participles is how learners start checking their own.

Returning Progress Test 1 (12 min) — keep it warm

1. Celebrate first (3 min). Before any paper comes out, write "We can…" on the board and list three things the whole class did well (for example: place events precisely in the future with be due to / be on the verge of · speculate about the past with must have / can't have · add emphasis with inversion and clefts · hedge a claim diplomatically). Say it out loud.

Four modules of C1! You can speculate precisely, imagine mixed time frames, emphasize with inversion, and soften a claim so it sounds professional. That's advanced English. Give yourselves a hand.

2. Return privately (2 min). Hand papers back face-down or in folders. Never read scores aloud and never compare learners. Everyone gets a written comment: one strength + one upgrade to work on.

3. "Let's fix it!" (5 min). Show 4 anonymous errors from the tests. Use your own learners' errors if you can; typical ones at this point: Not only she finished the report, but she also presented it. · If I had accepted the offer, I would live in Boston by now. · It was in 2019 when she moved to Denver. · We need to make a research before the meeting. (Also common: She must have forgot the password. · Hardly had I sat down than the phone rang. · The manager insisted that every employee attends.) Pairs fix them. Take answers. Praise the fixing, not the error-free papers.

4. One goal (2 min). Each learner writes one goal in the workbook box ("My goal after Progress Test 1"): "I want to refine ___. Every week I will ___." Give two examples: "I want to refine my inversion. Every week I'll rewrite three sentences from a news article with Not only… / Rarely…." / "I want to hedge more naturally. I'll note five hedges from a podcast each week and use two at work."

A test is a map. It tells us where we are. It doesn't tell us who we are.

Some adults carry hard memories of school and tests, and professionals retraining in a new country can feel a test as a judgment on their whole career. Keep your voice light and move on quickly. Offer a 1:1 chat at the break to anyone who struggled (below about 60%), and plan catch-up with the Module 1–4 online lessons. Don't let the test return grow past 12 minutes. The best encouragement is a successful lesson straight after it, and today's grammar rewards what C1 learners already have: a good ear for written English.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: compression, not a new meaning. A participle clause says what a full clause says, minus the conjunction and the repeated subject. The reader supplies the link (time, reason, condition, result).

"Knowing the road well, he took a shortcut." Who knew the road? (He did.) Why did he take the shortcut? (Because he knew the road.) Is because in the sentence? (No, the reader supplies it.) · "The storm hit the coast, flattening dozens of homes." What flattened the homes? (The storm.) Before or after it hit? (As a result of hitting.)

Meaning: choose by voice, not by tense.

"Having signed the contract, they celebrated." Which came first? (Signing.) Who signed? (They did: active.) · "Signed in March, the contract runs for two years." Did the contract sign anything? (No, someone signed it: passive, past participle.) · "Building in 1920, the museum…" Can a museum build? (No! Built in 1920.)
"Members wishing to attend are asked to register." Is this a full clause? (No, a reduced relative.) Full version? (Members who wish to attend…) · "Items damaged in transit will be replaced." Active or passive? (Passive: items that are damaged.)

Meaning: absolute clauses have their own subject.

"The weather being fine, we set off early." Was we fine? (No, the weather was fine.) How many subjects? (Two: the weather, we.) Why can't we write "Being fine, we set off"? (It says we were fine: it dangles.) · "The meeting over, the staff went home." What's missing? (being: the meeting being over.) Formal or casual? (Formal, written.)

Meaning: the dangling participle.

"Walking into the room, the noise was overwhelming." Grammatically, who is walking? (The noise!) Do we understand it anyway? (Yes, but it's a careless error in formal writing.) Two ways to fix it? (Change the subject: …, I found the noise overwhelming. Or give it its own subject / a full clause: As I walked into the room, …)
"Having said that, the second option is cheaper." Who said it? (No one in particular: a fixed phrase.) Is it a dangling participle? (No, fixed phrases are exceptions.)

Form.

Present participle-ing · active, same time / reason / resultFeeling exhausted, she declined the invitation. · The storm hit, flattening homes.
Perfect participlehaving + p.p. · active, earlier action · passive: having been + p.p.Having finished the report, he left. · Having been warned twice, the driver slowed down.
Past participlep.p. · passive meaningWritten in 1920, the novel still speaks to readers. · Given more time, we could have finished.
Reduced relativenoun + -ing / p.p.the candidates applying for the post · any items damaged in transit
Negativenot before the participleNot knowing the way, we asked for directions.
Absoluteown subject + participle (being often dropped)The funds having run out, the project was suspended. · Dinner over, the guests moved to the lounge. · He stood at the door, his coat over one arm.
Fixed phrasessentence adverbials; exempt from subject-matchinghaving said that · generally / strictly speaking · all things considered · weather / time permitting · given that · provided that · judging by · barring any delays

Pronunciation. A leading participle clause is its own tone unit: a slight rise and a short pause at the comma, then the main clause falls: "Having reviewed the figures ↗ | the board approved the plan ↘." Absolutes need the pause most, or listeners attach the noun to the wrong verb: "The meeting over ↗ | the staff went home ↘." Having is often weak /ˈhævɪŋ/ → /əvɪŋ/ in fast speech but should stay clear when reading aloud. Past participles keep their endings: canceled /ˈkænsəld/, damaged /ˈdæmɪdʒd/, based /beɪst/. Fixed phrases are single chunks with one main stress: "all things considered," "weather permitting," "having said that."

Drill script
  1. Compress it (T says a full sentence; learners say the compressed version): T: "Because he knew the area well, he took a shortcut." → S: "Knowing the area well, he took a shortcut." T: "After she had locked the door, she checked it twice." → S: "Having locked the door, she checked it twice." T: "The report was written in a hurry, and it contained errors." → S: "Written in a hurry, the report contained errors." Choral, then fast individuals.
  2. Voice switch (T gives a verb + a subject; learners decide): T: "write · the letter" → S: "Written in haste, the letter…" T: "write · the manager" → S: "Having written the letter, the manager…" T: "delay · the train" → "Delayed by snow, the train…" T: "warn · the driver (twice, earlier)" → "Having been warned twice, the driver…"
  3. Absolute or dangle? T: "The road was blocked. The drivers turned back." → S: "The road blocked, the drivers turned back." T: "The meeting had ended. Everyone left." → "The meeting over, everyone left." T: "The weather was fine. We set off." → "The weather being fine, we set off."
  4. Rhythm: choral with a clear rise–pause–fall: "Having reviewed the figures ↗ | the board approved the plan ↘." · "Weather permitting ↗ | we'll eat outside ↘." Then individuals read one line from Script 5.1-A.
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 says a two-clause sentence about something from today's news or workplace (real or invented); S2 compresses it and says which kind it is (present, perfect, past, absolute). 1 minute, then swap.
Condense the Report — how to run it
  • Teams of 3 with roles: scribe (writes only what the team agrees), checker (runs the one-question test on every leading participle and counts constructions), word-counter / timekeeper. Rotate roles in C15 and C16.
  • Round 1 — Dangler Hunt (5 min). Eight strips from "an intern's draft" of a building report. Teams sort them into two piles, sound and dangling. Five dangle (1, 3, 5, 7, 8). The fixed phrase (Having said that) and the absolute (The funding approved) are sound: learners who "fix" them are over-correcting, which is worth a quick word at feedback. Each team repairs two danglers aloud, one each way.
  • Round 2 — Condense (12 min). Three report cards (the library Wi-Fi upgrade, the Route 12 night-bus pilot, the clinic's text-alert trial), each about 125 words and deliberately over-connected with and, because, when, after, which. Target: 90 words or fewer, every fact kept, at least four participle or absolute constructions (one must be an absolute) and one fixed phrase. Model condensed versions are in the answer key.
  • Round 3 — Peer review (5 min). Teams swap drafts with a team that had a different report. On the review card: Praise (one construction that works well) · Question (one "who is doing this?" query) · Polish (one suggestion) · Danglers found: 0 / 1 / 2. Drafts go back; writers decide whether to accept the polish.
  • Judgment, not maximization. Remind teams of the online lesson's advice: if a sentence feels airless after three participles, restore one conjunction. A clear 92-word draft beats a cryptic 70-word one.
  • Dominant speakers make good checkers: they may only ask questions ("Who is doing the examining?"), never dictate wording. Quieter learners often make excellent scribes; give them the pen and the authority to write only agreed wording.
ICQs before the workshop
Round 1: How many strips dangle? (Five.) Is Having said that a dangler? (No, it's a fixed phrase.) · Round 2: Maximum words? (Ninety.) Can you delete a fact? (No!) How many constructions? (At least four, one absolute, plus one fixed phrase.) Who holds the pen? (The scribe.) · Round 3: How many comments do you write? (Three: praise, question, polish.) Do you rewrite their report? (No, you comment on the card.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Some C1 learners speak far more fluently than they read and write. Give a strip with the three voice boxes and color-code them (-ing blue, having green, p.p. orange); learners underline the participle in each Script 5.1-A chunk in its color. In the Dangler Hunt, read each strip aloud for the team. In Round 2, the learner takes the first two sentences of the report card only and compresses them orally before the scribe writes. Target: 3 constructions, spoken and then copied; Workbook Ex 2 items 1–4 only.

Stretch (toward C2)

Add with-absolutes (With the deadline approaching, the team worked late), literary absolutes without a participle (He entered, hat in hand), and participles after conjunctions (Once completed, the form should be returned; If left untreated, …). Discuss the accepted exceptions: considering, given, assuming, judging by, speaking of are dangling in form but standard. In the workshop, challenge strong teams to 75 words with no loss of clarity, then to rewrite one sentence in a lighter register for a staff newsletter.

L1 watch-outs
  • Dangling participles: speakers of topic-prominent languages (Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Vietnamese) often let the opening phrase set a topic rather than share a subject ("Walking home, the rain started" mirrors "As for walking home, rain started"). Make the one-question test a routine.
  • Positive transfer, different word order: Spanish, Portuguese and Italian have absolutes (Terminada la reunión, salimos; Finita la riunione, …), so the idea is familiar, but learners may keep the L1 order: "Finished the meeting, we left." English puts the subject first: The meeting finished / over, …
  • -ing for passive meaning: "Building in 1920, the museum…" is common across L1s, especially where one participle form covers both voices (German speakers sometimes mix -end / ge-…-t equivalents; Arabic and Turkish speakers transfer active verbal nouns). Point at the voice boxes.
  • Overusing having + p.p.: Russian, Ukrainian and Polish have perfective adverbial participles (прочитав), and French has ayant fini, so learners may put having everywhere, even for simultaneous actions ("Having walked home, I met a friend"). Ask: "Did the walking finish first?"
  • French en + gérondif: "By walking home, I met a friend" (from en rentrant) confuses manner with time. By -ing = method; a bare participle = time or reason.
Homework

Online lesson 5.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_13.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and read them back with the rise–pause–fall; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz; complete the "Compress and Polish" project with any choppy paragraph (a news item, a process, or a scene they invent). Workbook 5.1: finish the Condense the Report reflection (which construction did your team use best, and which sentence did you leave uncompressed, and why?), "My new words" and the journal (the online lesson's "write a dangler on purpose, then fix it" task).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B Rushing to the station, the train had already left. (dangles) · Q2 A The road blocked, the drivers turned back. · Q3 (new) C Damaged in transit, the parcel was returned to the sender. The chair's announcement (Ex 1): 1 Having reviewed · 2 the gymnasium being unavailable · 3 wishing to run · 4 printed for the fair · 5 The schedule being unusually full · 6 Weather permitting · 7 All things considered · 8 Having said that.

5.2 · C15

Advanced Prepositions & Prepositional Phrases

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • use the fixed preposition after key verbs (comply with, refrain from, account for, resort to, result in / from, object to, consist of) and adjectives (aware of, subject to, prone to, consistent with, reliant on, conducive to)
  • use the preposition an abstract noun requires (an increase in, a solution to, an alternative to, insight into, an impact on), and notice when it differs from the verb's
  • distinguish in (the area that changed) from of (the size of the change): an increase in unemployment of 3%
  • avoid intrusive prepositions after transitive verbs (discuss, enter, contact, emphasize)
  • use fixed formal phrases (with regard to, in light of, by virtue of, in the event of, on behalf of, in accordance with, in the wake of) in formal writing, and plain equivalents in conversation
  • correct a formal email and write a short formal reply with accurate prepositions
Materials
  • Workbook 5.2 (Key chunks, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Correction Challenge frame)
  • Slides 10–14 · Preposition chunk cards and fixed-phrase cards (Set A) · Riverside College parking email + score sheet + reply cards A / B (Set C)
  • Listening scripts 5.2-A (voicemail from Human Resources) and 5.2-B (dictation)

Left: three "families" in columns. of: aware · capable · devoid · indicative · a lack · with: comply · consistent · compatible · familiar · to: subject · prone · conducive · an alternative · a solution · object · resort. Beneath: on: depend · reliant · an impact · from: refrain · benefit · result (be caused by) · in: result (cause) · an increase · insight into.

Center: "verb → noun: the preposition can switch." increase sth → an increase in sth · solve sth → a solution to sth · demand sth → a demand for sth. Then: an increase in crime (area) of 5% (figure). And a big ❌ over "discuss about · enter into the room · contact with him."

Right: a register ladder: with regard to → about · in the event of → if · in the wake of → after · by virtue of → because of · in light of → considering. Label the top "report / formal email" and the bottom "conversation / text."

Far right: Fix · Upgrade · Fit columns for feedback.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: compress it8S–S → T–SReview C14. Four baggy sentences on the board (one needs an absolute, one a past participle, one having, one a fixed phrase). Pairs compress them in 3 minutes, then run the one-question test on a partner pair's answers. Two teams read their best condensed report sentence from C14.
Lead-in: which sounds wrong?5T–SSlide 10. Six quick pairs; learners vote by holding up one or two fingers: comply to / comply with · aware about / aware of · discuss about the plan / discuss the plan · an increase of crime / an increase in crime · depend of / depend on · married with / married to. Ask: "Could you have worked these out by logic?" (No: they're chunks.) "Do these errors stop communication?" (Rarely, which is why they survive for years.)
Verb & adjective + preposition12T–S → S–SSlide 10. Present in families (board left), not as a list. Meaning checks for the less obvious ones (account for, resort to, subject to, conducive to, devoid of). Same verb, different preposition, different meaning: result in / from; agree with / to / on. Pairs test each other with the chunk cards: one shows the word, the other says the chunk in a sentence.
Nouns, in vs of, no preposition8T–SSlide 11. The verb → noun switch (board center). in for the area vs of for the figure. Transitive verbs with no preposition. CCQs (below).
Fixed formal phrases8T–SSlide 12. The phrases, their meanings and the register ladder. Focus on the trio in the brief: in light of (considering new information), with regard to (about), by virtue of (because of a quality or status). Then the comic mismatch: "In the event of you being hungry, we could eat." Model dialogue on slide 13, in pairs, once each way.

Workshop: Formal Email Correction Challenge21S–S (teams of 3) → S–S (teams swap)Slide 14. (1) Correct (8 min): each team gets the Riverside College parking email. It contains 12 preposition errors and 5 correct chunks that look suspicious. Teams mark corrections on the score sheet: +1 for each correct fix, −1 for "fixing" something that was already correct. Reveal and score together (2 min, included). (2) Reply (8 min): each team takes reply card A (the student representative, writing on behalf of evening students) or B (the Facilities Office, replying to a complaint) and writes an 80–100-word reply with at least four dependent prepositions and two fixed phrases. (3) Preposition-only proofread (3 min): teams swap replies and read only for prepositions, circling any chunk they doubt; Praise · Question · Polish on the card. ICQs first.

Announce the top-scoring team and read the best reply aloud (with permission).

Total908 + 5 + 12 + 8 + 8 + 5 + 5 + 8 + 21 + 5 + 5
If you run long

Shorten the verb and adjective stage by giving the families as a handout and checking only the tricky meanings. Move Workbook Ex 3 to homework. Never cut the Correction Challenge scoring: the −1 for over-correcting is what teaches learners to trust a correct chunk as much as they doubt a wrong one. If time is very short, teams write the reply as homework and proofread in the C16 warmer.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: "dependent" = chosen by the word in front, not by logic.

"All contractors must comply with the regulations." What does comply mean? (Obey, follow.) Could we say "comply to"? (No. But conform to takes to: you can't reason it out.) · "Prices are subject to change." Will prices definitely change? (No, they may: it's a condition.)
"How do you account for the delay?" Am I asking for a number? (No, an explanation.) · "Young people account for 40% of users." Here? (A proportion: they make up 40%.) · "They resorted to legal action." Was it their first choice? (No, a last option.)
"Poor planning resulted in the loss." Cause or effect first? (Cause: planning → loss.) · "The loss resulted from poor planning." (Effect first.) Same facts? (Yes, opposite direction.) · "I agree with you." (a person / opinion) · "I agree to the proposal." (accept it) · "We agreed on a date." (reached a decision together)

Meaning: nouns, and in vs of.

"We need to increase funding." Preposition after increase? (None.) "There's been an increase ___ funding." (in.) "an increase in funding of 12%." What does of tell us? (The size.) · "We solved the problem" → "We found a solution ___ the problem." (to.) Is it of? (No.)
"Let's discuss about the budget." Correct? (No: discuss takes a direct object.) Other verbs like this? (enter the room, contact him, emphasize the point, approach the building, reach an agreement.) But the noun? (a discussion about / of the budget: the noun can take a preposition.)

Meaning: fixed formal phrases.

"In light of the new figures, we'll revise the plan." Did something new happen? (Yes, new information.) Plain version? (Considering / Because of the new figures.) · "She chairs the committee by virtue of her seniority." Why does she chair it? (Because she is the most senior.) Is by virtue of about being virtuous? (No! It means "because of a quality or position.") · "With regard to your inquiry…" Where would you see this? (A formal email or letter.) To a friend? (About your question…)

Form.

Verb + prepdepend on · comply with · account for · refrain from · resort to · result in / from · consist of · contribute to · object to · benefit fromPlease refrain from using your phone.
Adjective + prepaware / capable / devoid / indicative of · consistent / compatible with · subject / prone / conducive to · reliant onThe region is reliant on tourism.
Noun + prepan increase / rise / decline in · a reason / demand / need for · an alternative / a solution to · insight into · an impact / effect on · a lack of · a relationship betweena cheaper alternative to flying
Prep + -inga preposition is followed by -ing, not tocapable of managing · object to paying · refrain from smoking
No prepositiontransitive verbs: discuss, enter, contact, emphasize, approach, reach, marryWe discussed the plan. · She married a doctor. (but: married to)
Fixed formal phraseswith regard to · in light of · by virtue of · in the event of · on behalf of · in accordance with · in the wake of · for the sake of · in terms of · at the expense of · with a view to + -ingIn the event of fire, use the stairs.

Pronunciation. Dependent prepositions are almost always weak and linked to the word before: aware of /əˈwɛrəv/, comply with /kəmˈplaɪwɪð/, consist of /kənˈsɪstəv/, depend on /dɪˈpɛndɑn/. Because they are unstressed, learners mishear them. Drill them as one word. In fixed phrases the stress falls on the content noun: "with regard to," "in light of," "by virtue of," "on behalf of," "in the event of," "in accordance with." Exception: prepositions become strong at the end of a clause or in contrast: "It's not what you comply to, it's what you comply with."

Drill script
  1. Chunk drill (hold up a chunk card showing the word only): comply → S: "comply with" · aware → "aware of" · subject → "subject to" · an increase → "an increase in" · refrain → "refrain from" · insight → "insight into." Choral, then fast individuals. Then learners add a short object: "comply with the rules."
  2. Family drill: T: "of!" → learners call out as many of-adjectives as they can in 10 seconds (aware, capable, devoid, indicative, conscious). Then with, to, on.
  3. Verb → noun: T: "increase prices" → S: "an increase in prices" · T: "solve the problem" → "a solution to the problem" · T: "demand housing" → "a demand for housing" · T: "affect health" → "an effect on health."
  4. Register flip: T (formal): "In the event of rain…" → S (plain): "If it rains…" · T (plain): "After the decision, he resigned." → S (formal): "In the wake of the decision, he resigned." · T: "about your inquiry" → "with regard to your inquiry."
  5. Weak forms: choral /əˈwɛrəv/ "aware of it" ×3, "comply with the law" ×3, "consists of three parts" ×3, fast, linked.
Formal Email Correction Challenge — how to run it
  • Teams of 3, new roles: scribe, checker, timekeeper. Each team gets one copy of the Riverside College email about resurfacing the north parking lot (about 190 words, from the Facilities Coordinator to evening students) and one score sheet.
  • Round 1 — Correct (8 min). The email contains 12 preposition errors: wrong preposition, missing preposition, intrusive preposition, to instead of from + -ing, and a mangled fixed phrase. It also contains 5 decoys: correct chunks that often look wrong to learners (result in, reliant on, of about 120 spaces, In the event of further delays, agreed to the shuttle proposal). Teams underline each change and write the correction on the score sheet. +1 for each correct fix; −1 for changing a correct chunk. Teams may "bank" a doubtful item by writing "?" (0 points) instead of guessing.
  • Reveal (included). Go through the email line by line on the slide or board. Let teams argue their case: "We think of about 120 spaces is wrong" → "Why is of right here?" (It's the size of the change.) Highest score wins; ties go to the team with fewer −1s.
  • Round 2 — Reply (8 min). Reply card A: you are the elected student representative, writing on behalf of evening students; ask for more frequent shuttles and better lighting at Oak Street. Reply card B: you are the Facilities Office, replying to a student who complained that they were not warned in time. Both: 80–100 words, at least four dependent prepositions and two fixed formal phrases, formal but human.
  • Round 3 — Preposition-only proofread (3 min). Teams swap replies and read only for prepositions: circle every preposition attached to a pattern word and ask, "Is this the chunk?" Then Praise · Question · Polish on the review card.
  • Dominant speakers are good checkers in Round 1 (they must justify every change by naming the chunk), but in Round 2 they may not write: the scribe writes. Quieter learners can take Reply card A, which has a phrase bank.
ICQs before the challenge
How many errors are there? (Twelve.) Are there correct chunks that look wrong? (Yes, five.) What happens if you change a correct one? (Minus one point.) Not sure? (Write a question mark: zero points.) · Reply: how many words? (80 to 100.) How many fixed phrases? (At least two.) · Proofread: what do you read for? (Only prepositions.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give the email with each line numbered and a hint in the margin for lines with an error ("1 error here"). Pre-teach the three families with a one-page chunk card the learner can keep (of / with / to / on / from / in). In Round 2, provide a reply frame with gaps: "On ___ of the evening students, I am writing with ___ to…". Target: find 6 of the 12 errors; in writing, Workbook Ex 2 and Ex 3 only.

Stretch (toward C2)

Introduce preposition fronting in formal relative clauses (the policy in accordance with which the decision was made · the committee on whose behalf I write) and fine distinctions: in light of (new information) vs in view of (a fact to consider) vs given; regarding / as regards / with regard to (all correct) vs the common "with regards to" (avoid in formal writing); consist of vs consist in. Early finishers edit the reply to cut one fixed phrase that is only decoration.

L1 watch-outs
  • Direct translation of the L1 preposition: Spanish and Portuguese depender de → "depend of"; casado con / casado com → "married with"; consistir en → "consist in" (for "consist of"). French se conformer à → "comply to"; discuter de → "discuss about". German abhängig von → "dependent from"; interessiert an → "interested on". Ask learners to write their own three "L1 traps" at the top of the chunk list.
  • "an increase of": Russian (рост цен), Polish, Arabic (ziyādat al-…) and Chinese learners use a genitive structure, so "an increase of prices / of crime" feels natural. Use the "area vs figure" test every time.
  • Intrusive or missing prepositions: Chinese, Japanese and Korean speakers (postpositions or none) may drop the preposition ("aware the risk," "comply the rules") or add about after discuss, mention, explain. Arabic speakers often add prepositions to transitive verbs ("enter to the room").
  • to + infinitive after a preposition: "capable to do," "refrain to park," "object to pay." Most L1s use an infinitive here. Rule: after a preposition, -ing. The to in object to / look forward to / be committed to is a preposition, not an infinitive marker.
  • "in the light of" / "with regards to": learners from British-English schooling write in the light of (correct British; in light of is the American norm). With regards to is heard everywhere but marked in formal writing; teach with regard to or regarding.
Homework

Online lesson 5.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_14.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them back, linking the weak prepositions; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. For the online "Preposition-Perfect Message" project, learners may reuse their team's reply from class and extend it to 120–160 words. Workbook 5.2: Exercise 5 (correct the clinic email) if not finished, "My new words" (start the running preposition chunk list: ten chunks, five known and five new) and the journal.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C All staff must comply with the dress code. · Q2 C in · Q3 (new) A In light of the new figures, the board will revise its forecast. HR voicemail (Ex 1): 1 on behalf of · 2 With regard to · 3 In light of · 4 comply with · 5 refrain from · 6 In the event of · 7 consists of · 8 increase in.

5.3 · C16

Academic & Formal Vocabulary

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • replace everyday words with academic equivalents that fit the exact sense (get → obtain, show → demonstrate, big → substantial / significant, about → approximately / regarding)
  • move an idea across an Academic Word List family (analyze / analysis / analytical; significant / significance / significantly; evident / evidence / evidently)
  • report a source at the right strength with stance verbs (suggest, indicate, argue, demonstrate, acknowledge) and keep the source's hedges
  • prefer a precise single verb over a phrasal verb in writing (find out → determine, go up → increase) without purging every phrasal verb
  • recognize over-formalization (utilize, commence, endeavor in the wrong place) as an error of register
  • summarize a research abstract in academic register, and explain the same study plainly to a friend
Materials
  • Workbook 5.3 (Key words, Vocabulary box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Abstract to Summary frame, "look back at my goal")
  • Slides 15–18 · Academic word cards (Set A) · Research abstracts 1 and 2 + A / B "explain it to a friend" cards + summary peer-review cards (Set D) · The Editor's Path board (Set E) + dice / coins + counters
  • Listening scripts 5.3-A (a casual radio item about the study) and 5.3-B (dictation)

Left: two columns, everyday → academic: get → obtain · show → demonstrate · big → substantial (size) / significant (importance) · look at → examine · enough → sufficient · about → approximately (number) / regarding (topic) · get worse → deteriorate · find out → determine · go up → increase.

Center: a stance thermometer, bottom to top: note / observe (neutral) · suggest / indicate / imply (tentative) · argue / claim / contend (a position) · demonstrate / show (treated as proven). Beside it: acknowledge / concede (admits a limitation). A dashed line labeled "don't claim more than the evidence."

Right: the use / utilize test: "Does the long word add meaning or only length?" sharper ✔ vs just formal ✘. Beneath: ❌ I shall endeavor to commence… (to a friend).

Far right: Fix · Upgrade · Fit columns for feedback.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: The Editor's Path8S–S (groups of 3–4)Groups play The Editor's Path (Set E) for 7 minutes: squares ask them to compress a clause, fix a dangler, supply a preposition or flip register. Answers are on the back of the board. One minute: each group reports the square that caused the longest argument.
Lead-in: same meaning, different world5T–SSlide 15. Show "We got a lot of data, then we looked at it and found something big." Ask: "Grammatically wrong?" (No.) "Where would it be wrong?" (In an essay or report.) Pairs upgrade it in 60 seconds. Compare with "We obtained substantial data, examined it, and identified a significant finding." Ask: "Did the grammar change?" (Hardly: the words did.)
Everyday → academic10T–S → S–SSlide 15. The table on the board (left), with the sense-fit warning: substantial vs significant, approximately vs regarding; significant also has a statistical meaning. Then phrasal vs single verb (look into → investigate, set up → establish, cut down on → reduce), with the caveat that find out often beats ascertain. CCQs (below).
AWL word families7T–S → S–SSlide 15. What the Academic Word List is (a research-based list of word families common across all academic subjects). Show one family moving through a sentence: The result is significant. / the significance of the result / Results improved significantly. Pairs complete Workbook Ex 3 (the family table) together as the practice.
Stance verbs & hedging8T–SSlide 16. The stance thermometer. Misrepresenting a source is an accuracy error, not a style choice: "The data prove…" when the authors wrote "suggest." Model dialogue on slide 16 (casual) → the report version, and the over-formal text to a friend. CCQs.
Drill4T–S → SRegister ladder up and down, and the family drill (see Drill script).
Listening: the radio item4T–S → SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 5.3-A twice in a relaxed radio voice. Learners note six casual expressions and give an academic equivalent for each. This is the study they will summarize, heard first in casual English.
Controlled practice8S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2, 4 and 5 alone (replace the informal word · choose the stance verb · sharper or just formal?), then check in pairs. Board-check Ex 4 (stance strength).
Workshop: Abstract to Summary26S → S–S → S–S (teams of 3)Slide 17. (1) Read & annotate (5 min, alone): Abstract 1 (walking breaks and afternoon concentration; Abstract 2, a reusable-cup scheme, for fast teams). Underline AWL words; box every hedge and stance verb; circle the one result that was not significant. (2) Explain it to a friend (4 min, pairs): A explains the study in plain, friendly English (card A: no academic words allowed); B asks two questions from card B. Swap halfway. (3) Write (10 min, teams of 3): a 70–90-word summary for the "Research Roundup" in a staff newsletter: at least six academic items, two stance verbs at the right strength, one word-family shift, no claim stronger than the abstract's. (4) Peer review (5 min): swap with another team; review card: Praise · Question · Polish + "Does the summary claim more than the abstract?" + label two word choices sharper or just formal. (5) Share (2 min): two teams read their first sentence aloud.
Feedback & module wrap-up5T–SFix · Upgrade · Fit: Fix: "The study proves that walking improves accuracy" (it wasn't significant) · "a significant big group" · Upgrade: "The results show…" → "The results indicate…" · Fit: "utilize walking breaks" → "use / take walking breaks." Then the recap on slide 18 (see Closing the Module).
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 5.3; workbook "look back at my goal"; the journal.
Total908 + 5 + 10 + 7 + 8 + 4 + 4 + 8 + 26 + 5 + 5
If you run long

Shorten the warmer to 5 minutes (stop the game wherever it is). Set Workbook Ex 5 as homework. In the workshop, the "explain it to a friend" step can drop to 2 minutes, but keep it: saying the study plainly first is what stops learners copying the abstract's sentences word for word. Never cut the "does it claim more than the abstract?" check: faithful reporting is the core of Module 6.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: the formal word must fit the exact sense.

"a substantial increase" vs "a significant discovery." Which one is about size or amount? (substantial.) Which is about importance? (significant.) In a research report, "a significant difference" can mean what else? (Statistically significant: unlikely to be chance.) · "approximately 40%" vs "concerns regarding privacy." Can we say "concerns approximately privacy"? (No: approximately is only for numbers.)
"The researchers obtained the samples." Did they buy them, take them, or receive them? (We don't know: obtain is neutral and formal.) · "Conditions deteriorated." Better or worse? (Worse.) Plain English? (got worse.)

Meaning: stance verbs have strength.

"The data suggest a link." Is the link proven? (No, possible.) · "The trial demonstrates that the drug works." Stronger or weaker? (Much stronger: treated as proven.) · "Critics maintain the model is flawed." Does everyone agree? (No; they hold the position firmly, maybe stubbornly.) · "She concedes that the sample was small." Is she admitting a weakness? (Yes.)
The abstract says "may constitute a low-cost means." A student writes "proves that walking breaks are effective." Is that a style problem or an accuracy problem? (Accuracy: it misrepresents the source.)

Meaning: don't over-formalize.

A text to a friend: "I shall endeavor to commence my journey imminently." Is it grammatically wrong? (No.) What's wrong? (The register: absurdly stiff.) Better? (I'll leave soon.) · Does utilize add meaning to use? (Almost never.) Does demonstrate add meaning to show in a report? (Sometimes: it implies proof.)

Form.

Everyday → academicget → obtain / acquire · show → demonstrate / illustrate · big → substantial / significant · look at → examine · enough → sufficient · start → commence / initiate · a lot of → considerable / numerous · help → facilitate · get worse → deteriorateThere was insufficient evidence to draw a conclusion.
Phrasal → singlefind out → determine · look into → investigate · go up / down → increase / decrease · put off → postpone · set up → establish · cut down on → reduce · get rid of → eliminate · make up → constitute / compriseThe charity was established in 1990.
AWL familiesanalyze · analysis · analyst · analytical(ly) — significant · significance · significantly · signify — evident · evidence · evidently — establish · established · establishment — derive · derivation · derivative — constitute · constituent · consequent · consequence · consequentlyA detailed analysis revealed three patterns.
Stance verbs + thatnote / observe · suggest / indicate / imply · argue / claim / contend / maintain · highlight / emphasize · demonstrate / show · acknowledge / concedeThe authors acknowledge that the sample was small.
Grammar notesdata: plural in formal writing (the data suggest), singular widely accepted · suggest that + clause, not "suggest to" · emphasize with no preposition (the noun: emphasis on)The data indicate a modest effect.

Pronunciation. Academic words shift stress across the family, and learners who know the word in writing often stress it wrongly: analyze → analysis → analytical; establish; significant; evident → evidence; constitute → constituent; deteriorate (5 syllables, not "detoriate"); approximately. The noun and verb increase differ: an increase / to increase. Model with backchaining.

Drill script
  1. Register ladder up: T: "get funding" → S: "obtain funding" · "look at the causes" → "examine the causes" · "a big rise" → "a substantial rise" · "about 200 people" → "approximately 200 people" · "find out the cause" → "determine the cause." Choral, then individuals.
  2. Register ladder down (to a friend): T: "I shall commence" → S: "I'll start" · "utilize" → "use" · "endeavor to" → "try to" · "prior to" → "before" · "subsequently" → "then / later."
  3. Family drill (stress!): T: "analyze · noun" → S: "analysis" · "· adjective" → "analytical" · T: "significant · noun" → "significance" · "· adverb" → "significantly" · T: "evident · noun" → "evidence."
  4. Thermometer: T says a claim and points to a level; learners report it: T: "walking helps concentration · tentative" → S: "The study suggests that walking helps concentration." · "· proven" → "The study demonstrates that…" · "· admits a limit" → "The authors acknowledge that…"
Abstract to Summary — how to run it
  • Abstract 1 (about 145 words): "Short Walking Breaks and Afternoon Concentration in Office Workers: A Pilot Study." 62 employees at two municipal offices, four weeks, three five-minute walking breaks each afternoon; self-reported concentration about 18% higher than the control group; error rate on a data-entry task declined modestly but not significantly; the authors hedge ("may constitute") and acknowledge limitations. Abstract 2 (for fast teams): a reusable-cup deposit scheme in three university cafés, with a large fall in disposable cups but a small, uncertain effect on overall waste.
  • Step 1, alone: learners annotate. The key trap is the non-significant accuracy result: a faithful summary must not say accuracy "improved" without qualification.
  • Step 2, pairs: card A: "Explain the study to a friend over coffee. No word from the Academic Word List allowed!" Card B: "You're the friend. Ask two questions, e.g. 'So does it actually work?' 'How many people were in it?'" Swap. This makes the register contrast physical before anyone writes.
  • Step 3, teams: 70–90 words for the staff newsletter's "Research Roundup." Requirements on the frame: six academic items (words or phrases), two stance verbs (one must be suggest / indicate or acknowledge), one word-family shift (e.g. significant → significance), and no claim stronger than the abstract's. Sentence starters are on the frame for teams that want them.
  • Step 4, peer review: the review card asks: Praise · Question · Polish; Faithful? (Does it claim more or less than the abstract? Underline any over-claim.); Sharper or just formal? (label two word choices). The model summary in the answer key is for you, not a target to copy.
  • Dominant speakers take the fidelity checker role: they compare the draft with the abstract sentence by sentence and may only point, not rewrite. In Step 2, pair a dominant speaker with a confident partner and ask B to hold them to the "no academic words" rule.
ICQs before the workshop
Step 1: What do you circle? (The result that was not significant.) · Step 2: Can A use words like substantial or demonstrate? (No, plain English only.) · Step 3: How many words? (70 to 90.) How many stance verbs? (Two.) Can you say the study proves something? (Only if the abstract does, and it doesn't.) · Step 4: What's the extra question on the review card? (Does the summary claim more than the abstract?)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Read Abstract 1 aloud to the learner (or play it from your phone's text-to-speech) while they follow; give a glossed copy with six AWL words defined in the margin. In Step 3, the learner uses the summary frame with sentence starters ("A recent pilot study examined… · Participants who… reported approximately… · However, … did not reach… · The authors acknowledge that…"). Target: 4 academic items and 1 stance verb; Workbook Ex 2 items 1–5 only.

Stretch (toward C2)

Calibrate hedges precisely: may, appears to, tends to, is likely to, there is some evidence that, and spot over-hedging ("It may possibly perhaps suggest…"). Use academic collocations, not just words: conduct a study, draw a conclusion, a robust finding, a modest effect, warrant further research. Write a second, 25-word version as a tweet-length "plain-language summary," and discuss what is lost. Early finishers compare Abstracts 1 and 2 in one sentence with whereas.

L1 watch-outs
  • Latinate false friends: Spanish, Portuguese, French and Italian speakers have a head start with academic vocabulary but meet false friends: actual (≠ current: actual = real), eventual(ly) (≠ possible: = in the end), assist (≠ attend: French assister à, Spanish asistir a), realize (≠ carry out: Spanish realizar un estudio → conduct a study), sensible (≠ sensitive). Flag these in the vocabulary box.
  • Over-formalizing everything: learners from school systems that reward elaborate written style (many Arabic, Persian, Spanish-speaking and South Asian contexts) may see utilize, commence as "better English." Use the use / utilize test and praise plain precision.
  • Over-claiming: in some academic traditions strong assertion signals confidence, and learners from Chinese, Russian or Arabic academic writing may write "proves" or "it is clear that." Frame hedging as honesty about evidence, not weakness.
  • Stress on AWL words: speakers of syllable-timed languages (Spanish, French, Italian, Turkish) and many Asian languages give equal weight to each syllable ("a-na-ly-sis"); French speakers often stress the final syllable ("analysis"). Drill the stress shift in each family.
  • "The data is / are": learners may have been taught either. Accept both; in formal science writing prefer the data suggest.
Homework

Online lesson 5.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_15.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them back, watching the stress in analysis, significant, approximately; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. For the online "From Chat to Report" project, learners can use the class study, a news item, or something a colleague concluded (no personal health topics needed). Workbook 5.3: "My new words" (fifteen everyday → academic pairs, each marked sharper or just formal), the journal, and "Module 5: look back at my goal."

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A The results demonstrate that the plan was largely ineffective. · Q2 B I'll head off in about ten minutes. · Q3 (new) B The findings suggest that brief walking breaks may improve concentration. Radio item (Ex 1), any six: looked at → examined / studied · about → approximately · found out → found / determined · a lot more → considerably / substantially more · went down a bit → decreased / declined slightly · not enough to really prove anything → not statistically significant · kind of shows → suggests / indicates · do a bigger study → conduct a larger study.

Closing the Module

End of C16: Module 5 wrap-up

Recap on slide 18, "compress, connect, choose": Having reviewed the figures, the board… · The meeting over, … (compress, and keep the subject anchored) → comply with · an increase in · in light of · by virtue of (connect with the exact preposition) → obtain · demonstrate · the data suggest (choose the precise word and the honest strength, and don't over-formalize). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Review it in the C17 warmer.

Check the C14 goals. At the end of C16, invite learners to read their Progress Test goal again (the "Look back at my goal" box in Workbook 5.3): "Did you practice it? What will you do next?" This is private: no one reports to the class.

Watch for in Module 6: critical reading (6.1) turns today's stance verbs outward: learners will notice when a writer's demonstrates is stronger than the evidence. Essay writing (6.2) and professional writing (6.3) need all three skills at once: a condensed, anchored sentence, the right preposition in with regard to your application, and a register that suits the reader. Keep the Fix · Upgrade · Fit columns: they become the marking categories for Module 6 writing. Learners who have just discovered formal vocabulary may over-formalize their C17–C19 writing; praise precision, and keep asking "sharper, or just formal?"

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 6 — Reading & Writing at C1

Three skills workshops rather than three grammar lessons. First, a critical-reading lab: two short, invented opinion columns on the same workplace decision, taken apart for claims, evidence, bias and fallacies (with a fallacy card sort and a structured seminar). Then an essay-structuring workshop: thesis, paragraph architecture, counter-argument and rebuttal, conclusion, moving from outline to draft to structured peer review. Last, a professional-writing lab: the email, the report with headings and the cover letter, all tied to one invented workplace and written for real or fictional job-seeker profiles.

3 lessons Lessons 6.1–6.3 Classes C17–C19 Weeks 9–10 90 min per class CEFR C1

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. The shape is the kit's usual one (warmer → lead-in → clarify with CCQs → drill → controlled practice → freer task → feedback → homework), but in this module the "language" being clarified is mostly moves (claim, warrant, concession, rebuttal, purpose-first) and genre conventions, and the freer task is a real reading or writing product. Treat each class as a workshop: you model, learners try, peers test, learners revise.
  • The stage plan is your clock and adds up to 90 minutes. Writing stages are the ones that overrun. Protect them by cutting the controlled practice first (every exercise that is cut is listed as homework).
  • Present from slides/module-6-reading-writing.html. Print materials/module-6/classroom-materials.html before C17. It holds the flashcards, the professional profile cards, the seminar role cards and the pilot fact sheet, the fallacy card sort, the essay question cards with assigned sides, the peer-review sheet, the writing-lab pack (report data card, job postings, reader cards), Fallacy Bingo and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons for the five model sentences; the lesson projects (a critical evaluation, an essay plan, one real professional text) extend the class products.
  • Refinement, not first contact. At B2 learners met opinion essays, formal emails and linking words (B2 Module 4 and 7). Don't re-teach paragraphs or "Dear Sir/Madam." Spend the time on what is new at C1: the warrant, the difference between a strong case and a well-made argument, the fair concede → rebut, the purpose-first reader-focus of professional writing, and register control across genres. Recycle the course's own grammar deliberately: nominalization and academic style (3.1), cohesion (3.2), hedging (3.3), the subjunctive after recommend (1.3), academic vocabulary (5.3).
  • Feedback on range, accuracy and appropriacy. In every freer task, keep three columns: accuracy (errors), range (ambitious, precise language to praise and spread) and appropriacy (right words, wrong register or wrong move: an email that buries its request; a rebuttal that dismisses; a "verdict" that judges the topic, not the argument). At C1 the third column matters most. Board 2–3 items from each, without names.
  • Peer review is taught, not assumed. Adults from many education systems have never been asked to critique a classmate, and some feel it is rude. Model it (the 6.2 dialogue and slide 11), give a fixed protocol (reviewer speaks first, writer listens, writer asks one question, writer decides), and give the reviewer a job (a reverse outline and a checklist) rather than an invitation to "give feedback." Review the reviewers: check the peer-review sheets, not only the drafts.
  • Dominant speakers. The seminar in C17 has fixed roles: give the most talkative learner the Reporter (who must summarize the group, not their own view) or the Evidence Checker (who may speak only when figures are cited). Use a round: everyone speaks once before anyone speaks twice. In peer review, the timer protects the quieter writer: 3 minutes reviewer, 1 minute writer's question, no interruptions. Say it plainly: "Thanks, Omar. I'd like to hear the group's view now, not yours. Reporter?"
  • Stretching strong learners toward C2. Each lesson has a stretch box. The C2 direction for this module is judgment and style: when a "fallacy" is actually reasonable (a slippery slope with evidence, a relevant authority); how to rebut the rebuttal; synthesizing two sources rather than evaluating one; controlling rhythm and emphasis in prose (periodic sentences, fronting, the well-placed short sentence); switching register fluently between genres for the same content.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British spellings and conventions (recognise, sceptical, colouring, generalisation, organise, practise, enquire, favour, judgement, behaviour, neighbourhood, centralised; Ms Okafor without a period, Yours sincerely, CV) and a few British words (high street, footfall, pupils). Teach the American forms and business conventions: Ms. with a period, Sincerely, / Best regards,, résumé, inquire, downtown / main street, foot traffic, students, and dates written as March 3. Accept British forms when learners use them consistently.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs. Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Take it apart → build it → put it to work. One invented company, Harborview Logistics, runs through the module. In C17 learners read two columnists fighting over Harborview's four-day-week pilot and discover that both of them cherry-pick the same company's figures. In C18 they build the kind of argument those columnists failed to write: a thesis, disciplined paragraphs, a fair counter-argument. In C19 they step inside Harborview and write the documents a workplace actually runs on: an email to team leaders, a report with headings and recommendations, and a cover letter for a real-looking job posting. Every class ends with a text another learner reads critically, which is the whole module in one habit.

Trauma-informed and opinion-safe

Argument and application tasks can touch sensitive ground. Topics stay in the workplace, education, technology, health habits and community life: no party politics, religion or immigration policy, and don't bring in real news articles on those topics for the critical-reading lab. Every argument task uses an assigned side (the essay question cards say "Side A" or "Side B"), so nobody has to reveal a personal view; say so before you hand them out. The cover letter is the most personal task in the course: some learners have careers interrupted by migration, credentials that are not recognized here, employment gaps or jobs below their qualifications. Offer three options out loud, as equally valid: your real experience, a professional profile card, or a mix (your real skills in an invented job). Never ask learners to explain a gap, and never read a cover letter aloud to the class without permission. The data and people in every text are invented; say so.

Before the Module

  • Print the flashcards (set A): evaluative verbs, evidence types, connectors color-coded by function, professional formulae, and the few picture cards (read, notebook, pen, email, desk) for the "writer's toolkit" warm-up. Card stock helps.
  • For C17: the seminar role cards (set C: one set of four per group, including the Evidence Checker's pilot fact sheet) and one envelope of fallacy card-sort cards per pair (set D: 27 cards). Learners read Articles A and B in the workbook.
  • For C18: the essay question cards (set E: 6 questions × Side A / Side B; cut off the teacher strip of model theses) and one peer-review sheet per learner (set F). Print 4–6 Fallacy Bingo grids (set H) for the warmer.
  • For C19: the writing-lab pack (set G): one report data card per two learners, the five job postings (spread around the room), the reader cards for the reader panel, and the professional profile cards (set B) on a side table, where anyone can take one without asking.
  • Optional: a highlighter in two colors per learner (claims / evidence) for C17, and a phone timer visible to everyone for the peer-review rounds in C18.

Module at a Glance

LessonClassFocusLearners can…
6.1C17 · wk 9Critical-reading lab: fact vs opinion (and opinion in disguise); claim, evidence, warrant; types of evidence; purpose, stance, tone; loaded language; common fallacies (unsupported generalization, false cause, cherry-picking, false dilemma, appeal to emotion, + straw man, slippery slope, bandwagon, attacking the person); evaluative verbs (asserts, concedes, downplays, overstates, dismisses)analyze two opposing opinion articles, test their evidence against the full figures, and deliver a fair, reasoned group verdict on which makes the stronger case and which is better argued
6.2C18 · wk 9Essay-structuring workshop: thesis (specific, arguable, on-question); hook → context → thesis; PEEL body paragraphs; cohesion and signposting matched to logic; concede → rebut without straw men; restate → synthesize → implication; outline → draft → structured peer reviewplan a 4–5 paragraph argument essay on an assigned side, draft the introduction and one body paragraph, and give and use structured peer feedback
6.3C19 · wk 10Professional-writing lab: clarity, concision, register, reader-focus; the purpose-first email; the short report (headings, passive, nominalization, It is recommended that… be); the cover letter (evidence + relevance, confident not boastful); American business conventionswrite a purpose-first email from a voicemail, then a report section or a cover letter (real or fictional profile), and revise it after hearing it read aloud by a "reader"

Lesson Plans

6.1 · C17

Critical Reading — Evaluating Arguments & Bias

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • distinguish a checkable fact from an opinion, including an opinion dressed in the grammar of fact (clearly, it is widely accepted that…)
  • separate a writer's claim from the evidence, name the evidence type, and make the unstated warrant visible
  • identify purpose, stance and tone, and point to the loaded words that reveal them
  • name and locate common fallacies in a real text, and explain why each weakens the case
  • describe what a text does with precise verbs (asserts, contends, concedes, downplays, overstates, dismisses) and give a fair, hedged verdict on the argument rather than the topic
Materials
  • Workbook 6.1 (Key language, Language box, Exercises 1–5, Articles A and B, dialogue, seminar frame, model evaluation, rubric)
  • Slides 2–7 · Flashcards: evaluative verbs, evidence types · Fallacy card sort (1 envelope per pair) · Seminar role cards + pilot fact sheet (1 set per group of 4)
  • Listening scripts 6.1-A (radio commentary, "Second Opinion") and 6.1-B (dictation)

Left: the skeleton. EVIDENCE —(warrant)→ CLAIM. Three questions: What exactly is claimed? On what evidence? Does the evidence support this claim? Evidence types: statistics · research · expert testimony · anecdote · analogy · bare assertion.

Center: the verbs. asserts · contends · implies · concedes / acknowledges · downplays · dismisses · overstates · refutes / rebuts. Frame: "She concedes that…, but downplays…" · "The figure suggests a correlation but does not establish cause."

Right: two questions for the seminar. (1) Which article makes the stronger case? (2) Which article is better argued? → not the same question! Fallacy list grows here as the card sort goes on.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: upgrade the register7S–S → T–SReview C16 (5.3 Academic & Formal Vocabulary). Board four chatty sentences about an invented workplace trial: "The trial went really well and loads of people liked it." · "They looked into why people kept getting sick." · "Sales went up a bit." · "The boss thinks they should keep it." Pairs rewrite each in formal, precise English (The trial was broadly successful and widely welcomed. · The causes of staff absence were investigated. · Sales rose modestly.). Share two; praise precision (modestly, marginally) and note one error for later.
Lead-in: two headlines5T–SSlide 2. Two headlines about the same (invented) pilot: "FOUR-DAY WEEK DELIVERS" and "FOUR-DAY WEEK: CUSTOMERS PAY THE PRICE." Ask: "Same company, same six months. Which writer is for it? Which word told you? Could both headlines be factually true?" (Yes.) "Today you'll read both articles and find out what each one leaves out."
The toolkit10T–SSlides 3–4. Fact / opinion / opinion in disguise; claim → evidence → warrant; evidence types. Build the skeleton on the board with one sentence: "Sick days fell by 18%, so the pilot should be made permanent." Elicit the hidden warrant. Then the evaluative verbs (flashcards). Meaning, form, pronunciation, CCQs below.
Drill5T–S → SFrame drill (see Drill script): stress on the verb and the contrast, choral then individual, then open pairs describing a writer's move from a prompt.
Listening: "Second Opinion"6T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 6.1-A twice in a warm, opinionated radio voice. First listening: "For or against? How strongly?" Second: learners note claim, two pieces of evidence and types, a weakness, the tone. Pairs compare. Elicit the false dilemma at the end.
Controlled practice7S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2 (fact / opinion / disguise) and Ex 3 (verbs) alone, then check in pairs. Board-check only disagreements; item 7 ("Profits fell because of…") is worth a minute of debate.
Reading lab: Articles A and B13S → S–SPairs split: learner A reads Article A, learner B reads Article B (4 min, highlighting the claim in one color, evidence in another). Each fills their column of Ex 4, rows 1–4 (4 min). Then they swap articles, read quickly, and complete the remaining rows together (5 min). Monitor for "It's biased" with no anchor: "Which sentence? Which word?"
Fallacy card sort12S–SSlide 5. Envelopes of 27 cards: 9 fallacy names, 9 definitions, 9 examples. Round 1 (4 min): match names to definitions. Round 2 (4 min): add the examples. Round 3 (4 min): find each fallacy in Articles A or B and write the sentence number on the name card (some appear in both; one or two appear in neither). Check with slide 5; add names to the right of the board.
Freer: the critical-reading seminar15S–S → T–SSlide 6. Groups of 4 with role cards: Chair, Evidence Checker (holds the pilot fact sheet: the information gap), Devil's Advocate, Reporter. ICQs first. Discuss the two questions (9 min): the Evidence Checker reveals figures only when a claim is made that the figures test. Reporters prepare the four-move verdict with the workbook frame (2 min), then each Reporter gives the group's verdict in 60 seconds (4 min).
Feedback5T–SDelayed correction in three columns. Accuracy (e.g. "many evidences," "the writer critics," "it implies me that"). Range (e.g. "conflates correlation with cause," "to her credit, she concedes…"). Appropriacy (a verdict on the four-day week itself instead of on the article; "he is biased" as a conclusion rather than a starting point). Learners fix and discuss.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 6.1 on screen; point at a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Show the critical-evaluation rubric: "This is how I'll read yours." Slide 7 previews C18: from reading an argument to building one. Learners tick the "I can…" box.
Total907 + 5 + 10 + 5 + 6 + 7 + 13 + 12 + 15 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: fact, opinion, opinion in disguise. A fact can in principle be checked; an opinion is a judgment. The C1 skill is spotting opinions wearing the clothes of fact: clearly, obviously, it is widely accepted that, everyone knows, the results speak for themselves.

"Customer complaints rose by 11 percent." Can we check it? (Yes: fact.) Does it tell us whether that's serious? (No: that's interpretation.) · "The four-day week is clearly the future." Can we check "the future"? (No.) Which word makes it sound like fact? (Clearly.)

Meaning: the warrant. The unstated assumption that links evidence to claim. Often the weakest point, and the most powerful one to name.

"Sick days fell by 18 percent, so the pilot should be permanent." Is the 18 percent the claim or the evidence? (Evidence.) What must we assume for the evidence to prove the claim? (That the pilot caused the fall.) Could anything else explain it? (Yes: the season, a mild winter, a flu outbreak the year before.) Is the writer lying? (Not necessarily: the figure can be true and the argument still weak.)

Meaning: evaluative verbs.

"She concedes that response times slipped." Is that a point for her side or against it? (Against: she admits it.) · "…but downplays it as a teething problem." Does she make it bigger or smaller? (Smaller.) · "He dismisses the fall in turnover." Does he discuss it seriously? (No: he waves it away.) · "Refute" or "rebut": which one means he succeeded in proving it wrong? (Refute; rebut is the attempt.) · Writer implies, reader…? (Infers.)

Meaning: a strong case vs a well-made argument. The key conceptual move of the class.

Can an article be right but badly argued? (Yes: true conclusion, weak reasons.) Can an article be well argued and still wrong? (Yes: if its evidence turns out to be incomplete.) So when you judge an article, what are you judging? (The argument: the reasons and evidence, not whether we like the conclusion.)

Form.

Verb + that-clauseasserts / contends / concedes / acknowledges / implies that + clauseShe concedes that response times slipped.
Verb + objectdownplays / dismisses / overstates / exaggerates + noun phraseHe dismisses the fall in turnover. ✘ He dismisses that turnover fell.
Evaluating evidenceThe evidence is + adjective; the figure + suggests / indicates / does not establishThe evidence is anecdotal. The figure suggests a correlation but does not establish cause.
Uncountableevidence, information, research, advice (no plural)several pieces of evidence · a great deal of research

Pronunciation. Word stress on the evaluative verbs: conCEDE, conTEND, asSERT, imPLY, reFUTE /rɪˈfjuːt/, disMISS, overSTATE, downPLAY (the noun DOWNplay is rare). evidence = EVidence (three syllables, not ev-I-dence). In the concession frame, contrastive stress carries the meaning: "She conCEDES it, ↗ | but she DOWNplays it. ↘" Seminar language: a falling tone on a verdict sounds decided ("On balance, Article A is stronger. ↘"); a fall-rise on a concession invites the "but" ("It's a relevant figure… ↘↗").

Drill script
  1. Stress the verb (point at flashcards): T: "con-CEDE" → Ss chorally. Through the eight verbs; then T says a verb with the wrong stress (DIS-miss) and learners correct it.
  2. Name the move: T reads a line, S names it with a verb: T: "'Admittedly, response times slipped.'" → S: "She concedes that response times slipped." T: "'But it was only a minor teething problem.'" → S: "She downplays the problem." T: "'Sick days fell. Case closed.'" → S: "She overstates the evidence." T: "'Some say turnover fell, but young people always chase perks.'" → S: "He dismisses the fall in turnover."
  3. Evidence verdicts: T gives a piece of evidence, S evaluates it with one adjective + reason: T: "One bakery's delivery was late." → S: "That's anecdotal: one case can't show a pattern." T: "The best month's output." → S: "That's selective: it's cherry-picked."
  4. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 invents a one-line argument about an everyday topic ("My gym is the best, because it's always full."); S2 names the warrant ("You're assuming that popular means good."); S3 names the weakness ("That's a bandwagon argument."). Then all pairs, 1 minute.
The critical-reading seminar — how to run it
  • Groups of 4 (3 is possible: the Chair also reports). Roles on the cards. Give the Evidence Checker card to a careful reader, and the Reporter card to the strongest or most dominant speaker: they must summarize the group, not argue.
  • The information gap. Only the Evidence Checker has the pilot fact sheet. The rule: the Checker reveals a figure only when someone makes a claim the figure tests ("Whitfield says sick days fell by 18%." → "Compared with the previous six months, yes. Compared with last year, only 7%."). Groups soon discover that both columnists selected their data.
  • Devil's Advocate must argue the opposite of whatever the group seems to be concluding, at least twice. This keeps the verdict honest and gives confident learners a legitimate way to challenge.
  • Two questions, deliberately separated: "Which makes the stronger case?" (who is closer to right, given all the figures) and "Which is better argued?" (which uses evidence more fairly). Many groups conclude: Whitfield's case is stronger, but neither is well argued. That is the lesson.
  • Verdicts: each Reporter speaks for 60 seconds using the four-move frame. The class may ask one question per group. No vote on the four-day week itself: we judged the articles.
ICQs before the seminar
Who has the full figures? (The Evidence Checker.) · When can the Checker share a figure? (When someone makes a claim the figure tests.) · Is the Devil's Advocate giving their own opinion? (No: they argue the opposite.) · How many questions? (Two: stronger case, better argued.) · Are they the same question? (No!) · Are we deciding whether the four-day week is a good idea? (No: whether the articles argue well.) · Who speaks for the group at the end? (The Reporter, 60 seconds.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Some C1 learners have strong spoken English but read slowly, or learned to read in a non-Latin script. Let them work on Article A only (read it aloud to them once while they follow), with sentence numbers as anchors: their task is to find the claim (A17), one statistic (A3), one anecdote (A6–8) and two loaded phrases. In the card sort, give them the 5 core fallacies only (the ones from the online lesson). Give the seminar frame with the first words filled in. Make them the Chair (a speaking role) rather than the Evidence Checker (a reading-heavy role).

Stretch (toward C2)

Ask: "When is a 'fallacy' not a fallacy?" A slippery slope with evidence for each step is a reasonable prediction; an appeal to a relevant expert is good evidence; emotion is legitimate when the stakes are genuinely human. Then: write a one-paragraph synthesis of both articles for Harborview's managers, using the full figures, in neutral language, with no verdict (the C2 skill of reporting a debate fairly). Early finishers do Ex 5 and then load the neutral phrases the other way (euphemism is loading too).

L1 watch-outs
  • "Evidences," "informations," "researches": Spanish, French, Portuguese, Russian and Arabic speakers pluralize these (evidencias, informations, informações). Board "a piece of evidence / several pieces of evidence."
  • False friends: actual (= current in Spanish, Portuguese, French: "the actual situation" for the current situation) · argument (Spanish argumento can mean the plot of a story; Italian argomento means the topic; so "the argument of the article" may mean "what it's about") · critic used as a verb (Spanish criticar, Portuguese criticar): teach criticize, critique.
  • Reading "politely": learners from education systems where published texts and authorities are not questioned in class (common in parts of East and South Asia, the Middle East and Eastern Europe; also among learners with little formal schooling) may feel that criticizing a writer is disrespectful. Say it explicitly: "In this class, testing an argument is a sign of respect." The mindset box in the online lesson says the same.
  • Imply vs infer: most languages use one verb for both, or none. CCQ it: "The writer implies; the reader infers."
  • Tone words at C1: learners reach for "negative / positive / aggressive." Push for precision with the flashcard adjectives: scornful, indignant, dismissive, measured, wistful, alarmist, campaigning.
Homework

Online lesson 6.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_16.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "Critical Evaluation" project. Workbook 6.1: Ex 5 (loaded language) if not done in class; write a critical evaluation of Article A (150–250 words) using the model and the rubric (or of a real opinion piece on an everyday topic: a product review, a workplace blog, a column about city services); "My new words" and the journal.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C a false cause · Q2 A loaded language signals stance · Q3 (new) B answering emails quickly measures productivity. Radio commentary (Ex 1): claim = remove self-checkouts · anecdote (81-year-old neighbor) · statistic/testimony from one store (unrepresentative) · false dilemma (cashiers or nobody talks) · tone: indignant / nostalgic. Ex 2: F F D F D F D O · Ex 3: 1C 2E 3H 4F 5D 6B 7G 8A.

6.2 · C18

Academic & Argument Writing — Structuring an Essay

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • write a thesis that is specific, arguable and answers the exact question, and diagnose weak ones (topic announcement, truism, fence-sitting, off-question)
  • open with hook → context → thesis and build body paragraphs with PEEL (point, evidence, explanation, link)
  • signpost only where the logic needs it, matching the connector to the relationship (Consequently = result; By contrast ≠ Nevertheless)
  • concede the strongest opposing point fairly and rebut it without a straw man, and close with restate → synthesize → implication
  • take part in structured peer review: produce a reverse outline and checklist for a partner, and receive feedback without defending
Materials
  • Workbook 6.2 (Key language, Language box, Exercises 1–5, model essay, dialogue, outline frame, peer-review checklist, rubric)
  • Slides 8–12 · Connector flashcards (color-coded by function) · Fallacy Bingo grids (warmer) · Essay question cards with assigned sides (1 per learner) · Peer-review sheets (1 per learner) · a visible timer
  • Listening scripts 6.2-A (an introduction read aloud) and 6.2-B (dictation)

Left: the architecture. INTRO: hook → context → thesis · BODY ×2–3: Point → Evidence → Explanation → Link · COUNTER: concede → rebut · CONCLUSION: restate → synthesize → implication (no new points!).

Center: the thesis test. Could a reasonable person disagree? Does it answer this question? Weak: T (topic) · O (obvious) · F (fence) · Q (off-question). Frame: While / Although X, Y; therefore Z.

Right: connectors by job (colored flashcards). add: Furthermore · result: Consequently · compare: By contrast · concede: Admittedly / Granted · turn back: Nevertheless / Yet · example: For instance. Rebuttal openers: Yet this objection overlooks… · Such a view fails to account for…

Stage plan
