StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: Fallacy Bingo8T–S → S–SReview C17. Pairs share a bingo grid (set H). Read caller sentences from the list; pairs mark the device they hear. A pair calling "Bingo!" must name and justify each marked square. Two rounds maximum. Collect one or two homework evaluations to read later; ask two learners for the central claim of Article A in one sentence.
Lead-in: topic sentences only4T–SSlide 8. Show only the first sentence of each paragraph of the model essay. "What is the question? What is the writer's position? How many reasons?" Learners can reconstruct the argument from five sentences: that is the point of a topic sentence.
Model essay anatomy10S → S–S → T–SWorkbook model essay. Learners fold back or cover the right-hand annotation column. Pairs label the hook, context, thesis, each PEEL stage, the concession, the rebuttal, and the three moves of the conclusion (6 min), then uncover and compare (2 min). Board questions (2 min): "What does While do in the thesis? Where is the strongest objection? Is it a straw man?"
Thesis clinic8T–S → SSlide 9. Workbook Ex 2: diagnose, then rewrite two. CCQs (below). Three learners read a rewrite; the class applies the test ("Could a reasonable person disagree?").
Listening: an introduction5T–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 6.2-A twice at a measured academic pace. Learners note hook, context, thesis, and predict the body paragraphs. Elicit Although as the concession.
Cohesion & the fair rebuttal8T–S → S–SSlides 10–11. Connectors by function (flashcards on the board), then concede → rebut (slide 10); slide 11 previews the peer-review dialogue. Quick drill (below). Workbook Ex 3 (connectors) in pairs; Ex 4 part 1 (spot the straw man) together. Ex 4 part 2 and Ex 5 → homework.
Workshop 1: outline10S–S → SSlide 12. Hand out essay question cards (assigned sides; say out loud: "You argue the card, not yourself"). Learners with the same question and side sit together for 3 minutes to brainstorm evidence, then each completes the outline frame alone (7 min). Monitor theses first: every learner must have one you would accept before drafting.
Workshop 2: draft15SSilent writing: the introduction plus one full PEEL body paragraph (stronger writers: the counter-argument paragraph instead). Play quiet instrumental music if the group likes it. Circulate with a pencil; write one question in the margin, never a correction.
Workshop 3: structured peer review12S–SPairs with different questions swap drafts. Reviewers read twice and complete the peer-review sheet (6 min). Then two feedback rounds on the timer (3 min each): reviewer speaks (2 min), writer asks one clarifying question (1 min). ICQs first. Protocol below.
Feedback5T–SDelayed correction. Accuracy (comma splices, "On the other hand" used to add). Range (a well-built thesis; "This objection rests on the assumption that…"). Appropriacy (an informal slip in formal register; a rebuttal that dismisses rather than answers). Ask two writers: "What will you change after the review?"
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 6.2; point at a 🔊 button. Set the homework: finish the essay using the peer feedback; the rubric is how it will be read. Learners tick the "I can…" box.
Total908 + 4 + 10 + 8 + 5 + 8 + 10 + 15 + 12 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: thesis. The one-sentence position that every paragraph serves. Not a topic, not a fact, not a list of both sides.

"This essay is about training at work." Does it take a position? (No.) · "Training is important." Would anyone disagree? (No: so it's not a thesis.) · "There are arguments on both sides." Where does the writer stand? (Nowhere: fence-sitting.) · "Although paid training carries short-term costs, employers should treat it as an investment." Could a reasonable person disagree? (Yes: good.) What does Although do? (Concedes a point to the other side.)

Meaning: explanation in PEEL. The stage learners skip. Evidence doesn't speak for itself.

"Studies show tired workers make more mistakes." Is that the point, the evidence or the explanation? (Evidence.) Does it tell the reader why this matters for this essay? (No: that's the explanation's job.) Which phrase starts an explanation? (This matters because… · This suggests that…)

Meaning: concede vs rebut vs straw man.

"Admittedly, critics contend that rules ignore emergencies." Is the writer agreeing completely? (No: accepting a point, then answering it.) · "Yet this objection targets a blanket ban, not these limits." Is that a concession or a rebuttal? (Rebuttal.) · "Some people think workers should never learn anything." Does anybody really argue that? (No: it's a straw man, an invented weak version.) Why does a straw man make your essay weaker, not stronger? (A critical reader notices; you lose trust.)

Meaning: connectors.

"The course is expensive. Nevertheless, participants value it." Does "Nevertheless" add a point on the same side? (No: it turns against what we'd expect.) · "Small teams decide quickly. By contrast, large departments…" Are we conceding? (No: comparing two different things.) · "It saved money. Consequently, staff disliked it." Is disliking it a result of saving money? (No: wrong connector.)

Form.

Concessive thesisWhile / Although + concession, + main clause (position) + (because / for + reasons)While a total ban would be impractical, employers should set clear limits, because…
Sentence connectorfull stop / semicolon + connector + comma…the cost. Nevertheless, … · …the cost; nevertheless, …
Conjunctionalthough / whereas / while + clause, within one sentenceAlthough it is expensive, most participants value it.
Concede → rebutAdmittedly / Granted / It is true that … . Yet / However, this overlooks / fails to account for + noun phraseYet this objection overlooks the cost of replacing staff.
✘ Comma splicetwo main clauses joined by a comma only✘ Remote work saves time, it reduces costs. → ✔ …saves time; it also… / …saves time and…

Pronunciation. Reading a draft aloud is a proofreading tool, so teach it: pause at every full stop; if you run out of breath before one, the sentence is probably too long. Sentence connectors take a short pause and a slight rise: "Nevertheless ↗ | most participants value it ↘." Stress contrastive pairs in concessions: "Although it's exPENsive, | it's worth it." Word stress: THEsis (plural THEses /ˈθiːsiːz/), CONtrast (noun) / conTRAST (verb), conSEquently, ne-ver-the-LESS, adMITtedly.

Drill script
  1. Connector by function (hold up a colored flashcard): T: "Result." → S: "Consequently." T: "Concede." → S: "Admittedly." T: "Compare two things." → S: "By contrast." Choral, then fast individuals.
  2. Finish the logic: T: "Remote work saves commuting time. Furthermore…" → S: "…it widens the pool of applicants." T: "…saves commuting time. Nevertheless…" → S: "…some people feel isolated." T: "…Consequently…" → S: "…staff have more time for family."
  3. Concede → rebut chain: T gives an objection; S1 concedes it fairly, S2 rebuts: T: "Training is expensive." → S1: "Admittedly, training involves real costs." → S2: "Yet this overlooks the higher cost of replacing staff." T: "Phones in meetings help people take notes." → S1: "It is true that some staff use phones for notes." → S2: "However, a paper notebook does that without the notifications."
  4. Thesis upgrade (open pairs): S1 says a weak thesis on an everyday topic ("Libraries are good."); S2 upgrades it with Although / While + a position + a reason. Then closed pairs, 1 minute.
Structured peer review — the protocol
  • Swap across questions, not within them, so the reviewer reads as a genuine outsider (and can't "correct" the argument toward their own).
  • Reviewer's job (6 min, silent): read twice; write the thesis in your own words; write a one-line reverse outline of each paragraph; complete the checklist (Y / P / N); copy the strongest sentence; write one question that would make the draft stronger ("What would a skeptical manager say to paragraph 2?").
  • Round 1 (3 min): reviewer A speaks for 2 minutes in this order: thesis check → strongest sentence → the question. Writer B listens and does not defend or explain. Then B asks one clarifying question (1 min). Round 2: swap.
  • Language (slide 11, dialogue in the workbook): Can I check I've understood your thesis? · The strongest sentence for me is… · I wasn't entirely sure how… connects to… · You might consider… · I'd question… This is Lesson 3.3's hedging in a real job.
  • The writer owns the text. Say it: "Your reviewer gives you information, not instructions. You decide what to change."
  • Review the reviewers. Collect the sheets with the drafts. A sheet with all "Y" and "good" in the boxes goes back with: "Which sentence would you cut, and why?"
ICQs before peer review
Whose draft do you review: someone with the same question or a different one? (Different.) · What do you write for each paragraph? (One line: its point.) · Who speaks first? (The reviewer.) · Can the writer explain or defend while the reviewer is speaking? (No, just listen.) · How many questions can the writer ask? (One.) · Who decides what to change? (The writer.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give the thesis frame on a strip ("Although ______, ______ should ______, because ______.") and the PEEL openers (One clear reason is… · For instance, … · This matters because… · This shows that…). Draft target: the introduction only (3–4 sentences), then one body paragraph as notes. Pair the learner with a patient reviewer and let them review orally: the reviewer reads the draft aloud, and the writer hears where it works. Allow learners to plan orally in L1 with a same-language partner before writing in English (strategic L1 use).

Stretch (toward C2)

Write the counter-argument paragraph instead of a body paragraph, and then "rebut the rebuttal": what would the critic say back, and does the thesis still survive? Work on style: vary sentence length deliberately (a short sentence after a long one lands a point), use one cleft or fronted structure for emphasis (It is precisely this expectation that…, from Module 2), and cut every connector the logic doesn't need. Ask: "Could your thesis be more precise: to what extent, for whom, under what conditions?"

L1 watch-outs
  • Where the main point goes. English academic writing is deductive: thesis early, topic sentence first. Many writing traditions build toward the point (e.g. Japanese ki-shō-ten-ketsu, Chinese qǐ-chéng-zhuǎn-hé, Korean essay structure, some Arabic and Persian prose styles), or value elaborate introductions (Spanish, Portuguese, Italian academic prose). Present this as a convention of this genre, not a better way of thinking.
  • Comma splices and run-ons: Spanish, Portuguese, Vietnamese, Arabic and Chinese punctuation allows longer comma chains. Teach the semicolon and the full stop as tools, with the read-aloud test.
  • "On the other hand" to add a point: very common across L1s (it's used like also). It must contrast, and it sounds best after On the one hand. "Besides" is informal in English writing (French d'ailleurs, Spanish además are neutral): prefer Furthermore.
  • Articles in abstract nouns: "The education is important," "Technology is changing the society" (Spanish, French, Portuguese, Italian, Arabic, Greek use the article with general abstract nouns). Russian, Polish, Chinese, Japanese, Korean and Vietnamese speakers may drop articles entirely ("Employer should offer training"). Recast in feedback; it is a persistent C1 error in formal writing.
  • "I think" everywhere: some learners were taught never to use "I"; others use "In my opinion, I think" in every paragraph. Point to the online lesson's guidance: lean impersonal in formal essays (The evidence suggests… · This essay argues…).
Homework

Online lesson 6.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_17.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "Plan and Draft" project. Workbook 6.2: Ex 4 part 2 and Ex 5; finish the essay from the question card (4–5 paragraphs, 350–450 words), using the peer review and the rubric; "My new words" and the journal. Learners bring the finished essay to C19 (you mark it with the rubric and return it in C20).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B the concessive, arguable thesis · Q2 A move it into a body paragraph · Q3 (new) B the rebuttal that answers the cost objection. Intro (Ex 1): hook = observation about downtown parking · context = half-empty lots, reluctant councils · thesis = "Although drivers' needs cannot simply be ignored, converting underused lots into public squares would bring more people downtown…" · concession word = Although. Ex 2: T O F Q S · Ex 3: Consequently · By contrast · Nevertheless · Furthermore · Admittedly · For instance · (7) Nevertheless / However · Ex 5: B D A C.

6.3 · C19

Professional & Formal Writing — Reports, Emails & Applications

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • apply the four principles (clarity, concision, register, reader-focus) to diagnose and repair professional texts
  • write an email with a specific subject line, the purpose in the first sentence, and a polite but direct request with a deadline
  • organize a short report under headings, moving from findings to conclusions to specific recommendations, in an impersonal register (passive, nominalization, It is recommended that… be…)
  • write a cover letter that matches concrete, quantified evidence to an employer's stated needs, confident but not boastful
  • use American business conventions (Ms., Sincerely, résumé, inquire, unambiguous dates) and test tone by hearing the text read aloud
Materials
  • Workbook 6.3 (Key language, Language box, Exercises 1–5, three annotated models, dialogue, writing lab, three rubrics)
  • Slides 13–17 · Formula flashcards · Writing-lab pack: report data card, five job postings, reader cards (A = writer, B = reader) · Professional profile cards on a side table
  • Listening scripts 6.3-A (Karen Liu's voicemail) and 6.3-B (dictation)

Left: four principles. CLARITY · CONCISION · REGISTER · READER-FOCUS → "Direct is not rude; vague is not polite."

Center: three genres. EMAIL: subject → purpose → request + deadline → thanks → sign-off. REPORT: Purpose · Method · Findings · Conclusions · Recommendations (It is recommended that the form be replaced.). COVER LETTER: role + fit → evidence + relevance → confident close.

Right: US conventions. Dear Ms. Carter, · Dear Hiring Manager, · Sincerely, / Best regards, · résumé · inquire · March 3 (not 3/3). ✘ Dear Sir/Madam (when you know the name) · ✘ Respected Sir · ✘ Please revert back.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: what I changed7S–S → T–SReview C18. Learners bring their finished essays. In pairs: "Tell your partner one change you made after peer review, and why." Then two volunteers read their thesis aloud; the class applies the test. Collect the essays. Board one range example from what you hear.
Lead-in: the register ladder5T–SSlide 13. Four versions of one request (very formal · standard professional · too informal · absurdly stiff). Learners rank them and match them to readers: "a professor you've never met," "a colleague you know," "nobody." Elicit: the ask is equally clear in both correct versions; only the framing changes.
Genre jigsaw15S → S–SGroups of 3. Each member takes one annotated model in the workbook (email, report or cover letter), covers the annotation column, and works out what each part is doing (5 min); uncovers and checks (2 min). Then each "expert" teaches the genre to the other two in 2 minutes, using the matching rubric (8 min). Slides 14–16 as the class check.
Language focus & drill8T–S → SFormulae (flashcards) and report grammar: passive for findings, nominalization, It is recommended that + subject + base verb. Drill (below), then Workbook Ex 2 and Ex 4 in pairs. Ex 3 and Ex 5 → homework or early finishers.
Listening → email12T–S → S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 6.3-A (Karen Liu's voicemail) twice, naturally and a little rushed, like a real voicemail. Learners take notes, then write the email (8 min). Pairs swap and check with the email rubric: subject line, purpose in sentence 1, deadline, cc (2 min).
Writing lab: report or cover letter22SSlide 17. Learners choose: the report (data card: Findings, Conclusions, Recommendations with headings) or the cover letter (one of five job postings; as themselves or a profile card). Say the three options for the cover letter out loud. Before writing, each learner writes "My reader… My purpose…" (1 min). Circulate; conference briefly with each learner about their reader, not their grammar.
Freer: the reader panel11S–SPairs swap texts and take the matching reader card (B), which reveals what the hiring manager or the director really cares about (the information gap). The reader reads the writer's text aloud (2 min), then reacts in role for 2 minutes ("I stopped reading at… · What I needed first was… · I'd invite you to interview because…"). The writer listens and notes one change. Swap. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SDelayed correction. Accuracy ("Dear Karen Liu," "interesting in," "informations"). Range ("In light of these findings…," a well-quantified achievement). Appropriacy: the big one; board an anonymous buried request and an over-humble sentence and repair them with the class.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 6.3; point at a 🔊 button. Set the homework. Remind: never send a real application you haven't reread aloud. Learners tick the "I can…" box.
Total907 + 5 + 15 + 8 + 12 + 22 + 11 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: reader-focus and purpose-first. The commonest C1 failing is not grammar but self-centered structure: telling the story in the order the writer lived it.

"I hope you are well. We have been customers for some time. Recently there have been some issues…" After three sentences, does the reader know what you want? (No.) Where should the request be? (In the first or second sentence.) Is "Could you please confirm the delivery date?" rude? (No: it's polite and clear.) Where does the politeness live? (In the framing: Could you please.)

Meaning: findings vs conclusions vs recommendations.

"58% of respondents found the form difficult." Finding, conclusion or recommendation? (Finding: data.) · "The current system appears to be slow and unfair." (Conclusion: interpretation.) · "It is recommended that the form be replaced." (Recommendation: action.) Can an opinion go in the Findings section? (No: "Sadly, 58%…" mixes them.)

Meaning: confident vs boastful vs over-humble.

"I am an outstanding communicator." Can the reader check it? (No: empty.) · "I trained twelve new staff, and all passed probation." Can they check it? (Yes: that's why it's confident, not boastful.) · "I'm sorry if my English isn't perfect." What does it make the reader notice? (Your English. Delete it.)

Form.

Purpose openersI am writing to + verb · I am writing with regard to + noun · Following up on our conversation, …I am writing to request the Q3 figures.
RequestsCould you please + verb · I would be grateful if you could + verb · I would appreciate it if you could + verbI would be grateful if you could send it by Wednesday.
Report findingspassive (was distributed, were found to) · nominalization (a decline in) · figures in bracketsSwaps were found to take an average of four days.
RecommendationsIt is recommended / advised / proposed that + subject + base verb (subjunctive, Lesson 1.3)It is recommended that the form be replaced. ✘ …that the form is replaced (British, acceptable)
Cover letterevidence (In my current role…, I reduced / led / trained…) + relevance (…which would allow me to… / Your posting emphasizes…, and…)I reduced repeat calls by 15 percent, and your posting emphasizes quality.

Pronunciation. This class's pronunciation is the read-aloud test: a text that sounds curt, pleading or pompous aloud will read that way too. Model reading an email aloud: a polite request rises gently at the end of Could you please…? and falls on the deadline ("by Wednesday ↘"). Word stress: RÉsumé /ˈrezəmeɪ/, reSPONdent, recommenDAtion, adVERtisement (US also /ˌædvərˈtaɪzmənt/), PURpose (not pur-POSE). Weak forms in formulae: "I'd be grateful if you could /kəd/ send it."

Drill script
  1. Formula match (flashcards): T: "You're sending a document." → S: "Please find attached…" T: "You spoke yesterday." → S: "Following up on our conversation…" T: "First contact, asking for information." → S: "I am writing to inquire about…" Choral, then individuals.
  2. Report register: T reads a chatty note, S gives the report sentence: T: "We asked everyone." → S: "A survey was distributed to all staff." T: "Most people hated the form." → S: "A majority of respondents described the form as difficult." T: "Get rid of the form." → S: "It is recommended that the form be replaced."
  3. Soften, don't bury: T gives a bare command; S makes it polite but keeps the deadline: T: "Send the figures Friday." → S: "Could you please send the figures by Friday?" T: "Call me back." → S: "I would be grateful if you could call me back today."
  4. Evidence, not adjectives (open pairs): S1 says a boastful sentence ("I'm a brilliant organizer."); S2 turns it into evidence ("I organized a conference for 200 people, on budget."). Then closed pairs, 1 minute. Learners may invent.
The reader panel — how to run it
  • Why aloud, and why someone else's voice? Hearing your text in another person's voice exposes tone instantly: curtness, pleading, stiffness, and the moment the reader loses interest.
  • Reader cards (B) are the information gap. Each job posting has a hiring-manager card; the report has a director card. They reveal what that reader really cares about ("I have 80 applications. If the first paragraph doesn't name the role and one concrete reason, I stop."). The writer (A) didn't know this, which is exactly the point about reader-focus.
  • Sequence (per text, 5 min): the reader reads the text aloud (2 min) → reacts in role (2 min: what they noticed first, where they stopped or stumbled, what they would do next) → the writer notes one change (1 min). Swap.
  • Cover letters are personal. The reader reacts only to the letter as a letter. Before the panel, say: "If you wrote as yourself and prefer not to share, swap your email instead." Never ask anyone to read a cover letter to the whole class.
ICQs before the reader panel
Who reads your text aloud: you or your partner? (My partner.) · Is the reader reacting as themselves? (No: as the person on the reader card.) · Can the writer explain what they meant? (No, just listen and take notes.) · How many changes do you note? (At least one.) · If your cover letter is personal, what can you do? (Swap the email instead.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give a skeleton for the chosen genre: the email with gaps (Subject: ______ · Dear ______, · I am writing to ______. · Could you please ______ by ______? · Thank you. · Best regards,). For the report, three headings and one sentence frame each ("___% of respondents reported that…" · "These findings suggest that…" · "It is recommended that…"). For the cover letter, the opening and closing are given; the learner writes one evidence + relevance paragraph (3 sentences). The email in Ex 1 is enough as the main product; the report/cover letter can be finished at home.

Stretch (toward C2)

Write both the report and a 60-word email to the director that summarizes it (same content, two registers). Then write a reply to a difficult email: a customer complaint in which the company is partly at fault (acknowledge, apologize precisely, state the remedy, without admitting more than the facts show). For the cover letter: cut it to 180 words without losing a single achievement (preview of Lesson 8.2, precision and concision).

L1 watch-outs
  • Indirect, context-first requests: learners from high-context business cultures (e.g. Japan, Korea, China, parts of South and Southeast Asia, the Middle East) and from very polite letter traditions (Spanish, Portuguese, Arabic, Persian) may place the request after a long courteous preamble. Present purpose-first as a convention of US workplace email, not as more polite or more honest; warmth can still come in the closing.
  • Regional business English: South Asian and West African English use Kindly do the needful, Please revert (back), Respected Sir, Please find the attached herewith; Philippine English often opens with Good day! These are correct in their own contexts. Explain that US readers may find them unfamiliar, and offer equivalents (Could you please…, Please reply, Dear Mr. …).
  • Titles and names: Dear Karen Liu (full name), Dear Mrs. Carter (marital status assumed), Dear Ms. Karen (title + first name: common in Tagalog, Spanish and Arabic usage). Teach Ms. + family name, or first name only once invited.
  • German, Dutch and Hebrew speakers may write requests that sound curt in English ("Send me the file until Friday."). Also until for by (German bis, Spanish hasta, Portuguese até): "by Friday" = no later than; "until Friday" = continuously up to.
  • Cover-letter conventions differ: in some countries a photo, age, marital status or nationality is expected, and modesty formulas are obligatory. In the US these are left out (and some are legally sensitive). Explain the convention; never frame it as a language error.
Homework

Online lesson 6.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_18.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "Write One Real Professional Text" project (if it's real, send it). Workbook 6.3: Ex 3 and Ex 5; a clean final version of the report or cover letter, revised after the reader panel, and self-assessed with the rubric (circle a band for each row); "My new words" and the journal.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C "I would be grateful if you could send the figures by Friday." · Q2 B "I led a team of four and delivered the project two weeks ahead of schedule." · Q3 (new) B "Dear Ms. Carter," … "Sincerely,". Voicemail (Ex 1): to the four Receiving team leaders (Dev, Maria, Olu, Chris) · trial starts Monday, March 3 · two volunteers each by noon Thursday · training Wednesday 2–3 p.m., Conference Room B, please confirm · cc Karen Liu. Ex 2: inquire · attached · Following up · grateful · do not hesitate · look forward. Ex 5: B C H C B H.

Closing the Module

End of C19: Module 6 wrap-up

Recap on slide 18, "take it apart, build it, put it to work": claim · evidence · warrant · "She concedes…, but downplays…" (take it apart) → thesis · PEEL · concede → rebut · no new points (build it) → purpose first · findings → recommendations · evidence + relevance (put it to work). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you; the essays you collected in C19 show you which rubric rows need a revisit.

Marking the essays. Return the C18 essays in C20 with a rubric score and one comment per criterion. Don't correct every error: underline up to five, and write the category in the margin (art · splice · reg · conn) so learners fix them themselves. Praise one sentence explicitly.

Watch for in Modules 7 and 8: the C17 seminar roles return in Lesson 7.3 (seminar and discussion skills): same Chair / Devil's Advocate structure, now with live turn-taking. Note-taking in 7.1 uses the same skeleton (claim, evidence, signposts) applied to a lecture. Lesson 8.2 (precision and concision) edits the texts learners wrote here: keep their essays and cover letters. The 8.3 review project is a position paper and its oral defense: the thesis, concede → rebut and evidence standards of this module are its assessment criteria, and the essay rubric in the workbook is its basis.

Final assessment (C26) includes a C1 writing task and a reading section. From this module it tests: identifying claims, evidence types and common fallacies in a short opinion text; describing a writer's moves with precise verbs; thesis quality; connectors matched to logic; concession and rebuttal; purpose-first professional email; report register (It is recommended that… be…).

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 7 — Listening, Speaking & Delivery

Three workshop classes about live spoken English: taking it in, giving it out, and managing it with other people. First, a lecture-listening and note-taking lab: learners hear a five-minute signposted mini-lecture twice, take Cornell notes, answer comprehension and inference questions and write a summary a classmate reviews. Then a pronunciation and intonation workshop: sentence stress and weak forms, thought groups and pausing, tonic placement and contrastive stress, and intonation for attitude, practiced by shadowing marked-up scripts. Last, a seminar: learners discuss an assigned reading in fixed roles (facilitator, summarizer, challenger) and practice turn-taking, building on others' points and respectful challenge.

3 lessons Lessons 7.1–7.3 Classes C20–C22 Weeks 10–11 90 min per class CEFR C1

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. The usual shape still applies: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill → controlled practice → freer practice → feedback with delayed error correction → homework. These are skills classes, though, so plan each one as a workshop. There is less new language and more doing: listening twice, marking scripts, shadowing, discussing, then reflecting on how it went.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-7-delivery.html. Print materials/module-7/classroom-materials.html before C20. It holds the flashcards, the new identity cards, the Cornell notes sheet, the Pass the Notes mini-talk cards, the One Sentence, Many Meanings and attitude cards, the seminar pack (reading cards, role cards, move cards, observer sheet), the Signpost Bingo game and every script you read aloud, including the mini-lecture.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons and a 🎧 audio control (US or UK voice, slower speed). In this module that audio is especially useful: learners can shadow the model sentences at home.
  • You are the recording. The mini-lecture (C20) and the marked-up scripts (C21) are written to be read by you. Practice the lecture aloud once before class, with a timer: it should take about five minutes at a relaxed lecturing pace (about 650 words at 130–140 words a minute). Read it like a real lecturer, not like a dictation. Stress the signposts, pause at section breaks, and let the weak forms be weak. If a colleague with a different accent can record it for you, even better: C1 listeners need more than one voice.
  • Feedback on range, accuracy and appropriacy. In every freer task, take notes in three columns: range (ambitious language to praise and spread: "the upshot is," "I'd take that one step further"), accuracy (errors to fix: "discuss about," "I am agree," a rising statement) and appropriacy (correct English that lands wrong: a blunt "No, that's wrong," a paraphrase that sounds sarcastic, a fall-rise where the learner meant full agreement). At C1 the third column matters most. In the feedback stage, put 1–2 of each on the board without names.
  • Dominant speakers. The roles are built for this. In C20 give a dominant learner the job of summary reviewer; in C21 the coach in the delivery clinic, who listens and marks up and doesn't perform; in C22 the summarizer or the observer, not the challenger. The seminar move cards limit airtime without singling anyone out: when your cards are gone, you may only speak to hand over to someone else. Say it kindly and generally: "Great discussions have lots of voices. Let's hear from someone who hasn't come in yet."
  • Quiet learners. Many strong C1 learners go silent in groups because they don't know how to get in, not because they have nothing to say (lesson 7.3 says exactly this). Give quiet learners the facilitator role: the role is to hand over by name, so they speak often without having to argue. Warn them privately the class before if you can.
  • Stretching strong learners toward C2. Each lesson has a stretch box. The C2 direction for this module is implication and control: hearing what a speaker implies but doesn't say (C20), using prosody deliberately for irony, diplomacy and emphasis (C21), and managing a discussion so smoothly that nobody notices you are doing it (C22).
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British spellings and words (recognise, summarise, organised, centre, neighbourhood, pavement, car park, flats, high street, practise as a verb). Teach recognize, summarize, organized, center, neighborhood, sidewalk, parking lot, apartments, Main Street / downtown shops, practice. The online audio lets learners choose a US or UK voice; either is fine, but model American stress and weak forms in class (for example for /fɚ/, or /ɚ/). Accept British forms when learners use them.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Take it in → give it out → share the floor. In C20 learners listen to a talk as a structure: they hear the signposts, separate the claim from the example and put the argument on a page as a map, not a transcript. In C21 they turn the same knowledge around and make their own speech easy to follow: stress the words that matter, pause at meaning boundaries, fall to sound certain and fall-rise to sound diplomatic. In C22 they use both skills with other people: listening closely enough to build on and paraphrase what others said, and delivering challenge with the melody that keeps it respectful. The C20 mini-lecture comes back in C21 as a shadowing script, and its topic (how we learn) is one of the C22 seminar readings. Tell learners this: it shows them the module is one skill seen from three sides.

Trauma-informed: assigned sides, optional recordings, accents are welcome

Opinions are assigned. Every seminar reading gives two positions, and every contributor card assigns one. Learners argue the card, not themselves, so nobody has to reveal a personal view. The topics stay in the workplace, education, technology, health habits and city life. Don't swap in party politics, religion, immigration policy or national comparisons ("In your country, how…?"). Pronunciation work can feel exposing. At C1 it touches identity: "my voice," "my accent." Say explicitly (lesson 7.2 does too) that the goal is clear rhythm and melody, not a different accent. Never imitate a learner's accent, even affectionately. Recording is homework and is optional in class: learners who don't want to hear their own voice in front of others can shadow quietly with you or a partner instead. Listening and memory: the mini-lecture is about how we learn and forget. It's upbeat and practical, but some learners have had interrupted schooling. Frame the topic as "a tool for this course," never as "why you did badly before." For every personal task (the journal, the talk you choose for homework), offer three choices out loud: your real life, a new identity card, or something invented.

Before the Module

  • Print the flashcards (one set for the board): the signpost-function cards and note symbols (C20), the prosody cards (C21) and the discussion-move cards, color-coded by function (C22). Cut them before C20.
  • For C20: one Cornell notes sheet per learner (two if you want them to redo it for homework), and the Signpost Bingo cards (one per learner, four versions) if you have time at the end or want a C21 warmer. Rehearse the mini-lecture (Script 7.1-A) aloud with a timer.
  • For C21: the Pass the Notes mini-talk cards (warmer, one card per pair), the One Sentence, Many Meanings cards and attitude cards (one set per pair), and the marked-up shadowing scripts, which are also in the workbook. Learners who have phones may record themselves for homework; nobody has to record in class.
  • For C22: the seminar pack. For each group of 5–6, one reading card (the same reading for everyone in the group), one set of role cards, one set of move cards (four per learner) and an observer sheet. Decide the groups in advance: mix first languages, and spread strong, quiet and dominant speakers across groups.
  • Print the new identity cards and keep them on a side table where anyone can take one without asking.
  • Optional: a phone timer with a gentle sound for the seminar rounds, and a small object for each group (a pen or a card) to act as a talking token if a group needs it.
