Lesson Plans

7.1 · C20

Understanding Lectures & Talks — Following & Note-taking

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • recognize signposting language and say what each phrase predicts: a preview (This talk falls into three parts), a sequence (That brings me to my second point), an example (A case in point is…), emphasis (Crucially… · I can't stress this enough), a digression (As an aside… · I won't dwell on…) or a recap (To recap… · The upshot is…)
  • separate a main idea from its supporting detail in real time, and ask "an example of what?"
  • use discourse cues to predict (It's widely believed that… → a reversal is coming) and hear the speaker's stance (one explanation is = hedged; one of the most reliable findings = boosted)
  • take structured notes: keywords, stable abbreviations and symbols, indentation, and the Cornell layout (notes, cues, summary)
  • write a 3–4-sentence summary of a talk (claim, support, conclusion, no examples) and review a partner's summary against a checklist
Materials
  • Workbook 7.1 (New words, Language box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Listening Lab with Cornell page, summary and peer review)
  • Slides 3–7 · Signpost-function flashcards + note-symbol cards · Cornell notes sheet (1 per learner) · New identity cards · Signpost Bingo (optional, for the end or for C21)
  • Listening scripts 7.1-A (the mini-lecture, Why Cramming Fails, about five minutes) and 7.1-B (signpost dictation)

Left: the road signs. PREVIEW This talk falls into three parts → draw the map · SEQUENCE That brings me to… → new heading · EXAMPLE A case in point is… → indent, keep it short · EMPHASIS Crucially… · here's the crucial part → ⭐ write it fully · ASIDE As an aside… · I won't dwell on… → skip · RECAP To recap… · The upshot is… → the exam answer.

Center: predict. It's widely believed that… → "…but in fact" · Now, you might think… → objection + answer · So why does this matter? → main idea next · Stance: arguably, one explanation is (hedge) vs clearly, one of the most reliable (boost).

Right: the Cornell page. A narrow left column (CUES: questions, keywords, added after) · a wide right column (NOTES: taken live) · a strip along the bottom (SUMMARY: from memory). Symbols: → leads to · ↑ ↓ rise / fall · = is · ≠ is not · ∴ therefore · b/c because · w/ with · e.g. · ⭐ stressed · ? check.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: the subject-line game7S–SReview C19 (6.3, professional writing). Board: three weak email subject lines ("Question," "Meeting," "Hi!"). Pairs rewrite each as a precise, professional subject line (Request: budget figures for Q3 report by Friday), then tell a partner the three-part structure of the report or email they wrote for homework (purpose, key points, action). Note one register error and one well-chosen phrase for the feedback stage.
Lead-in: bricks or blueprint?5T–SSlide 3. Ask: "When you listen to a long talk in English, what happens in your head when you miss a word?" Collect answers (panic, lose the next three sentences, give up). Introduce the lesson's image: reading every brick vs reading the blueprint. "Today you're going to listen to the shape of a talk, and you'll hear a five-minute lecture twice."
Signposting12T–S → S–SSlide 4. Stick the function flashcards (PREVIEW, SEQUENCE, EXAMPLE, EMPHASIS, ASIDE, RECAP) across the board. Read Script 7.1-B (the signpost dictation) one line at a time; learners hold up fingers or say the function, and you stick a phrase strip under the right card. Then workbook Ex 1 in pairs. Meaning and CCQs (below): which two categories matter most to a listener, and why?
Drill: say the signpost5T–S → SSignposts are only useful if learners recognize their sound at speed. Drill five of them with lecture stress and a pause after (see Drill script), choral then individual. Then "prediction ping-pong": you say a cue, learners say what's coming (Slide 5, then Workbook Ex 2 orally in pairs).
Note-taking techniques8T–S → SSlide 6. Show the gap between a sentence and a note: "climate change is causing an increase in migration" → climate → migration ↑. Elicit symbols and abbreviations learners already use (in any language). Each learner fixes a personal shorthand of five symbols and five abbreviations in the workbook box, which they must keep stable for today, and tries it on Workbook Ex 4, items 1–3. Show the Cornell layout and hand out the Cornell sheet.
Pre-listening5T–S → S–SShow the title: "Why Cramming Fails." Pairs predict three things the lecturer will say, from the title alone. Pre-teach only three items (slide 7): to cram, retrieval (getting information out of memory) and the spacing effect. Everything else learners must ride past, which is the point of the lab.
Listening 1: chunked9SRead Script 7.1-A at a slightly slower lecturing pace, stopping at each ‖ mark for about 30 seconds of note time (there are four). Learners take notes on the right side of the Cornell sheet only. No questions yet, no repeats.
Compare & cue5S–SPairs compare notes: "What were the three parts? What did she stress?" They mark gaps with "?" and write 4–6 cue questions in the left column (Forgetting curve = ? · Why does spacing work?). Circulate: are the notes hierarchical, or are they sentences?
Listening 2: natural speed6SRead the whole script again, without pauses, at natural speed (about five minutes). Learners fill the "?" gaps in a different color. Tell them before you start: "This time, like a real lecture. No stops."
Comprehension & inference8S → S–SWorkbook Ex 5, Part B: six comprehension questions (from the notes only, don't look at the script) and six inference questions (stance, implication, why the speaker said something). Pairs discuss the inference questions; then open-class check on 3–4 of them. Push for evidence: "Which words told you?"
Freer: summary & peer review10S → S–SEach learner writes the Cornell summary strip, then a 3–4-sentence summary of the talk in the workbook (5 min): the speaker's claim, the key support, the conclusion, no examples. Pairs swap and review with the peer-review checklist (see "how to run it"), giving one "keep" and one "change" (4 min). One or two volunteers read theirs aloud (1 min).
Feedback5T–SDelayed correction, three columns. Accuracy: "The lecture was about the cramming." · "She said that we must to test ourself." Appropriacy: a summary that is really a list of examples (Ebbinghaus, two students, the 2006 study) with no claim. Range to praise: "The upshot is that…," "she concedes that…, but argues…".
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 7.1 on screen and point at a 🔊 button and the 🎧 control. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box in the workbook.
Total907 + 5 + 12 + 5 + 8 + 5 + 9 + 5 + 6 + 8 + 10 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: top-down listening. A fluent listener uses knowledge of how talks are built (preview, points, recap) to predict and slot content in. Missing a word is normal; losing the structure is the real problem.

"You missed one word in the middle of point two." Is that a disaster? (No.) What do you do? (Let it go and listen for the next signpost.) · "The speaker said, 'This talk falls into three parts.'" How many things are you listening for? (Three.) What should you do on your page? (Leave space for three.)

Meaning: the signpost functions.

"As an aside, I won't dwell on his methods." Will this come back later? (No.) Should you write it down? (No: it's optional.) · "The upshot is that walkability is a public-health strategy." Is this new information or a summary? (A summary of the main idea.) Where does it go in your notes? (At the top of the takeaways, starred.) · "A case in point is the post-war suburb." Is this the claim or the example? (The example.) What's the question to ask? ("An example of what?")

Meaning: prediction and stance.

"It's widely believed that the more hours you put in, the more you remember…" What word are you waiting for? (But.) Does the speaker agree with the belief? (Only partly.) · "One explanation is that a little forgetting is useful." Is the speaker sure? (No: it's one possible explanation, a hedge.) · "It's one of the most reliable findings in psychology." Is the speaker sure now? (Yes: a booster.)

Meaning: main idea vs detail. The recap test: would this survive into the speaker's summary? Main ideas are flagged, stressed and repeated; details come in bunches, arrive with for instance, and are said once.

"Both students study for three hours. One crams, one spaces it out." Is this the main idea? (No, it's an example.) What's the main idea it supports? (When you study matters as much as how long.) · You remember the story but not the point. What did you keep? (The packaging.) What did you lose? (The content.)

Form: the language of notes.

Keywordsnouns, main verbs, numbers; drop articles, auxiliaries and filler"memory fades fast: > ½ lost in 1 day"
Symbols→ leads to · ↑ ↓ · = · ≠ · ∴ therefore · & · ⭐ stressed · ? checkspacing → stronger memory
Abbreviationsstable, personal, decided in advanceb/c · w/ · e.g. · info · impt · govt · approx.
Hierarchymain points flush left, detail indented under them2. spacing ⭐
   – e.g. 3 hrs Sun vs M/W/F
Cornellright = live notes · left = cues added after · bottom = summary from memorycue: "Why does spacing work?"

Form: the summary. Reporting verbs and neutral register: The speaker argues / maintains / concedes / concludes that… One sentence for the claim, one or two for the support, one for the conclusion. Present simple is normal for summarizing a talk (She argues that…).

Pronunciation: what signposts sound like. Lecturers stress the content word in a signpost and pause after it: "That brings me to my second point ‖," "But here's the crucial part ‖," "So, to recap ‖." Learners from syllable-timed languages often expect every word to be clear. Show them that to, my, the and that are tiny (/tə/, /mə/, /ðə/, /ðət/) and the stressed syllable is the thing to catch. Emphasis is often carried by slowing down and a higher pitch as much as by the words: "I can't stress this enough."

Drill script
  1. Say it like a lecturer (choral, then individuals): "This talk falls into three parts ‖" · "That brings me to my second point ‖" · "A case in point is… ‖" · "But here's the crucial part ‖" · "So, to recap ‖." Tap the stressed syllable on the desk; exaggerate the pause.
  2. Function flash: T says a signpost; S says the function. T: "I won't dwell on this." → S: "Aside." T: "Turning to my third reason…" → S: "Sequence." T: "The upshot is…" → S: "Recap." Fast.
  3. Prediction ping-pong (Slide 5): T: "On the one hand…" → S: "On the other hand." T: "There are two problems with this." → S: "Problem one… problem two." T: "Now, you might think…" → S: "An objection, then the answer." T: "It's often assumed that…" → S: "But actually…"
  4. Sentence to note: T reads a sentence; S says the note aloud in keywords. T: "Remote work is causing city-center apartment prices to fall." → S: "remote work → city-center prices down." T: "This is not about laziness; it's about design." → S: "not laziness, design ⭐."
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 reads a sentence from Workbook Ex 4; S2 compresses it into a note aloud. Then all pairs, 1 minute.
The Listening Lab — how to run it
  • Before you read. Everyone has a Cornell sheet and their personal shorthand. Say: "You will hear the lecture twice. The first time I'll stop four times so you can write. The second time is natural speed, with no stops. You won't catch every word, and that's normal."
  • Listening 1 (chunked). Read Script 7.1-A at a relaxed lecturing pace, a little slower than natural. At each ‖ mark, stop, say "Thirty seconds," and wait. Don't repeat, don't explain. Look at the notes as you walk around: sentences mean the learner is transcribing; a clear 1 · 2 · 3 means they have heard the preview.
  • Compare & cue (5 min). Pairs compare, mark gaps with "?" and write cue questions in the left column. This is where the learning happens: they have to decide what the main points were.
  • Listening 2 (natural speed). Read the whole script again, straight through, at your normal speaking speed. Keep the signpost stress and the pauses at section breaks, but don't slow down for learners. Learners fill gaps in a second color. Afterwards ask: "What did you catch the second time that you missed the first time? Which signposts helped?"
  • Questions. Comprehension first (from notes only), then inference in pairs. Inference questions have no single sentence to point to, so ask for the words that support each answer.
  • Summary & peer review. Writers have 5 minutes. Reviewers use the four-point checklist: ☐ the claim is in the first sentence · ☐ the three points are there, in order · ☐ no examples, names or dates · ☐ the conclusion is the speaker's, not the writer's opinion. The reviewer gives one "keep" (a sentence or phrase that works) and one "change" (a specific, doable edit), and says them in that order. Put the protocol on the board: "Keep: … · Change: … · Because: …"
  • Roles. A dominant speaker can be the reviewer for two pairs (they must listen and read before they talk). A learner who struggled with the notes can summarize from a partner's notes: it's still a summary.
ICQs before the Listening Lab
How many times will you hear the lecture? (Twice.) · Will I stop the first time? (Yes, four times.) The second time? (No, natural speed.) · Full sentences or keywords? (Keywords and symbols.) · Which column do you write in while you listen? (The right, the wide one.) · When do you fill the left column? (After listening, with your partner.) · In your summary, do you include the examples? (No, only the claim, the points and the conclusion.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give a pre-structured Cornell sheet: write "Thesis:", "1.", "2.", "3.", "Recap:" down the notes column before class, so the learner fills slots rather than building the hierarchy. Allow notes in their first language or with drawings: the notes are for them. For the questions, do comprehension items 1–4 only, orally with you or a partner. For the summary, give a frame: "The speaker argues that ___. First, ___. Second, ___. Third, ___. She concludes that ___." Target: the three main points in the right order.

Stretch (toward C2)

Ask for stance and implication, not only content: "Where does the lecturer concede a point? Where is she ironic? What does she imply about students who say 'but I read my notes five times'?" Ask strong learners to take the second listening without the first (turn the paper over and start a clean page) and compare. Early finishers write a one-sentence summary (under 30 words), then a one-sentence critique: "The talk assumes that… but it doesn't address…"

L1 watch-outs
  • Waiting to hear every word clearly. Speakers of syllable-timed languages (Spanish, Portuguese, Italian, French, Tagalog, Turkish, Japanese, Cantonese) are used to each syllable being audible, so reduced function words sound like "missing" language, and they stop to look for it. Teach them to listen for the stressed syllables and accept that the small words are small.
  • Note-taking in full sentences. Learners from education systems that value copying the teacher's words (many, not only one region) transcribe. The fix is the rule "no verb be, no articles." Praise the ugliest, most efficient notes.
  • Summary with "the" and "about": "The lecture is about the cramming." Arabic, French, Spanish and Portuguese speakers overuse the with general nouns; Russian, Chinese, Japanese and Korean speakers may drop it where it's needed ("Speaker argues…"). Keep it light: one board example.
  • Reporting verbs + infinitive: "She suggests us to test ourselves." Review from B2: suggests testing ourselves / suggests (that) we test ourselves.
  • Summaries that judge. In some academic traditions a summary includes the writer's evaluation. At this stage, ask for the speaker's argument only; evaluation belongs in a separate sentence ("In my view…").
Homework

Online lesson 7.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_19.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back with the lecture stress; do Exercises 1–2, the Quick Quiz and the "Listen, Note, Summarize" project (a 5–12-minute talk of the learner's choice: a podcast segment, a recorded lecture or a TED-style talk). Workbook 7.1: Exercise 3 (main idea, detail or aside?) and the rest of Exercise 4, a clean Cornell page for that talk, a 3–4-sentence summary, a list of the signposts heard (aim for six), "My new words" and the journal.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C It's a digression: optional detail you can safely skip. · Q2 A Write keywords in a hierarchy, not full sentences. · Q3 (new) B The speaker will raise an objection and then answer it. Listening Lab (Ex 5) key points: thesis = cramming is inefficient; spacing and self-testing make a big difference · 1 forgetting: over half lost within a day; review flattens the curve · 2 spacing beats cramming the same hours · 3 testing yourself beats rereading ⭐ (the point she values most) · recap: study at intervals and test yourself; "close your notes and see what you can remember." Full answers in the answer key.

7.2 · C21

Pronunciation & Intonation for Fluency

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • speak with English stress-timing: stress content words and use weak forms for function words (to /tə/, of /əv/, and /ən/, can /kən/, for /fɚ/)
  • divide speech into thought groups and pause at meaning boundaries, not wherever they run out of breath, and hear how pausing changes meaning (my brother, who lives in Rome, … vs my brother who lives in Rome…)
  • place the tonic (the main stress in a thought group) on new information and move it for contrast (I didn't say SHE took it)
  • use intonation for attitude: a fall for certainty and completion, a rise for yes/no questions and "more is coming," a fall-rise for reservation and an implied "but," and avoid rising statements (uptalk) when they need to sound confident
  • mark up a text (CAPITALS, /, ↘ ↗ ↘↗, ‿) and shadow a model, then deliver a short extract clearly
Materials
  • Workbook 7.2 (New words, Language box, Exercises 1–5, marked-up dialogue, Delivery Clinic with coach's checklist)
  • Slides 8–13 · Prosody flashcards (● stress · ə weak · / pause · ↘ ↗ ↘↗) · Pass the Notes cards (warmer) · One Sentence, Many Meanings cards · Attitude cards · New identity cards
  • Scripts 7.2-A (Sam and Alex, marked up for shadowing), 7.2-B (the recap of the C20 lecture, marked up) and 7.2-C ("Which meaning?" contrastive stress)

Left: beats. I'd ● LIKE to ● BOOK a ● TABLE for ● TWO at ● EIGHT. · content words = strong · function words = weak: to /tə/ · of /əv/ · and /ən/ · can /kən/ · for /fɚ/ · at /ət/ · was /wəz/ · them /ðəm/. Weak ≠ lazy.

Center: groups and tonic. When the meeting finishes / I'll send you the notes / and we can talk tomorrow. · Let's eat / Grandma. ≠ Let's eat Grandma. · Stress the new. "Where's the report?" "It's on your DESK." · Contrast. I didn't say SHE took it (someone else did).

Right: attitude. ↘ fall = certain, finished (I live in Chicago ↘ · Where do you work ↘?) · ↗ rise = yes/no question, more to come (Are you ready ↗? · First ↗, …) · ↘↗ fall-rise = reservation, "but…" (It's a good idea ↘↗…) · ✘ uptalk: I finished the report ↗?

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: Pass the Notes8S–SReview C20. Pairs, one Pass the Notes card each. A gives a 90-second signposted mini-talk from the skeleton on the card (preview, three points, recap); B takes Cornell-style keyword notes, then summarizes the talk back in two sentences; A confirms or corrects. Swap. Listen for signposts and note one strong example for later.
Lead-in: which is easier to follow?5T–SSay one sentence twice: first with equal stress on every syllable, like a robot ("I-WOULD-LIKE-TO-BOOK-A-TA-BLE…"), then naturally. "Which one was easier to follow? Why?" Elicit: the beats tell you what matters. Slide 8: "Prosody isn't decoration: it carries meaning." Remind learners: the goal is clear rhythm and melody, not a different accent.
Sentence stress & weak forms10T–S → SSlides 8–9. Tap the beats on the desk: "I'd LIKE to BOOK a TABLE for TWO at EIGHT." Learners tap and say it. Then the "same time" demo: "I'll TELL you" vs "I would have been going to TELL you" fit into roughly the same beats. Weak-form table: choral, then individual. CCQs (below).
Thought groups & pausing7T–S → S–SSlide 10. "Let's eat, Grandma" / "Let's eat Grandma" (let them laugh). Then the brother pair (one brother or several?). Pairs do Workbook Ex 3 aloud, marking / at meaning boundaries, then say each sentence with a small pitch step at each /.
Tonic & contrastive stress10T–S → S–SSlide 11. "Stress the new, de-stress the given" with two quick Q&A pairs. Then "I didn't say she took it": read Script 7.2-C; learners call out the meaning (A–F on the slide). Then pairs play One Sentence, Many Meanings: A says the card's sentence with stress on one word; B chooses the meaning from the list on the card. Swap after three.
Intonation for attitude9T–S → S–SSlide 12. Fall, rise, fall-rise. Model "It's a good idea ↘" (agreement) vs "It's a good idea ↘↗…" (doubt); learners say which. Then the uptalk trap: "I finished the report ↗? And sent it ↗?" How does this person sound in a job interview? Pairs play the attitude cards: A reads a line in the attitude on the card, B guesses the attitude.
Controlled practice7S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1, 2, 4 and 5 (mark the beats, choose the stressed word, stress the new, match the pattern). Pairs check by saying each answer aloud, not only by comparing pages. Board-check the disagreements.
Shadowing lab9T–S → S–SRead Script 7.2-A (Sam and Alex, marked up in the workbook) line by line; learners shadow half a second behind you, copying the stress and the arrows, not just the words. Then pairs shadow each other (A reads Sam, B reads Alex, swap). Focus on the two key moves: the contrastive stress on I vs MARCUS, and the fall-rise on "It's Friday ↘↗… in theory."
Freer: Delivery Clinic15S → S–S–SGroups of 3: speaker, coach, listener. Each learner marks up a 60–90-second text (the recap of the C20 lecture, Script 7.2-B, or the summary they wrote in C20, or an extract of a talk from their homework): CAPITALS for stress, / for pauses, arrows for groups that end a statement. (5 min.) Then rounds of 3 minutes: the speaker delivers; the coach follows on the speaker's marked-up page and fills the coach's checklist; the listener says in one sentence what the main point was. Rotate. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SBoard, no names. Accuracy: a strong, long "TO" or "OF"; a pause after "the." Appropriacy: a fall-rise heard as sarcasm, or a rising statement that undercut a confident point. Range to praise: a well-placed contrast ("It's not impossible, it's difficult"), a clean rise-rise-fall list.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 7.2 and the 🎧 control (slow speed; US or UK voice). Set the homework (below): recording is recommended but private. Learners tick "I can…".
Total908 + 5 + 10 + 7 + 10 + 9 + 7 + 9 + 15 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: stress-timing. In English the stressed syllables come at roughly regular intervals; everything between them is squeezed to fit. Weak forms are correct, standard pronunciation, and they make the stressed words stand out.

"I want TO go." Is a strong TO clearer? (No, it sounds less natural and pulls the focus to the wrong word.) · Which words carry the meaning in "I'd like to book a table for two"? (like, book, table, two.) Which words are weak? (I'd, to, a, for.) · Is a weak form lazy? (No, it's standard.)

Meaning: thought groups. Speech arrives in chunks of meaning with a small pause and a pitch movement at the end of each. Pause placement works like punctuation.

"My brother, / who lives in Rome, / is a chef." How many brothers? (One; the Rome part is extra information.) · "My brother who lives in Rome / is a chef." How many brothers? (More than one: this one, the Rome one, is a chef.) · A speaker pauses after "the" to breathe. Does it help the listener? (No: it breaks a meaning unit.)

Meaning: tonic placement and contrast. By default the tonic falls on the last content word of the group. It moves forward to new or contrasted information, and given information is de-stressed.

"Where's the report?" "It's on your DESK." Why not "It's on your desk, the RE-port"? (The report is already known; stressing it sounds like a contrast with something else.) · "I didn't say SHE took it." Did someone take it? (Yes.) Who? (Not her, someone else.) · "No, the RED one." What did the other person think? (Another color.)

Meaning: intonation for attitude.

"The food was nice ↘↗…" Did the speaker love the evening? (Not completely.) What word is hidden? (But.) · "Where do you work ↗?" with a rise. Wrong? (Not wrong, but a wh-question normally falls; a rise can sound surprised or like "Did you say…?") · "I finished the report ↗?" In an interview, how does the speaker sound? (Unsure, as if asking for approval.) · "First ↗, we book; then ↘, we pay." Why the rise? (More is coming.) Why the fall? (The list is finished.)

Form: the mark-up code (learners use it on every script this module).

CAPITALSthe stressed syllable of a content word; the tonic in bold capitalsI'd LIKE to BOOK a TAble.
/a thought-group boundary (short pause, pitch step)When it's ready / I'll let you know.
↘ ↗ ↘↗fall · rise · fall-rise on the tonic at the end of a groupIt's FRIday ↘↗…
‿link a final consonant to a following vowelturn‿it‿off → "tur-ni-toff"
(ə)a weak forma CUP (ə)f TEA

Pronunciation: American weak forms and the can / can't contrast. In American English can is normally weak, /kən/ ("I can COME"), while can't keeps a full vowel and is often stressed, /kænt/ ("I CAN'T come"), and the final /t/ may be barely audible. Learners who pronounce both with a full vowel are heard as saying can't. For is /fɚ/, or is /ɚ/, and is /ən/ or just /n/ ("rock 'n' roll"). Linking: "an apple" → /ə‿ˈnæpəl/; "far away" → /fɑr‿əˈweɪ/ (the /r/ is always pronounced in American English, so linking is natural).

Drill script
  1. Tap the beats (choral, then individuals): T taps five even beats and says "I'd LIKE to BOOK a TABLE for TWO at EIGHT." S repeat, tapping. Then build up without changing the beat: "I'll TELL you." → "I'll have TOLD you." → "I would have been going to TELL you." The small words get faster, not the beat slower.
  2. Weak-form pairs: T: "a cup of tea" → S: "a CUP (ə)f TEA." T: "fish and chips" → S: "FISH (ə)n CHIPS." T: "I can come." → S: "I (kən) COME." T: "I can't come." → S: "I CAN'T COME."
  3. Stress the new: T asks, S answers with the tonic on the new word. T: "Is the meeting on Monday?" → S: "No, it's on TUESday." T: "Did Anna send it?" → S: "No, MARco sent it." T: "Did you call or email?" → S: "I CALLED."
  4. Attitude switch: T says a line and holds up an arrow card; S say it with that tune. "It's a good idea" ↘ (agreement) / ↘↗ (doubt). "That's fine" ↘ (really fine) / ↘↗ (not really). "Are you coming ↗?" / "Where are you going ↘?"
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 says "I didn't say she took it" with stress on one word; S2 says the meaning. Then all pairs, 1 minute.
Shadowing & the Delivery Clinic — how to run it
  • Shadowing means speaking along with a model, half a second behind it, copying the melody and rhythm rather than just the words. Do it first with you as the model (whole class, quietly, so nobody is exposed), then in pairs. Tell learners to exaggerate: over-doing the stress while it is conscious is exactly the practice stage.
  • Delivery Clinic, groups of 3. Roles rotate: speaker (delivers 60–90 seconds from a marked-up page), coach (holds the speaker's marked-up page and the coach's checklist, ticks what happened and chooses one thing to change), listener (doesn't look at any page and says the main point in one sentence at the end: that's the real test of clarity).
  • The coach's checklist: ☐ content words stressed, small words weak · ☐ pauses at the / marks, not in the middle of a phrase · ☐ statements end with a fall (no uptalk) · ☐ the tonic moves for contrast where marked · ☐ one fall-rise used where the text implies a "but" (if any) · ☐ pace: not rushed. Peer feedback protocol: the coach says one thing that worked ("Your pauses made the list really clear"), then one thing to change, with a demonstration ("Try 'crucially' slower, like this…"). Never more than one change per round.
  • Second try. If time allows, the speaker repeats only the part the coach chose. The group listens for the difference. This is the lesson's key moment: learners hear that one change makes them easier to follow.
  • Coaches, not judges. Accent is not on the checklist. If a coach comments on a sound ("your /θ/…"), redirect: "Today we're only coaching rhythm and melody."
  • Give the coach role to a dominant speaker first: they have to listen with the page and give exactly one comment.
ICQs before the Delivery Clinic
How many roles? (Three: speaker, coach, listener.) · Does the listener look at the page? (No.) What does the listener say at the end? (The main point, in one sentence.) · How many changes does the coach suggest? (One.) · Does the coach talk about accent? (No, only stress, pauses and intonation.) · How long is each speaker's turn? (60–90 seconds.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Work with sound first, symbols second. Let the learner tap and hum the rhythm before marking anything. Give a pre-marked text (Script 7.2-B is fully marked up in the workbook) instead of asking them to mark their own. Reduce the mark-up code to two symbols: CAPITALS and /. In the clinic, give a 30-second extract (the last three sentences of the recap). Target: five clear beats and no pause in the middle of a phrase.

Stretch (toward C2)

Work on prosody for irony and diplomacy: the same words as sincere praise, faint praise (a low fall-rise) and irony (an exaggerated, slow high fall: "Oh, great."). Ask strong learners to mark up a paragraph from a real talk they heard for homework and explain why the speaker put the tonic where they did. Discuss pitch range: a wide range sounds engaged; a narrow, flat range can sound bored or unfriendly even with polite words. Early finishers write their own "one sentence, many meanings" card with five readings.

L1 watch-outs
  • Syllable-timed rhythm (equal weight on every syllable): Spanish, Portuguese, Italian, French, Tagalog, Turkish, Japanese, Cantonese, Mandarin, Vietnamese and many others. Learners sound clear sound-by-sound but hard to follow. The desk-tapping drill is the fix.
  • Strong forms everywhere: learners who learned English from print pronounce to, of, for, and, can, was fully. Explain that full forms are for emphasis or the end of a sentence ("Who's it for?").
  • Stress on the last word by default: French speakers tend to put stress on the last syllable of every group and find contrastive stress unnatural (French uses c'est… qui instead). Link this to C1 Lesson 2.3 (clefts): English can do the same job with voice alone.
  • Tone languages (Mandarin, Cantonese, Vietnamese, Thai, Yoruba): pitch is part of each word, so learners may not expect it to carry attitude across a sentence, and the fall-rise can come out as a word-level tone. Practice it on one word first ("Well ↘↗…").
  • Flat or rising statements: some varieties (and some learners' nerves) push statements up at the end; the listener hears uncertainty. Russian speakers often produce yes/no questions with a sharp rise-fall on the key word, which can sound like a demand in English; practice the gentle rise.
  • Vowel added to break consonant clusters: Spanish and Portuguese "es-tress", Arabic and Persian "fi-rist," Japanese "su-to-re-su." The added syllable breaks the beat. Tap the word as one beat: "STRESS."
  • Final consonants and linking: Vietnamese, Cantonese and Thai speakers may drop final consonants, so linking ("turn‿it‿off") disappears too. Practice linking as a way to keep the final sound: it moves onto the next vowel.
  • can / can't: everyone. Weak can /kən/ vs full can't /kænt/ in American English.
Homework

Online lesson 7.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_20.html. Shadow the five model sentences with 🔊 at the slow speed, then at normal speed; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "Record, Compare, Refine" project (a 4–6-sentence passage, marked up, recorded privately, compared with the 🎧 model and re-recorded). Workbook 7.2: the "I didn't say she took it" drill in all six positions (write the meaning of each), "My new words" and the journal. Recording is recommended but private: nobody has to share it.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B like, book, table, two. · Q2 C Reservation: an implied "but I have doubts." · Q3 (new) C "No, I sent it on TUESday." (The tonic moves to the corrected, new information.) Ex 2 (choose the stressed word): 1 red · 2 table · 3 I · 4 today · 5 say · 6 (new) Marco.

7.3 · C22

Seminar & Discussion Skills

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • manage turn-taking: come in politely (Could I just come in here?), hold the floor (If I could just finish my point…) and hand over by name (What are your thoughts, Priya?)
  • build on another speaker's point by naming or echoing it before extending it (Building on Anna's point about training, …)
  • clarify and paraphrase to check understanding (When you say "scalable," do you mean…? · So if I understand you correctly, …)
  • challenge respectfully with acknowledge → soften → challenge (I take your point about speed, but I'm not sure we've thought through the cost) and find common ground (Where we do agree is…)
  • steer and summarize (So where does that leave us? · So, to sum up where we are, …) and perform a seminar role: facilitator, summarizer or challenger
Materials
  • Workbook 7.3 (New words, Language box, Exercises 1–5, the seminar dialogue, Seminar prep page and self-review)
  • Slides 14–18 · Discussion-move flashcards (color-coded) · Seminar pack: reading cards (6 topics), role cards, move cards (4 per learner), observer sheet · New identity cards
  • Scripts 7.3-A ("The meeting that went wrong") and 7.3-B (the four-day-week seminar, for Ex 4)

Left: the floor. IN: Could I just come in here? · Sorry to jump in, but… · HOLD: If I could just finish my point… · Just to finish my thought… · OVER: What are your thoughts, ___? · Does anyone see it differently? · BUILD: Building on what ___ said… · That ties in with ___'s point… · CHECK: When you say ___, do you mean…? · So if I understand you correctly, …

Center: challenge. ACKNOWLEDGE I take your point about ___, → SOFTEN but I'm not sure → CHALLENGE we've thought through ___. · Up to a point, yes, but… · Isn't there a risk that…? · Where we do agree is… · Say it with a fall-rise on the acknowledgment: "I take your point ↘↗…"

Right: roles. FACILITATOR opens, hands over by name, keeps time, refocuses · SUMMARIZER tracks the thread; summarizes at half time and at the end · CHALLENGER tests every claim respectfully ("What's the evidence?") · CONTRIBUTORS argue the side on their card · OBSERVER tallies moves; gives feedback. Steer: So where does that leave us? · Shall we move on?

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: stress the new7S–SReview C21. Pairs, correction chain: A makes a deliberately wrong statement about the class or the course ("The seminar is on Monday."); B corrects with contrastive stress ("No, it's on THURSday."); A corrects something else in B's sentence, and so on. Then 2–3 learners say "It's a good idea" with a fall and a fall-rise; the class says which is which. Note one good contrast and one uptalk example.
Lead-in: the meeting that went wrong10T–S → S–SRead Script 7.3-A (change voices or give the parts to three confident learners). Everyone has good ideas; nothing works. Pairs list what went wrong (talking over, no links, blunt "No, that's wrong," nobody checks what "flexible" means, no summary, one person silent). Elicit onto the board as the five skill areas: turn-taking, building, clarifying, disagreeing, steering. "Today you'll fix every one of these."
Turn-taking10T–S → SSlide 14. In · hold · over. Meaning and CCQs: interrupting vs coming in. Pronunciation: the softener just is weak and quick, and the request is a gentle rise ("Could I just come in here ↗?"). Drill (below). Then "fishbowl": four volunteers talk for 60 seconds about a light topic (the best time of day to study); the class calls out "IN," "HOLD," "OVER" when they hear one.
Building, clarifying & paraphrasing8T–S → S–SSlide 14 (lower half). Contrast "I think we should focus on training" with "Building on Anna's point about skills, I think training is where we should focus." What changed? Paraphrase: "So if I understand you correctly, you're saying…?" Pairs: A says an opinion from Workbook Ex 5; B paraphrases it with a rise; A confirms ("Exactly.") or corrects ("Not quite. What I meant was…").
Respectful challenge8T–S → S–SSlide 15. Acknowledge → soften → challenge. CCQs. Pairs do Workbook Ex 3 (soften the blunt disagreement) aloud first, then write two. Pronunciation: fall-rise on the acknowledgment ("I see your point ↘↗, but…"), then a clear fall on the challenge. Firm on the idea, warm to the person.
Steering & summarizing5T–SSlide 16 (the model seminar). Read Script 7.3-B or project the slide; learners find the steering and summarizing moves ("So where does that leave us?" "So, to sum up…" "Shall we move on to…?"). Point out that Priya disagrees twice and the discussion never sours.
Controlled practice7S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1, 2 and 4 (complete the phrase, complete the exchange, name the move). Pairs check aloud. Board-check disagreements only.
Seminar prep10S → S–SSlide 17. Groups of 5–6 (pre-planned). Each group gets one reading card; everyone reads it (4 min). Hand out role cards and move cards (4 each). Learners fill the prep page in the workbook: two points for their assigned side, one question for the other side, the four moves they'll play (4 min). ICQs (2 min).
Freer: the Seminar15S–S → S12 minutes of seminar. The facilitator opens with the reading's question and hands over by name; the challenger tests claims; the summarizer gives a half-time summary at minute 6 (you call it) and a final summary at minute 11. Learners lay a move card face up each time they use that move. Then (3 min) the observer gives feedback from the observer sheet: two strengths, one move nobody used.
Feedback5T–SDelayed correction across groups: accuracy ("I am agree," "discuss about," "According to me"), appropriacy (a paraphrase that sounded like a trap; "You're wrong" from a challenger; a facilitator who never spoke), range to praise ("That ties in with what Lena said earlier…," "Up to a point, yes, but…"). Learners fill the self-review in the workbook.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 7.3 and a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick "I can…".
Total907 + 10 + 10 + 8 + 8 + 5 + 7 + 10 + 15 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: interrupting vs coming in. Coming in is a request that leaves the speaker a moment to yield; interrupting takes the floor.

"Could I just come in here?" Is this a question or an instruction? (A question: the speaker can say "Let me just finish.") What does just do? (Makes the request smaller, more polite.) · Someone talks over a colleague with a brilliant idea. Is that OK in an English-language seminar? (Usually no: it costs goodwill.) · "What are your thoughts, Priya?" Why use the name? (It draws in a quieter person and shows you're tracking everyone.)

Meaning: building and paraphrasing.

"Building on Anna's point about skills…" Whose idea is this? (Anna's, extended.) How does Anna feel? (Heard.) · "So if I understand you correctly, your worry is coverage?" Is the speaker agreeing? (Not yet: checking.) What can the other person do? (Confirm or correct.) Does a paraphrase sound weak? (No: attentive and precise.)

Meaning: respectful challenge. The idea is challenged, not the person. The acknowledgment must be genuine, and it must name something specific.

"I take your point about speed, but I'm not sure we've thought through the cost." Does the speaker agree? (Partly: about speed.) Is the challenge weak? (No, it's clear: the cost.) · "No, that's wrong." Is the view stronger? (No, only the temperature is higher.) · "Great point, BUT…" said very fast every time. Does it sound genuine? (No: the acknowledgment must be specific.)

Form.

Come inCould I just + verb…? · Sorry to + verb, but… · If I could just + verb…Sorry to jump in, but…
BuildBuilding on / Following on from + noun phrase (what you said · Tom's point)Building on what Tom said, …
ClarifyWhen you say + X, do you mean…? · Could you clarify what you mean by + noun?…what you mean by "flexible"?
ParaphraseSo if I understand you correctly, you're saying (that)… · So your point is that…?…you're saying cost matters more than speed?
ChallengeI take your point about + noun, but… · I'd question whether + clause · Isn't there a risk that + clause?I'd question whether it works for a client-facing team.
SteerShall we + base verb? · So where does that leave us? · It sounds like we broadly agree that…Shall we move on to the budget?

Pronunciation. The moves only work with the right melody (a direct link to C21). Softeners (just, sorry, I think) are weak and quick. A paraphrase ends on a rise: it's a real question ("…your worry is coverage ↗?"); with a fall it sounds like an accusation. The acknowledgment takes a fall-rise ("I take your point ↘↗…"): it signals that a "but" is coming, gently. The challenge itself falls: clear and calm. Name + question for handing over: "What do you think, Priya ↘?" (a fall is warmer and more confident than a rise here).

Drill script
  1. The door handles (choral, then individuals, with the melody): "Could I just come in here ↗?" · "If I could just finish my point…" · "What are your thoughts, Priya ↘?" · "Building on what you said…" · "So where does that leave us ↘?"
  2. Function to phrase: T says a function; S says a phrase. T: "Hold the floor." → S: "If I could just finish my point…" T: "Check understanding." → S: "So if I understand you correctly, …" T: "Common ground." → S: "Where we do agree is…"
  3. Blunt to respectful: T says a blunt line; S rebuilds it. T: "That won't work." → S: "I see the appeal, but I'm not sure how it would work in practice." T: "You're wrong about the cost." → S: "I take your point about the cost, but I'd question whether…"
  4. Paraphrase with a rise: T states a view; S paraphrases with a rise. T: "Phones in meetings are a distraction, but banning them treats adults like children." → S: "So you're saying they're a problem, but a ban isn't the answer ↗?"
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 gives a view on a light topic (the best way to learn vocabulary); S2 builds on it by name; S3 challenges it respectfully. Then groups of three, 2 minutes.
The Seminar — how to run it
  • Groups of 5–6, pre-planned: mixed first languages, one confident and one quiet learner in each, dominant speakers spread out. Each group discusses one reading card. The six readings: the four-day workweek, phones in meetings and classes, exams vs portfolios, car-free downtown streets, AI writing tools in college courses, and spaced practice vs cramming (the C20 lecture topic). All readings are short, neutral briefings with two positions and one seminar question.
  • Roles (role cards): Facilitator: opens with the question, hands over by name, watches the clock, refocuses ("Could we come back to the main question?"). Summarizer: tracks each person's key point on the observer grid, gives a half-time summary ("So far we broadly agree that…, but we're split on…") and the final summary. Challenger: tests claims on both sides, respectfully ("What's the evidence for that?" "Isn't there a risk that…?"), and must acknowledge before every challenge. Contributors A and B: argue the side printed on their card, not their own view. Observer (sixth learner, or rotate): doesn't speak; tallies moves on the observer sheet and gives feedback at the end.
  • Move cards. Each learner gets four cards (e.g., COME IN · BUILD ON · PARAPHRASE · RESPECTFUL CHALLENGE). Each time they use a move, they lay the card face up. Goal: all four down by the end. When your cards are all down, you may only speak to hand over to someone who still has cards. This limits dominant speakers and gives quiet ones a reason to speak, without anyone being singled out.
  • Timing. Call "Half time: summarizer" at minute 6 and "One minute: summarizer" at minute 11. The facilitator closes: "Thanks, everyone. So, to sum up…" then hands to the summarizer.
  • Observer feedback protocol (3 min): two strengths first, each with a name and a quote ("Lena built on Omar's point about training: 'That ties in with…'"), then one move nobody used, as a group target, not a personal criticism. No comments on accent or grammar here; that's your job in the feedback stage.
  • Your job during the seminar: circulate with the three-column sheet (range · accuracy · appropriacy). Don't join a group. If a group stalls, hand the facilitator a prompt card quietly ("Ask the challenger to respond to B's last point").
  • If you have fewer than five learners in a group, merge the summarizer and the facilitator. With a very large class, run two rounds of 10 minutes with new roles for the second round.
ICQs before the Seminar
Are you giving your own opinion? (No, the side on my card.) · Who opens the discussion? (The facilitator.) · When does the summarizer speak? (At half time and at the end.) · Does the challenger challenge only one side? (No, both sides.) · What must the challenger do before every challenge? (Acknowledge the point.) · What do you do with a move card when you use the move? (Put it face up.) · When all your cards are down, can you keep arguing? (No, only hand over to someone else.) · Does the observer speak during the seminar? (No, only at the end.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Pre-teach the reading one class ahead, or read it aloud to the group while they follow. Give the learner two move cards instead of four, each with the full phrase printed on it, and a role with a script: the facilitator card has every line written out ("What are your thoughts, ___?"). Pair them with a supportive summarizer. Target: two moves used, including one hand-over by name.

Stretch (toward C2)

Give strong learners the challenger role with an extra rule: every challenge must also offer something ("…so could we trial it first?"). Or a synthesis task: the summarizer's final summary must link at least three people's points by name and name the one remaining disagreement precisely. At C2 the moves disappear: ask them to use the functions without the textbook phrases ("Hang on, Priya's point changes things, doesn't it?").

L1 watch-outs
  • Directness norms travel badly in both directions. Learners from more direct discussion cultures (for example Dutch, German, Russian, Hebrew or Polish) may sound blunt with "No, that's wrong," while learners from cultures that value waiting and harmony (for example Japanese, Korean, Chinese, Vietnamese or Thai) may never come in at all. Present both as habits to adjust, never as national traits, and practice the same moves with everyone.
  • Overlap as engagement: in many Spanish, Portuguese, Italian and Arabic conversations, talking at the same time shows interest. In an English-language seminar it reads as interrupting. The coming-in phrase is the bridge.
  • "I am agree" / "I am not agree" (Spanish estoy de acuerdo, French, Portuguese and Italian): agree is a verb: "I agree," "I don't agree."
  • "Discuss about" (Spanish discutir sobre, Arabic and others): discuss the idea, no preposition. Also "explain me" → explain to me.
  • "According to me" (French selon moi, Hindi, Urdu and Bengali speakers, Spanish según yo): use In my view, As I see it; according to introduces someone else's view.
  • Literal politeness: "Please give me the floor" or "I want to say something" (a direct translation of a polite form in the learner's language) can sound abrupt in English. "Could I just…" does the same job.
Homework

Online lesson 7.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_21.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back with the melody; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "Run a Real Discussion" project (with family, colleagues, classmates online, or scripted solo; for the topic, use one of the seminar readings or another from the kit's topic areas). Workbook 7.3: finish the self-review, "My new words" and the journal. Optional: watch a recorded panel discussion and note every turn-taking and building phrase you hear.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A "Sorry, could I just come in here?" · Q2 B "I take your point, but I'm not sure we've thought through the cost…" · Q3 (new) B "Thanks, Omar, that's really useful. I'd love to hear from someone who hasn't come in yet. Lena?" Ex 1: in · finish · Building · understand · point · leave. Ex 2: finish · come in · thoughts · correctly · agree · move.

Closing the Module

End of C22: Module 7 wrap-up

Recap on slide 18, "take it in, give it out, share the floor": signposts, the recap test and Cornell notes (take it in) → stress the new, pause at meaning, fall to finish, fall-rise for "but" (give it out) → come in, build on, paraphrase, acknowledge before you challenge, summarize (share the floor). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Revisit it in the C23 warmer; Signpost Bingo is ready for that if you didn't use it in C20 or C21.

Watch for in Module 8: Lesson 8.1 (fluency and spontaneity) builds directly on C22. Thinking-time phrases (That's a good question… · Let me think about that for a second) are the solo version of "holding the floor," and paraphrasing around a missing word uses the same skills as paraphrasing a colleague. Lesson 8.3 (the position paper and its defense) is the module's real test: learners will present with signposting (7.1), deliver with clear prosody (7.2) and handle challenges from the floor (7.3). Keep the seminar roles and the coach's checklist for that class.

Final assessment (C26) covers all eight modules. From this module, the listening section uses a signposted talk with comprehension and inference questions (signpost functions, main idea vs example, stance), and the speaking interview and discussion task reward turn-taking, building on the examiner's or partner's points, paraphrasing and respectful disagreement, delivered with clear stress, sensible pausing and statements that fall.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 8 — Fluency, Craft & the Final Project

The last three classes of C1 pull in two directions and then bring them together. First, fluency under pressure: buying time, using fillers with control, talking around a missing word and repairing a sentence mid-flight, rehearsed in impromptu one-minute talks. Then craft: editing for precision and concision, cutting wordiness, redundancy, empty intensifiers and buried verbs, with a 30% Cut Challenge. Finally, the review project, A Position Paper & Its Defense: every learner writes a paper of about 500 words on an everyday, workplace, education or technology question and defends it in a short viva-style Q&A with peers.

3 lessons Lessons 8.1–8.3 Classes C23–C25 Weeks 12–13 90 min per class CEFR C1

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. The first two follow the usual shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice → feedback with delayed error correction → homework. At C1, 8.1 and 8.2 are really skills workshops: the "language" is a set of strategies and editing moves, and most of the class time goes to doing them. C25 is a project class: short input, then the vivas.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-8-position-paper.html. Print materials/module-8/classroom-materials.html before C23. It holds the Talk Around It picture cards and the phrase cards, the new identity cards, the impromptu topic cards and curveball cards, the strategy spotter sheets, the before/after editing cards, the four 30% Cut Challenge texts, the Keep Talking board game, the position paper kit (topic cards, side cards, viva roles, the rotation sheet and the chair's script), the six question-card sets, the paper rubric and defense rubric, the teacher observation checklist and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences again at home. For this module the online lessons are also where learners record themselves: the two-minute impromptu talk (8.1) and the recorded defense (8.3).
  • The project starts in C23, not C25. A 500-word paper cannot be written in class. Learners choose a topic at the end of C23, fill in the planning frame (Workbook 8.3) for C24, write and edit the paper after C24 and bring it (or email it to their viva group) before C25. C25 itself is the defense. The timeline is in the box "The project timeline" below; put it on the board in C23 and leave it there.
  • Refinement, not a first meeting. Learners already hedge (C1 3.3), nominalize (3.1), build cohesion (3.2), structure an argument essay (6.2) and manage a seminar (7.3). Don't re-teach any of it. This module asks them to do it in real time (8.1), economically (8.2) and all at once (8.3). Point back to the earlier lesson whenever a learner reaches for a tool: "That's your 3.3 hedge. Good."
  • Feedback on range, accuracy and appropriacy. In every freer task, take notes in three columns: Fix (errors), Upgrade (correct but could be more precise or more natural) and Wow (ambitious, well-judged language to spread). At C1 the most useful feedback is often appropriacy: the hedge that makes a claim too weak, the formal phrase in a chatty answer, the filler that turns into a tic. Board 2–3 items from each column, without names.
  • Dominant speakers. In C23 the strategy spotter role (listens, tallies, reports) and a strict 60-second timer do most of the work. In C24 give the strongest voice the meaning-keeper job: they may only defend ideas, not add words. In C25 the viva format is naturally fair: each learner has one defense and fixed examiner turns, and the chair keeps them. Say it kindly and early: "Great question, Ana. Let's give Wei the next one."
  • Quiet or anxious learners. Impromptu speaking is the most exposing task of the course. Let learners choose the easy topic pile first, allow a 15-second silent planning time, and treat any visible strategy ("Let me think… Well, it depends…") as a success, even if the content is thin. In C25, a nervous learner may present to their group seated and may keep their paper in front of them.
  • Stretching strong learners toward C2. Each lesson has a stretch box. The C2 direction for this module is control and invisibility: at C2, strategies stop being noticeable. Fillers become rhetorical pauses, circumlocution becomes precise paraphrase, a repair becomes a deliberate refinement ("or, more precisely…"), editing becomes rhythm and emphasis, and a defense becomes genuine intellectual exchange (reframing the question, identifying the assumption behind it). Ask strong learners to explain why a choice works, and give them the hardest topic cards.
  • Peer review, the C1 way. All three classes use the same protocol, and learners already know it from C17–C18: (1) say what the text or talk does well, specifically; (2) ask a question before you suggest a change ("What did you mean by…?"); (3) suggest one or two changes, not ten; (4) the writer or speaker decides. Post it on the wall. Peer comments are about the work, never the person.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British spellings and words (recognise, apologise, nominalisation, practising, centre, organisation, pedestrianised, pavements, footfall, shop, full stop, defence). Teach recognize, apologize, nominalization, practicing, center, organization, pedestrianized / car-free, sidewalks, foot traffic, store, period, defense. Accept British forms when learners use them, but ask for one system per paper.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Keep going → cut back → stand your ground. In C23 learners learn to keep speech moving when a word, an idea or a sentence fails them: That's a good question… It's a kind of… or rather… In C24 they learn the opposite discipline, going back over writing to cut everything that doesn't earn its place: due to the fact that → because, make a decision → decide. In C25 the two come together. The paper is the edited, concise product of C24; the defense is the spontaneous, strategic speaking of C23, with hedging and concession from earlier modules. Every class ends with something real that other learners hear or read: a one-minute talk, an edited text, a defended position.

Trauma-informed: topics, sides and "pass"

Impromptu speaking and a public defense are high-stakes for adults who have had to defend themselves in far more serious settings: interviews with officials, workplace disputes, family conflict. Keep every topic in everyday, workplace, education, technology, health-habit and community territory. The kit's topic cards contain no party politics, religion, immigration or border policy, policing, war or national identity; don't add any, and don't use the policy-style prompts listed in the online lesson 8.3 (on government regulation and public debate) as in-class topics. Learners may choose a topic card or draw one, and they may draw a side card so that the paper argues an assigned position: "You're arguing the card, not confessing your opinion." In C23, personal topics ("a skill I'm glad I learned") can always be answered as a new identity or invented, and any learner may say "Pass, next card." In C25 the viva is never an interrogation: examiners challenge the argument, the chair stops any question that becomes personal, and nobody is asked where they are from or what they personally believe.

The project timeline (board it in C23)
End of C23Choose or draw a topic card (and a side card, if you want an assigned position). Read Workbook 8.3: the structure and the planning frame.
Before C24Fill in the planning frame: thesis, two or three arguments with support, the strongest objection, your rebuttal, your conclusion. Notes only.
C24 (last stage)Frame check in pairs: is the thesis arguable? Is the objection real, not a straw man?
After C24Write a first draft (it will probably be 600–700 words), then edit it with the C24 checklist down to about 500 words (450–550). Send it to your viva group (email or group chat) by the evening before C25, or bring 4 printed copies.
C25Read two group members' papers closely, prepare questions, then the vivas: a 2-minute opening statement and about 5 minutes of questions each.
After C25Hand in the paper (with the rubric self-check). You return it with written feedback at C26.

Why ~500 words when the online lesson says 250–400? The online capstone is written in one sitting. In class, learners have a week, an editing lesson in between, and a real audience, so the paper can carry two or three developed arguments and a proper rebuttal. Five hundred words is still short enough that every sentence must earn its place, which is the point of C24. If a learner's life is too full for 500 words this week, 350–400 well-edited words are fine: tell them so privately.

Before the Module

  • Print the flashcards: the Talk Around It picture cards (16) and the phrase cards (time-buyers, repairs, editing moves, defense moves). Card stock helps. Cut them before C23.
  • For C23: the impromptu topic cards (18, in three piles: Warm-up, Opinion, Stretch) and the curveball cards (6), one set per group of 3; one strategy spotter sheet per group; a phone timer per group (learners' phones are fine). The Keep Talking board game (1 per group of 3–4, a die or coin, counters) is for early finishers in C23 and the C24 warmer.
  • For C24: one set of before/after cards per pair and one 30% Cut Challenge text per group of 3 (four different texts, A–D). Pencils and highlighters: editing is physical work.
  • For the project: the position paper topic cards (16) and side cards at the end of C23. For C25: the viva role cards and rotation sheet (1 per group), the chair's script (1 per group), the question-card sets (1 full set of 24 per group), the paper rubric and defense rubric (1 of each per learner), and your observation checklist on a clipboard.
  • Print the new identity cards and keep them on a side table where anyone can take one without asking.
  • Set up a way for viva groups to share papers before C25 (a group email, a shared folder, or printed copies). Decide the viva groups at the end of C24, so that learners know whose papers they will read.
  • Read the kit's C1 final assessment before C25. Your C25 observation notes feed straight into how you run C26.

8.1C23 · wk 12Fluency as continuity, not speed; buying time (That's a good question… · Let me think… · Well, it depends… · How can I put this?); fillers and discourse markers with a job vs as tics; circumlocution (the thing you use to… · a kind of… · it's like…, but… · the person who…); smooth self-correction (or rather… · what I mean is… · let me rephrase that)give an impromptu one-minute talk on a topic they did not choose, without freezing or restarting, using at least three strategies deliberately, and notice their own filler habits
8.2C24 · wk 12Editing for concision: wordy phrases (due to the fact that → because) and redundant pairs (end result, each and every); empty intensifiers (very tired → exhausted); strong verbs over noun + light verb (make a decision → decide) as the reverse of nominalization; double negatives and tangled sentences; the redraft mindsetcut a wordy text by at least 30% without losing a single idea, name each cut they make, and judge when not to cut (formal density, genuine emphasis, evidence sources)
