The six-part structure (thesis → argument A → argument B → counter-argument → rebuttal → conclusion); the C1 toolkit in one piece; the viva: opening statement, question types, concede → hold → hedge, fluency strategies under questioningwrite a concise, well-structured position paper of about 500 words, then summarize it aloud and defend it against challenging questions, conceding what is fair and holding what is sound

Lesson Plans

8.1 · C23

Fluency & Spontaneity — Speaking Strategies

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • explain the difference between fluency (keeping going coherently) and speed, and between a filled pause and a frozen one
  • buy thinking time with phrases that also carry meaning: That's a good question… · Let me think for a second… · Well, it depends… · How can I put this? · The way I see it…
  • use discourse markers (well, I mean, you know, sort of, actually) where they do a job, and thin out the reflexive ones, including um and er
  • talk around a missing word with a category, a function, a comparison, a role or a situation, so that one gap never stops a sentence
  • repair a sentence on the move (or rather… · what I mean is… · to put it another way…) without apologizing or restarting
  • give an impromptu one-minute talk from a topic card, using at least three strategies on purpose
Materials
  • Workbook 8.1 (New words, Strategy box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Impromptu Talks + strategy checklist)
  • Slides 3–8 · Phrase cards (time-buyers, repairs) · Talk Around It picture cards (16) · Impromptu topic cards (Warm-up / Opinion / Stretch) + curveball cards · Strategy spotter sheets · phone timers · Keep Talking board (early finishers) · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 8.1-A (Freeze or flow?), 8.1-B (the filler audit), 8.1-C (the interview with Maya) and 8.1-D (What am I describing?)
  • Position paper topic cards and side cards (for the project launch at the end)

Left: fluency = keep going. ✘ freeze (long silence, "sorry, I don't know how to say it") · ✔ filled pause (a phrase, then a calm beat). Buy time: That's a good question… · Let me think for a second… · Well, it depends… · How can I put this? · The way I see it… · Bear with me a moment…

Center: talk around it. a kind of… (category) · the thing you use to… (function) · it's like…, but… (comparison) · the person who… (role) · it's when / where… (situation) · the, oh, whatever it's called… Repair: or rather… · what I mean is… · let me rephrase that · to put it another way…

Right: fillers, job or tic? well (a start) · actually (a mild surprise / correction) · I mean (a clarification) · you know (shared feeling) · sort of (an honest approximation) → ✔ one, with a job · ✘ five in a sentence. Corner: the project timeline (see above).

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: seminar rewind6S–SReview C22 (7.3). Groups of 3, one light question on the board: "Should meetings have a strict time limit?" Four minutes; each learner must use one move from 7.3 (Could I just come in here? · Building on what you said… · So if I understand you correctly… · Up to a point, yes, but…). One learner per group reports the best move they heard. Note one Fix and one Wow.
Lead-in: freeze or flow?6T–SSlide 3. Read Script 8.1-A: two answers to the same hard question. Workbook Ex 1: learners note what Speaker 2 does at each of the three "walls." Ask: "Who was faster?" (Neither.) "Who sounded more fluent? Why?" Elicit the key idea: fluency is continuity, not speed. Quick show of hands: "What do you do when you freeze in English?"
Buying time & fillers12T–S → S–SSlides 4–5. Elicit the time-buyers learners already use; add the rest from the phrase cards. CCQs (below). Then the filler audit: read Script 8.1-B once at natural speed; learners tally fillers (Workbook Ex 5). Compare tallies (there are about 23). Read the clean version. Pairs decide which two or three fillers in the transcript do a real job.
Talk around it10T–S → S–SSlide 6. The five frames. Hold up a Talk Around It picture card and describe it without naming it ("It's the thing you use to change the channel without getting up…"); learners shout the word. Then Script 8.1-D: six quick descriptions, learners write the word. Pairs: each draws 3 picture cards face down and describes them with a different frame each time.
Repair on the move6T–SSlide 7. Read the clumsy and the smooth repair from the slide in two voices. CCQs. Elicit why "sorry, sorry, that's wrong" hurts (it points at the mistake). Workbook Ex 4 items 1–2 together.
Drill6T–S → SHot-seat drill (see Drill script): hard question → time-buyer + first sentence; forgotten word → frame; wrong detail → or rather. Choral for the phrases (with intonation), then fast individuals.
Controlled practice9S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2–4 alone (choose the strategy phrase, talk around it, clumsy → smooth), then check in pairs. Board-check only the disagreements. Read Script 8.1-C (the interview) once while learners follow the dialogue and underline every strategy.
Freer: Impromptu Talks25S–S (groups of 3) → T–SSlide 8. Groups of 3: speaker, timekeeper (60 seconds, calls "time" and asks one follow-up question), strategy spotter (tallies on the spotter sheet). The speaker draws a card, gets 15 seconds silent thinking time, then talks for one minute, then answers the follow-up (20–30 s). Spotter reports: "You bought time twice, talked around one word, one smooth repair; one silence at 40 seconds." Rotate roles. Round 1 Warm-up pile · Round 2 Opinion pile · Round 3 Stretch pile + a curveball card. ICQs first. See "How to run it."
Feedback5T–SDelayed correction. Board 2 Fixes (e.g. "Sorry, sorry, I mean… sorry." · "It's a thing for… how do you say… for, um… no."), 2 Upgrades ("I think… I think… I think" → The way I see it… / If you ask me…) and 1–2 Wows. Ask the class: "Which strategy felt the most natural? Which one is hardest to use under pressure?"
Homework & project launch5T–SShow online lesson 8.1 and a 🔊 button; set the homework (below). Then launch the project: board the timeline, show the topic cards on slide 14, and let learners choose or draw a topic (and a side card if they want one). Point to the planning frame in Workbook 8.3. Learners tick the 8.1 "I can…" list.
Total906 + 6 + 12 + 10 + 6 + 6 + 9 + 25 + 5 + 5
If you run long

Protect the Impromptu Talks: they are the lesson. Cut in this order: (1) Script 8.1-C becomes homework (it's the online dialogue); (2) Script 8.1-D shortens to three items; (3) the warmer shrinks to two minutes. If the project launch gets squeezed, hand out the topic cards at the door and launch properly in the first five minutes of C24, but learners then have one day less to plan.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: fluency. At C1, fluency means sustaining connected, comprehensible speech while you are still thinking. Pauses are normal; silent, frozen pauses and restarts are what break fluency.

"The reason is… um… [long silence] … sorry, I don't know how to say it." Did the speaker know the answer? (Probably, yes.) What went wrong? (She stopped and apologized; the listener lost the thread.) · "The reason is, well, how can I put this? It's partly about cost." Was this speaker faster? (No.) Why does it sound more fluent? (There was no dead silence; the phrase filled the gap.)

Meaning: time-buyers carry meaning.

"That's a good question…" Is it only stalling? (No, it also acknowledges the person who asked.) · "Well, it depends…" What does the listener expect next? (A balanced answer with conditions.) · "How can I put this?" Is the speaker lost? (No, they're choosing words carefully: it signals precision.) · Could you use "That's a good question" to answer "What time is it?" (No: it only fits a hard or interesting question. Overuse sounds evasive.)

Meaning: fillers, job or tic?

"It was, you know, a difficult decision." Does you know do a job here? (Yes: it invites the listener to share the feeling.) · "I was, you know, walking to, you know, the store, you know." (No job: it's a tic, and we start counting instead of listening.) · "Actually, it worked well." What does actually tell us? (It's a bit surprising, or it corrects an expectation.) · "It's sort of a compromise." Hedge or tic? (A hedge: it's an honest approximation.) · "It's sort of on Tuesday." (A tic: Tuesday is a fact; don't hedge it.)

Meaning: circumlocution and repair.

"It's the thing you use to open a wine bottle." Does the speaker know the word? (No, not right now.) Did the conversation stop? (No.) What often happens next? (The listener says the word: "A corkscrew!") · "We met on Tuesday, or rather, Wednesday." Which day was it? (Wednesday.) Did she apologize? (No.) Does the listener trust her more or less? (More: she's monitoring her own speech.)

Form.

Buy timeThat's a good / an interesting question… · Let me think (for a second)… · Well, it depends (on…) · How can I put this? · The way I see it… · If you ask me… · Bear with me a moment…then a short calm beat, then the answer
Talk arounda kind of / a type of + category · the thing (that) you use to + verb · it's like + noun, but… · the person who + verb · it's when / it's where + clause"the person who fixes pipes" (plumber)
Flag a gapwhat's the word…? · the, oh, whatever it's called… · I can't think of the word, but…keep going, don't stop
Repairor rather… · or should I say… · what I mean is… · let me rephrase that · to put it another way…no "sorry," no restart
Markerswell (start / turn) · actually (surprise, mild correction) · I mean (clarify) · you know (shared feeling) · sort of / kind of (honest hedge)one, with a job

Pronunciation. Time-buyers only sound natural with the right tune. That's a good question is said slightly slower, with a fall on question and a short pause after: "That's a good QUEStion ↘ | …". Well, it depends has a fall-rise on well (↘↗ = "it's not simple"). Let me think is often "lemme think." Repairs are said lightly and quickly, a little lower in pitch, as if in brackets: "We met on Tuesday (or rather) WEDnesday." Stress the corrected word, not the repair phrase. After a time-buyer, slow down slightly: a measured pace sounds more fluent than a rushed one. Um and er can often be replaced by a silent beat with eye contact: it sounds confident, not lost.

Drill script
  1. Tune the time-buyers (choral, then individuals, with the intonation): "That's a good QUEStion…" · "Well ↘↗, it dePENDS…" · "How can I PUT this?" · "Let me THINK for a second…"
  2. Hot seat: hard question → time-buyer + one sentence. T: "What's the biggest problem with modern work?" → S: "That's a good question. I'd say it comes down to…" T: "Is it better to rent or buy?" → S: "Well, it depends on…" T: "How would you describe your ideal manager?" → S: "How can I put this? Someone who…"
  3. Talk around it (T says a word only the learner sees on a card; S describes it with the frame T names): T: "Function!" (ladder) → S: "It's the thing you use to reach something high." T: "Role!" (landlord) → S: "It's the person who owns the apartment you rent." T: "Situation!" (traffic jam) → S: "It's when all the cars stop and nobody can move."
  4. Or rather chain: T gives a wrong detail and the right one: "Tuesday → Wednesday." → S: "We met on Tuesday, or rather, Wednesday, and…" T: "twenty people → thirty." → S: "There were about twenty, or rather, thirty people…"
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 asks a hard question from the board; S2 answers for 20 seconds using a time-buyer, one circumlocution (S1 whispers a "forbidden" word S2 may not use) and one repair. Then all pairs, 2 minutes.
Impromptu Talks — how to run it
  • Groups of 3, sitting in a small triangle. Each group has the three card piles face down, the curveball cards, a spotter sheet and one phone timer.
  • One turn = about 2 minutes: draw a card (or choose between two, see below), 15 seconds of silent thinking (no writing, or three key words at most), 60 seconds of talk, then the timekeeper asks one follow-up question and the speaker answers in 20–30 seconds. The spotter reports in 20 seconds. Rotate: every learner speaks once per round.
  • Round 1: Warm-up pile (familiar, personal-ish topics; new identity cards welcome). Round 2: Opinion pile ("Are online reviews trustworthy?"). Round 3: Stretch pile (abstract: "Is boredom useful?") plus a curveball card the timekeeper reveals at 30 seconds ("Now argue the opposite," "Explain it to a 12-year-old," "Your listener interrupts: 'Sorry, what do you mean by that?'").
  • Rules on the slide: no silence longer than about 3 seconds; no restarting; no "sorry." If you dry up, use a time-buyer, repeat your main point in other words, or say "Let me come at this from another angle."
  • The spotter sheet has four columns: time-buyers · talked around a word · smooth repairs · long silences / restarts, plus one line: "a filler you used a lot." The spotter reports numbers and one example, not a judgment. This is the job for a dominant speaker: they must listen, count and report briefly.
  • Choice lowers anxiety. In Rounds 1 and 2 the speaker draws two cards and chooses one. Anyone may say "Pass, next card" once per round. Nobody watches the whole class: all groups talk at the same time.
  • You circulate with a notepad (Fix / Upgrade / Wow). Spend one full turn with each group. Early-finishing groups play the Keep Talking board game.
ICQs before the Impromptu Talks
How long do you talk? (One minute.) · How long can you think first? (Fifteen seconds.) Can you write a script? (No, three words at most.) · What does the timekeeper do at the end? (Asks one follow-up question.) · What does the spotter do? (Counts strategies and silences, then reports numbers.) · If you forget a word, do you stop? (No, talk around it.) · If you make a mistake? (Repair it: "or rather…" and keep going.) · Can you say "Pass"? (Yes, once per round.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give the learner four phrase cards only to hold during the talks: That's a good question · Well, it depends · It's a kind of… · or rather. Let them talk for 30–40 seconds and choose from the Warm-up pile in every round. Pair them in the triangle with a patient speaker, and make them the timekeeper first so they watch two turns before speaking. In the workbook, do Ex 2 items 1–4 and Ex 3 with the four picture items. Target: one time-buyer and one circumlocution, used on purpose.

Stretch (toward C2)

Make the strategies invisible: a rhetorical question instead of a stock time-buyer ("So why does this matter? Well…"), a precise paraphrase instead of "the thing" ("a device for measuring air pressure"), a refinement instead of a correction ("or, more precisely…"). Speak for 90 seconds and take the Stretch pile from Round 1. Give the curveball before the talk and ask them to build it in. Ask: "Which of your fillers did a job? Which were habits?" Homework: the online two-minute talk, recorded twice.

L1 watch-outs
  • L1 fillers leaking into English. Under pressure, learners fill pauses with the filler of their first language: Spanish este…, o sea; Japanese ēto, ano; Mandarin nàge, jiùshì; Arabic yaʿni; Tagalog ano…, parang; Russian nu…, tak skazat'; French euh, du coup; Korean eum, geu. Treat it kindly and privately: it's a sign the learner is thinking. Give them one English replacement to practice (Well… / Let me think…).
  • "How do you say…?" in the middle of a turn, often followed by the L1 word. Fine in class, fatal in an interview. Teach the frame that keeps going: "It's a kind of…"
  • Over-apologizing. Learners from cultures where apologies soften any imposition (Japanese sumimasen, Korean, and many others) may say "sorry" at every repair. Repairs in English need no apology.
  • "Actually" as a false friend: Spanish actualmente, French actuellement, Portuguese atualmente, German aktuell, Italian attualmente mean "currently." "Actually, I work in a hotel" (meaning "at the moment") will confuse. Actually = in fact / surprisingly.
  • Over-hedging vs no hedging. Learners whose academic culture values indirectness (Japanese, Korean, Chinese, Thai, Vietnamese) may put sort of / maybe / a little on everything; learners from more direct traditions (German, Dutch, Russian, Hebrew, Polish) may drop even useful markers and sound abrupt. Both are appropriacy issues, not errors: show the middle.
  • Speed as a status symbol. Some learners (often very able ones) believe speed = fluency and rush. Time them and praise the slower, clearer version out loud.
Homework

Online lesson 8.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_22.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and shadow them with the same tune; do Exercises 1–2, the Quick Quiz and the Two-Minute Impromptu Talk (record it, play it back and tally your strategies). Workbook 8.1: finish Ex 5 if needed, "My new words" and the journal. Project: choose your topic and fill in the planning frame in Workbook 8.3 (notes only) for C24.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B keeping going coherently, even while you think. · Q2 C describe it: "the thing you use to open a wine bottle." · Q3 (new) A "The meeting is on Thursday, or rather, Friday, so we have an extra day." Ex 1 (Script 8.1-A): wall 1 a hard question → "That's a good question. Let me think for a second." · wall 2 a missing word (deadline) → "the date when everything has to be finished" · wall 3 a wrong detail (three projects) → "or rather, four."

8.2 · C24

Precision & Concision — Editing Your English

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • replace wordy stock phrases with lean ones (in order to → to · due to the fact that → because · at this point in time → now · in the event that → if) and cut redundant pairs (end result, free gift, each and every, completely eliminate)
  • delete empty intensifiers and qualifiers (very, really, quite, basically, just, actually) and upgrade the word instead (very tired → exhausted · really important → crucial), while keeping intensifiers that add genuine emphasis or precise degree
  • dig strong verbs out of noun + light-verb strings (make a decision → decide · carry out a review of → review), and explain when nominalization (C1 3.1) is still the right choice
  • resolve double negatives (not uncommon → common) and untangle an overloaded sentence by splitting it or fronting the main idea
  • cut a text by at least 30% without losing any idea, count before and after, and name each cut
Materials
  • Workbook 8.2 (New words, three Grammar boxes, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, the 30% Cut Challenge)
  • Slides 9–13 · Phrase cards (editing moves) · Before/after cards (4 pairs, 1 set per pair) · 30% Cut Challenge texts A–D (1 per group of 3) · highlighters, pencils · Keep Talking board (warmer)
  • Listening scripts 8.2-A (In the Editing Room) and 8.2-B (Say it shorter)
  • Learners' planning frames (homework from C23) for the frame check

Left: cut the padding. in order to → to · due to the fact that → because · at this point in time → now · in the event that → if · a large number of → many · has the ability to → can · in spite of the fact that → although · in the near future → soon. Redundant pairs: end result · free gift · past history · advance planning · each and every · completely eliminate.

Center: upgrade and dig out. very tired → exhausted · really important → crucial · very surprised → astonished · basically just really actually. make a decision → decide · reach a conclusion → conclude · carry out an investigation of → investigate · give consideration to → consider. Match the tool to the goal: strong verbs = clear, direct (email, summary, argument) · nominalization = formal, dense (report, legal).

Right: untangle. not uncommon → common · not without merit → has some merit · one breath? no → split it / front the main idea. The editor's questions: Fewer words? Padding? Is this intensifier earning its place? A verb hiding in a noun? Does the reader already know this? Read it aloud. 30% = cut 3 words in 10. No idea lost.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: Keep Talking7S–SReview C23. Groups of 3–4 play the Keep Talking board game for 6 minutes: each square asks for a 30-second answer using a named strategy (buy time, talk around, repair). Then ask: "Which strategy is now automatic?" Note one Fix and one Wow.
Lead-in: one editing pass5T–SSlide 9. Read the famous line "I didn't have time to write a short letter, so I wrote a long one" and ask what it means. Show the first draft (35 words) and the edited version (16 words). Ask: "Which ideas were lost?" (None.) "What exactly was cut?" Learners name four or five cuts. "Today: the editor's eye."
The four cuts18T–S → S–SSlides 10–12, one cut at a time, each with 2–3 CCQs (below) and a 1-minute pair task: (1) wordy phrases and redundant pairs; (2) empty intensifiers → one strong word (and when an intensifier stays); (3) buried verbs, linked explicitly to C1 3.1: "Last month we nominalized on purpose. Today we reverse it on purpose"; (4) double negatives and tangled sentences (the "one breath" test). Listening Script 8.2-A (In the Editing Room): learners list the four cuts the editor names.
Drill: Say it shorter6T–S → SScript 8.2-B as an oral drill: you read a wordy sentence, learners say the concise version aloud (choral for the first two, then individuals). Then the "strong word" drill (see Drill script).
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1–5 alone (tighten, redundant word, upgrade, dig out the verb, untangle), then check in pairs. Board-check only the items where pairs disagree, especially Ex 3 (keep or cut?).
Freer: Editing Workshop26S–S → groups of 3 → T–SSlide 13. Round 1 (5 min), before/after cards: pairs match each "before" card to its "after" and name every cut (wordy phrase, redundant pair, intensifier, buried verb, double negative, tangle). Round 2 (15 min), the 30% Cut Challenge: groups of 3 get one text (A–D, about 150 words). Target: cut at least 30% (the target is printed on the card) and lose no idea. Roles: editor (holds the pen), word-counter, meaning-keeper (ticks each idea on the original as it survives). Round 3 (6 min): each group reads its final version aloud and says the word count and one cut they're proud of. The class listens for lost meaning: "Did they cut an idea?" ICQs first.
Project: frame check6S–SPairs swap planning frames (Workbook 8.3). The partner checks only two things and writes one question: (1) Is the thesis arguable? Could a reasonable person disagree? (2) Is the objection real? Would someone on the other side actually say it, or is it a straw man? Use the peer-review protocol: strength, question, one suggestion, writer decides.
Feedback7T–SBoard 2 Fixes from the workshop (an over-cut sentence that lost an idea or became unclear, e.g. "The app helps time." · a meaning change: "Many adults think teenagers are lazy" → "Adults think teenagers are lazy"), 2 Upgrades (a correct cut that could go further) and 1–2 Wows. Point out one good decision not to cut (e.g. keeping "according to the developers' own research": the source matters).
Homework & viva groups5T–SShow online lesson 8.2 and a 🔊 button; set the homework (below). Announce the viva groups for C25 (3–4 learners) and how papers will be shared. Learners tick the 8.2 "I can…" list.
Total907 + 5 + 18 + 6 + 10 + 26 + 6 + 7 + 5
If you run long

Protect Round 2 of the workshop and the frame check (a learner who arrives at C25 with an unarguable thesis has a hard viva). Cut in this order: (1) the warmer becomes 3 minutes of one-minute talks in pairs; (2) Round 1 (the before/after cards) moves to homework, since the workbook dialogue covers the same ground; (3) the controlled practice becomes Ex 1, 3 and 4 only, with Ex 2 and 5 at home.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: concision. Concision means saying the same thing in fewer, stronger words. The goal is clarity, not a low word count: a cut that removes an idea, a source or a necessary nuance is a bad cut.

"Due to the fact that we did not have a sufficient amount of time…" → "Because we lacked time…" Is any meaning lost? (No.) Which is easier to read? (The second.) · "Research by the developers shows…" → "Research shows…" Is anything lost? (Yes! Who did the research. That matters for trust.) So is shorter always better? (No: clarity and accuracy come first.)

Meaning: redundancy and intensifiers.

"a free gift": can a gift cost money? (No: free adds nothing.) · "completely eliminate": can you eliminate something halfway? (No.) · "very tired" vs "exhausted": which is stronger? (Exhausted.) Which uses fewer words? (Also exhausted.) · "I'm absolutely certain." Should we cut absolutely? (Not necessarily: it's genuine emphasis, a choice.) · "slightly undercooked": cut slightly? (No: it gives a precise degree.)

Meaning: strong verbs vs nominalization.

"The committee will carry out a review of the proposal." Where's the real action? (Review.) Which verb is doing the grammar? (carry out: an empty verb.) Shorter version? ("The committee will review the proposal.") · In C1 3.1 we turned verbs into nouns. Was that wrong? (No, it's right for formal, impersonal writing: a report, a legal clause.) So which do you use in an email to your team? (Strong verbs.) In a formal research summary? (Often nominalization.) Who decides? (The writer, for the reader and the purpose.)

Meaning: double negatives and tangles.

"It is not uncommon for delays to occur." Common or uncommon? (Common.) How many switches did your brain flip? (Two.) Is "not uncommon" ever useful? (Yes, for deliberate understatement, but not as a default.) · A 30-word sentence with four clauses: can you say it in one breath and remember how it began? (No.) What can you do? (Split it into two, or put the main idea first.)

Form.

Wordy → leanin order to → to · due to / owing to the fact that → because, since · at this point in time → now · in the event that → if · a large number of → many · has the ability to → can · in spite of the fact that → although · with regard to → about · in the near future → soon
Redundant pairend result · free gift · past history · advance planning · each and every · completely eliminate · close proximity · final outcome · join together → keep the word that carries the meaning
Intensifier → wordvery tired → exhausted · very big → huge, vast · really important → crucial, vital · very good → excellent · very bad → terrible, dire · very surprised → astonished · very hungry → starving
Light verb + noun → verbmake a decision → decide · reach a conclusion → conclude · carry out an investigation of → investigate · give consideration to → consider · have a discussion about → discuss · provide assistance to → help · make an improvement to → improve
Untanglenot uncommon → common · not unlike → like / similar to · not impossible → possible · one long sentence → two sentences, or main idea first

Pronunciation. Reading aloud is the editor's best test, so teach learners to read their drafts in thought groups: a pause at each natural break, stress on the new information. Wordiness shows up as too many unstressed syllables in a row ("due to the fact that we are currently at a point where…"): the voice runs out of energy. A concise sentence has a clear rhythm of stressed words: "Because COSTS are SOARing, we've deCIDed to reVIEW our SPENDing." Where a learner runs out of breath, the sentence probably needs to be split.

Drill script
  1. Say it shorter (Script 8.2-B; each sentence once, learners say the concise version). T: "In order to save money, we are going to walk." → S: "To save money, we'll walk." T: "Due to the fact that it was raining, the game was canceled." → S: "Because it was raining, the game was canceled." T: "At this point in time, we have the ability to deliver on Saturdays." → S: "We can now deliver on Saturdays."
  2. One strong word (fast): T: "very tired" → S: "exhausted." T: "really important" → "crucial." T: "very surprised" → "astonished." T: "very big" → "huge." T: "very bad" → "terrible." T: "very clean" → "spotless."
  3. Find the verb: T: "make a decision" → S: "decide." T: "reach a conclusion" → "conclude." T: "give an explanation of" → "explain." T: "have a discussion about" → "discuss." T: "make an improvement to" → "improve."
  4. Cut or keep? T reads a sentence; S says "Cut!" or "Keep!" and why. T: "It's basically just a really simple idea." → S: "Cut! 'It's a simple idea.'" T: "I'm absolutely certain the door was locked." → S: "Keep: real emphasis." T: "The meat was slightly undercooked." → S: "Keep: exact degree."
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 reads a sentence from Workbook Ex 4 with its wordy form; S2 says it concisely without looking. Swap. Then all pairs, 1 minute.
The 30% Cut Challenge — how to run it
  • Four texts, four genres (materials set F): A an email to front-desk staff about a schedule change (151 words → target 105 or fewer) · B a community center newsletter item (156 → 109) · C an app description (143 → 100) · D a paragraph from a student report on teenage sleep (159 → 111). Give different texts to neighboring groups so they can compare at the end. Text D connects to the model position paper in Workbook 8.3.
  • Roles: the editor holds the pen and marks cuts on the card (cross out, write above); the word-counter counts after each sentence and keeps a running total; the meaning-keeper has a list of the text's ideas (on the back of the card) and ticks each one as it survives. The meaning-keeper can veto any cut: "No, that loses the time." Give this job to a dominant speaker: they defend ideas but can't add words.
  • Sequence: (1) read the text aloud once, together; (2) first pass: wordy phrases and redundant pairs (the fast wins); (3) second pass: intensifiers and buried verbs; (4) third pass: tangles and double negatives; (5) count; (6) read the final version aloud and fix any sentence where the reader stumbles.
  • Honesty rules: no idea may disappear; numbers, names, times, and sources of evidence stay; don't change the register (the email stays an email). If the group reaches 30% quickly, the stretch target is 50%: the model versions in the answer key cut 53–57%.
  • Round 3 read-out: each group reads its version and gives "before / after / percent." Classmates with the same text (if any) compare choices; everyone else listens for lost meaning and asks one question. Vote for "clearest," not "shortest."
  • Keep the cut texts. At the end of the class, show how the same moves apply to the position paper: "Your first draft will be about 650 words. You'll cut it to 500 the same way."
ICQs before the workshop
Round 1: what do you write next to each cut? (Its name: wordy phrase, intensifier, buried verb…) · Round 2: how much do you cut? (At least 30%, the number on the card.) · Can you cut an idea? (No, never.) · Can you cut a number or a source? (No.) · Who holds the pen? (The editor.) Who can say "No, that loses meaning"? (The meaning-keeper.) · Which version wins: the shortest or the clearest? (The clearest.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on the fast wins only: the wordy-phrase list and the redundant pairs. Give the learner the Grammar box as a separate strip to hold while editing, and a highlighter to mark padding before crossing it out. In the workshop, make them the meaning-keeper (tick the ideas) or the word-counter rather than the editor, and give the group the shortest text (C). Workbook: Ex 1, Ex 2 and Ex 3 items 1–4. Target: five correct swaps, read aloud.

Stretch (toward C2)

Aim for 50% in the Cut Challenge and then do the reverse: rewrite the cut text in a deliberately formal, nominalized register for a different reader, and explain which version suits which purpose. Look at rhythm: vary sentence length on purpose (a long sentence, then a short one for impact). Find one sentence where a double negative is the better choice (polite understatement: "Her proposal is not without merit"). Early finishers edit a paragraph of their own planning notes.

L1 watch-outs
  • "Long = educated." In many academic traditions (Spanish, Portuguese, French, Italian, Russian, Arabic, Persian, and German with its Nominalstil), long, nominal, subordinate-rich sentences signal education and respect. Learners may feel that cutting makes them sound simple. Frame concision as an English reader-responsible convention, not "better thinking," and show that the C1 3.1 formal style still has its place.
  • "very" as a default: Mandarin 很 hěn often links a subject and an adjective with little intensifying force ("She is very tall" = "She is tall"), so learners overuse very. The strong-word drill helps.
  • Double negatives from negative concord: Spanish, Portuguese, Italian, Russian, Polish and others require multiple negatives ("No sé nada" → "I don't know nothing"). That's a grammar error; "not uncommon" is a style choice. Keep the two separate so learners don't start avoiding all negatives.
  • Comma splices in tangled sentences: Spanish, Russian, Vietnamese and Chinese allow clauses joined by commas where English needs a period, a semicolon or a linker. When learners "split" a sentence, check they really split it.
  • "in order to" everywhere: Romance para / pour / per + infinitive translates naturally as "in order to." Plain to is enough in most cases.
  • Over-cutting (any L1): dropping articles, that or auxiliaries to hit the target ("Committee will review proposal"). That's headline style, not concision. Check grammar after every cut.
Homework

Online lesson 8.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_23.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the Cut It by 25% project (a paragraph of your own). Workbook 8.2: "My new words" and the journal. Project: write the first draft of your position paper from your planning frame, put it aside for a day if you can, then edit it with the 8.2 checklist to about 500 words. Send it to your viva group by the evening before C25 (or bring four printed copies).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A We will decide now. · Q2 A when the genre calls for formal, impersonal abstraction: match the tool to the goal. · Q3 (new) C "Because many staff were absent, we postponed the review." Editing Room (Script 8.2-A): the four cuts the editor names are (1) a wordy phrase (due to the fact that → because), (2) padding cut (we are currently at a point where), (3) an intensifier upgraded (very high → soaring), (4) two buried verbs dug out (made the decision to → decided to · carry out a review of → review). 29 words → 11.

8.3 · C25

Review Project — A Position Paper & Its Defense

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • write a position paper of about 500 words with a clear thesis, two or three supported arguments, a fairly stated counter-argument, a rebuttal and a conclusion, edited for concision
  • use the C1 toolkit where the argument needs it: hedging, a participle or absolute clause, an inversion or cleft for emphasis, a conditional for consequences, nominalization and cohesive reference, academic collocation
  • give a two-minute opening statement that summarizes the paper from notes, without reading it
  • understand and answer clarifying and challenging questions: check the question when needed, concede what is fair, hold what is sound, and hedge a claim rather than overclaim or collapse
  • keep going under questioning with fluency strategies (8.1), and ask a peer a sharp, respectful question as an examiner
Materials
  • Workbook 8.3 (structure, language bank, Exercises 1–5, the model paper, the planning frame, the pre-hand-in checklist, defense prep, rubric self-check, final C1 journal)
  • Slides 14–18 · Phrase cards (defense moves) · The position paper kit (materials set H): viva role cards, the rotation sheet and the chair's script (1 per group) · Question-card sets (set I, 24 cards per group) · Paper rubric and defense rubric (set J, 1 each per learner) · Teacher observation checklist (set K) on a clipboard · a timer on the screen
  • Listening scripts 8.3-A (Priya's defense) and 8.3-B (a model opening statement and two model answers)
  • Learners' papers (shared the evening before, or 4 printed copies each) · 2 spare printed copies of the model paper for anyone who arrives without a paper

Left: THE VIVA (per learner, about 9 min). 🪑 Chair introduces · 🎤 Opening statement (2 min, from notes) · ❓ Lead examiner: 2 questions · ❓ Second examiner: 1–2 questions · ❓ Chair: 1 question (if time) · ⭐ Examiners: one star + one wish (1 min) · → rotate

Center: CONCEDE → HOLD → HEDGE. That's a fair point, and I don't want to brush it aside. · You're right that… · That said, … · I'd still argue that… · To some extent, yes; however… · So I'd put it slightly more cautiously: … · If I've understood your question, you're asking whether…

Right: QUESTION TYPES. 🔍 clarify (What exactly do you mean by…?) · 📊 evidence (How do you know that…?) · ⚖️ objection (What about people who…?) · 🔮 consequence (If this were adopted, what might go wrong?) · 📏 scope (Where do you draw the line?) · 🃏 wildcard (Which of your arguments is weakest?)

Corner: Challenge the argument, never the person. · The chair stops personal questions. · Not a grade: C25 is practice. · C26 = final assessment + celebration 🎉

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: thesis in 30 seconds5S–SReview C23–C24. Pairs (not from the same viva group): A states their thesis and main reason in 30 seconds; B may interrupt once with "Why?" or "Such as?"; A answers with a time-buyer if needed. Swap. Then everyone sits with their viva group and checks that all papers have arrived.
Pre-task: a model defense10S → S–SSlides 14–15. Quick recap of the six-part structure (the paper is already written, so keep it to 2 minutes). Read Script 8.3-A (Priya's defense: three voices; two confident learners can read the questioners). Listen 1, gist: "What's her position? Does she change it?" Listen 2: Workbook Ex 1. Pairs check. Point out the three moves: she concedes (a blanket ban would be unfair), holds (the principle stands) and hedges (it works best alongside investment).
Defense language9T–S → S–SSlide 16. The question types and the concede → hold → hedge moves. CCQs (below). Drill (below): collapse → poised, stonewall → poised. Read Script 8.3-B (the model opening statement and two model answers); ask "How is the opening statement different from reading the paper?" (It's shorter, spoken, signposted, and it names the objection before the examiners do.) Workbook Ex 5 in pairs if time; otherwise at home.
Viva set-up4T–SSlide 17. Hand out the rotation sheet, role cards, chair's script and question-card sets. Each learner checks on the rotation sheet whose paper they lead-examine and whose they second-examine. ICQs.
Reading & question prep12SSilent reading. Each learner reads the two papers they will examine (the lead paper closely). They choose or adapt three question cards for their lead paper (at least one challenging: evidence, objection or consequence) and two for their second paper, writing a specific version on a sticky note ("You say that later start times improve grades. How strong is that evidence?"). Meanwhile, every learner jots 3–4 key words for their own opening statement. You circulate and help examiners sharpen one question each.
TASK: the vivas36Groups of 4 (or 3)Four vivas of about 9 minutes each, run by the chair from the script: chair introduces the candidate and the topic (15 s) → opening statement (2 min) → lead examiner 2 questions → second examiner 1–2 questions → chair 1 question if time (about 5 min of questions in all) → examiners give one star and one wish and circle the defense rubric (1 min). Put a big timer on the screen and call "Rotate" every 9 minutes so all groups stay together. You sit in on every group in turn with the observation checklist. Groups of 3: three vivas of 12 minutes (longer Q&A).
Feedback & language focus9T–SFirst the content: name two excellent concede-and-hold answers and one sharp examiner question you heard (no names, or with permission). Then board 2 Fixes, 2 Upgrades and 1–2 Wows, e.g. Fix: "I am agree with you, but…" · "According to me…" · Upgrade: "Yes, you're right, I'm wrong." (a collapse) → That's a fair point. I'd still argue, though, that… · "This proves that…" → This strongly suggests that…. Learners complete the rubric self-check in Workbook 8.3 for their defense.
Looking ahead & homework5T–SSlide 18: preview C26 (see "Looking Ahead"). Collect the papers (or ask learners to email them tonight) with the paper self-check. Homework: the online capstone lesson, the final C1 journal. Learners tick the 8.3 "I can…" list.
Total905 + 10 + 9 + 4 + 12 + 36 + 9 + 5
If you run long

Protect the vivas: a learner who wrote a paper and doesn't get to defend it will be hurt. Cut in this order: (1) Script 8.3-B becomes a single model answer; (2) the reading time shrinks to 8 minutes if papers were shared the night before (they should be); (3) the Q&A in each viva shrinks from 5 to 4 minutes (the chair skips their own question). Never cut Looking Ahead: learners must leave knowing exactly what C26 looks like.

Why a peer viva in class, but a recorded solo defense online?

The online capstone lets self-study learners present and then answer their own prepared list of questions. In class the real C1 challenge becomes possible: questions you didn't write, asked by people who have read your paper closely. That's why the Q&A matters more than the opening statement. A learner whose opening is plain but who concedes a fair point gracefully and then holds their position with a hedge has given a strong C1 performance. Note it on the checklist.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation (review)

No new grammar. The work today is integration (the whole C1 toolkit in one paper), interaction (answering what the examiner actually asked) and poise (conceding without collapsing). Keep CCQs fast.

"This paper argues that high schools should start at 9 a.m." Is this a survey of views or a position? (A position.) Could a reasonable person disagree? (Yes, so it's arguable: a good thesis.) · "Admittedly, it might be objected that a later start would disrupt family routines." Does the writer agree with the objection? (Partly: they grant it's a real concern.) What must come next? (The rebuttal: why the position still holds.) · Is a paper stronger with or without an objection? (With: it shows the writer has weighed the other side.)
An examiner asks: "What about parents who start work at 7 a.m.?" Response A: "Yes, you're right. It's a bad idea, then." (Collapse: the whole case is gone.) Response B: "That's not a problem." (Stonewall: no concession, no reason.) Response C: "That's a fair point, and I don't want to brush it aside. That said, schools could open at 7:30 for supervised breakfast, so the principle still stands." Which is poised? (C.) What are its three parts? (Concede → hold → a reason.)
"This proves that later start times raise grades." Safe claim? (No: overclaiming invites attack.) Better? ("Some studies suggest modest gains in grades.") Is hedging a weakness? (No: it shows you know exactly how strong your evidence is.) · "If I've understood your question, you're asking whether…" Why say this? (To check the question, and to buy a few seconds.)
🎤 Opening statementThank you. My paper addresses the question of whether… · I argue that… · My case rests on two points: … and … · The strongest objection, I think, is that… · My answer to that is… · So, on balance, …2 min, from notes, signposted
🔍 Check the questionIf I've understood your question, you're asking whether… · Do you mean… or…? · Could you say a little more about what you mean by…?checks, and buys time
🤝 ConcedeThat's a fair point, and I don't want to brush it aside. · You're right that… · I take your point. · To some extent, yes. · I'll admit that my evidence here is thinner.genuine, specific
🧱 HoldThat said, … · Even so, I'd still argue that… · However, that doesn't change the main point, which is… · The principle stands, provided that…a reason, not repetition
🪶 Hedge / refineSo I'd put it slightly more cautiously: … · I'd suggest that, in most cases, … · It seems likely that… · The evidence points toward… rather than proves…calibrate, don't collapse
❓ ExaminerYou say that… What's your evidence? · How would that work for…? · Someone might object that… How would you respond? · If this were adopted, what might go wrong? · Which of your arguments is the weakest?about the argument, never the person

Pronunciation for defending. The concession has a fall (it's sincere, not sarcastic): "That's a FAIR point ↘." The hold has a fresh start: a short pause, a slightly higher pitch and stress on the contrast word: "| That SAID, ↗ the PRINciple still stands." Keep the pace calm and unhurried: speed sounds defensive. In the opening statement, pause after each signpost ("My second point | is fairness."). And use 8.1 in real time: a time-buyer before a hard answer is not a weakness; a long silence is.

Drill script (quick, choral → individual)
  1. Concede → hold chain: T gives a challenge: "Isn't that too expensive?" S1: "That's a fair point; it isn't cheap." S2: "That said, the cost is paid once, and the benefit recurs." S3: "So I'd put it slightly more cautiously: it's worth it if it's phased in." New challenge, new chain.
  2. Collapse → poised: T (collapse): "Yes, you're right. Forget my idea." → S: "You're right that it's a risk. Even so, I'd still argue that…" · T (stonewall): "No, that's wrong." → S: "I see why you'd say that. However, …"
  3. Overclaim → hedge: T: "This proves that…" → S: "This strongly suggests that…" T: "Everyone agrees that…" → S: "Most researchers seem to agree that…" T: "It will definitely work." → S: "It's likely to work, provided that…"
  4. Check the question: T asks a long, slightly unclear question. S: "If I've understood your question, you're asking whether… Is that right?" Then S answers in one sentence.
Setting up the vivas (end of C24) — groups, roles, rotation

Groups: 4 is ideal (3 works). Mix topics inside a group, so examiners read something new, and mix strong and less confident writers. Avoid putting close friends together: they ask gentle questions. Announce the groups at the end of C24 so papers can be shared. Each group gets a rotation sheet (materials set H):

VivaCandidateLead examinerSecond examinerChair & timekeeper
1ABCD
2BCDA
3CDAB
4DABC

Every learner is candidate once, lead examiner once, second examiner once and chair once. They read closely only two papers (lead and second). Groups of 3: A–B–C, with the second examiner also acting as chair. Odd numbers / absences: you become the second examiner in one group, or run one group of 5 with two second examiners (10-minute vivas). A learner who arrives without a paper still takes part as an examiner and chair, and defends the model paper (Workbook 8.3) or their planning frame: the defense is the C1 skill you most want to see.

Who chairs well: the chair keeps time, keeps questions fair and stops any question that becomes personal ("Let's keep to the argument. Next question, please."). Brief every chair on this. A dominant speaker makes a good chair, because the role is about bringing others in; so does an anxious learner who likes structure, because the chair's script tells them exactly what to say.

ICQs: How long is your opening statement? (Two minutes.) Do you read your paper? (No, notes only.) · Lead examiner: how many questions? (Two, at least one challenging.) · Who keeps time? (The chair.) · A question about my family or my country: OK? (No. The chair stops it.) · When the examiner makes a fair point, what do you do? (Concede it, then hold with a reason.) · What do examiners give at the end? (One star and one wish.) · Is this a grade? (No, it's practice for C26.)
Running a viva — the chair's order (about 9 minutes)
  1. Chair introduces (15 s): "Welcome. Our candidate is Ana. Her paper addresses the question: should high schools start later? Ana, you have two minutes."
  2. Opening statement (2 min): the candidate summarizes thesis, arguments, the objection and the rebuttal from 3–4 key words. The chair shows "30 SECONDS" and "TIME."
  3. Lead examiner (about 2½ min): two questions. After each answer, one short follow-up is allowed ("So would you accept that…?").
  4. Second examiner (about 1½–2 min): one or two questions, preferably of a different type from the lead examiner's.
  5. Chair's question (if time, 30–60 s): often a wildcard ("If you could keep only one argument, which one?").
  6. Close and feedback (1 min): the chair thanks the candidate; each examiner says one star (a specific strength) and one wish, and circles the defense rubric. Then rotate.
The rubrics and the observation checklist → C26

The paper rubric (set J, and the self-check in Workbook 8.3) has six criteria: 1 position & thesis · 2 arguments & support · 3 counter-argument & rebuttal · 4 organization & cohesion · 5 range & precision (the C1 toolkit) · 6 accuracy, register & concision. The defense rubric also has six: 1 opening statement · 2 understanding the questions · 3 concede & hold · 4 hedging & calibration · 5 fluency strategies · 6 delivery & interaction. Three levels: ★★★ strong C1 (reaching toward C2) · ★★ C1 · ★ not yet. Examiners circle the defense rubric for their candidate; learners self-assess both; you use both for written feedback on the paper, returned at C26. Don't grade C25: it's practice. Tell learners so, clearly.

The observation checklist (set K) has one row per learner and 10 can-do items. Three marks: ✓ did it clearly · ~ did it with support, or with errors that affect meaning · ○ not yet / not observed. You can't sit through every viva: see each learner's whole opening statement or at least two of their answers, and one of their examiner questions. After class, use it to:

  • Plan each learner's C26 assessment. The C1 final assessment is an individual speaking interview (about 10 minutes) plus listening, reading and writing tasks — see the kit's C1 final assessment. Before C26, read it and match your checklist notes to its speaking criteria: which topics suit each learner, and where to start the interview so that each learner begins with something they did well.
  • Plan the C26 warm-up: the two items with the most ~ / ○ across the class become a short review before the assessment starts.
  • Write one sentence of feedback for each learner on the returned paper: one real strength from the defense, named specifically ("Your concession to Omar's question about cost was excellent, and you held your thesis with a clear reason").
  • Spot who needs reassurance: anyone who froze or collapsed under questioning gets a quiet word at the end of C25: "Next class is one-to-one and friendly. No audience. You'll be fine."
Differentiation
Literacy support

Before C25 (in C24 or by message), agree a shorter paper: 250–350 words, with thesis, two arguments, one objection and a rebuttal, using the planning frame's sentence starters. In the viva, allow a 1-minute opening statement from the planning frame, and ask the group's examiners to use clarifying and objection cards (not wildcards). Give the learner the four defense phrase cards to hold: That's a fair point · That said · I'd still argue that… · If I've understood your question…. As an examiner, they may read a question straight from a card. Target: one clear concession and one hold with a reason.

Stretch (toward C2)

Answer the question behind the question: identify the examiner's assumption ("That question assumes that parents' schedules can't change. I'd question that."). Reframe rather than defend ("I'd put the issue differently: it's not about lateness but about alignment"). Use a precise concession frame (Granted, … nevertheless… · Be that as it may, …) and concede a point you didn't anticipate, then refine the thesis in real time. As an examiner, ask the wildcard cards and a genuine follow-up. At home: write a 150-word "response to examiners" that revises the paper's weakest paragraph.

L1 watch-outs
  • Where the thesis goes. Some rhetorical traditions build up to the main point (the Japanese ki-shō-ten-ketsu structure, Korean gi-seung-jeon-gyeol, and some Chinese, Arabic and Persian essay conventions value context before claim). English position papers put the thesis in the first paragraph. Frame it as a genre convention, not a better way of thinking.
  • "According to me" / "In my opinion, I think" (French selon moi, Spanish según yo / en mi opinión, pienso que, Italian secondo me): not used in English. Teach This paper argues that… · I would argue that… · In my view, … (without I think).
  • "I am agree" / "I am not agree" (Spanish, French, Portuguese): still surfaces under pressure at C1. I agree / I don't agree. Board it after the vivas, not during.
  • Challenging a peer. Learners from cultures where questioning a classmate publicly causes loss of face (Japanese, Korean, Chinese, Thai, Vietnamese, Filipino, and many others) may ask only gentle clarifying questions; learners from more direct traditions may challenge bluntly. The role card and the question cards help both: "The card asks the question, not you." Teach the softener: I wonder whether… · Someone might object that…
  • Memorized openings. Learners from exam systems that reward memorized speeches may recite the paper. Allow a full script at home for confidence; in the viva, key words only. Kindly collect scripts if needed.
  • "Discuss about," "explain me," "emphasize on" (Spanish, Arabic, Hindi, Urdu and others): discuss something · explain it to me · emphasize something. Common in papers: mark them in written feedback.
Homework

Online lesson 8.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c1/lesson_24.html. Listen to Priya's defense with 🔊 and shadow her concession and hold lines; do Exercises 1–2 and the Quick Quiz. Do the Capstone as a recorded solo defense: present your paper in two minutes and answer the three hardest questions your examiners asked you today, better than you did in class. Workbook 8.3: the rubric self-check (if not finished) and the final C1 journal. Bring the workbook to C26.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C It acknowledges a counter-argument and rebuts it. · Q2 B concede the fair point, then explain why your position still holds. · Q3 (new) B "If I've understood your question, you're asking whether the evidence applies to smaller cities. To some extent, yes; however…" Ex 1 (Script 8.3-A): 1 city centers should be closed to private cars · 2 that it would hurt local businesses; foot traffic on car-free streets tends to rise, not fall · 3 she concedes a blanket ban would be unfair, then clarifies that her proposal exempts accessible transportation and permit holders · 4 whether the rise in foot traffic only happens in wealthy cities with good public transportation · 5 she hedges the claim: the ban works best alongside investment in transportation, not instead of it · 6 she checks the question ("If I've understood your question…") and says the policy should be reviewed after a trial period.

Closing the Module and the Course

End of C25: Module 8 and C1 wrap-up

Recap on slide 18: "That's a good question… it's a kind of… or rather…" → "due to the fact that" → "because" → "That's a fair point. That said, I'd still argue…" Keep going, cut back, stand your ground. Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook and look back at the "I can…" lists from Module 1. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you, for the C26 warm-up.

The C26 final assessment covers the whole C1 course. From this module the most relevant skills are: sustaining an extended spoken turn without freezing, paraphrasing when a word is missing, answering unexpected follow-up questions with poise, and writing concisely with a clear position. Check the kit's C1 final assessment for exactly how it tests them.

Looking Ahead — C26: Final Assessment & Celebration

What to tell learners at the end of C25 (slide 18)

C26 has two parts. First, the C1 final assessment: an individual speaking interview (about 10 minutes) plus listening, reading and writing tasks — see the kit's C1 final assessment. Read it before C26 and tell learners how the class will be organized (the order of the tasks and when each learner will be interviewed), so there are no surprises. Then a celebration: certificates, a look back at what everyone can do now, and the next steps, the C2 course, Mastery English, or the specialist tracks (IELTS, Business English).

Say it simply: "Next class is our last class. You'll have a one-to-one conversation with me for about ten minutes, and some listening, reading and writing. It's everything we've practiced. Today you answered questions you didn't expect, about a paper you wrote. That was harder than anything in C26. Then we celebrate."

Reduce anxiety: show C1 lesson 1.1 on screen and ask: "Week 1: could you have written 500 concise words on a debatable question and then defended them against three sharp examiners?" Let them hear how far they've come. Remind them that the online lessons stay open for review, and that their recorded defense is the best practice for the interview.

Celebration planning: if learners want to bring food to share in C26, great. Ask everyone to label their dish with its main ingredients (allergies, halal, vegetarian). Nobody should feel they have to bring anything. Invite 2–3 volunteers to give a one-minute impromptu "closing speech" drawn from a card: the C23 skill, with a friendly audience.
